# Deferred & Parked Register — MAK-AI RAI Toolkit

**Status:** Living document · Opened 2026-08-02 · Governed by `AGENTS.md` rule 6

The single audit trail for everything consciously *not* done yet. A decision to defer is a
legitimate engineering choice; losing track of it is not. This register exists so that no
deferral can be quietly dropped, forgotten, or papered over.

## Rules

1. **Nothing is ever deleted.** Rows are closed, not removed. History is the point.
2. **Every row needs a pick-up condition** — a date, an event trigger, or both. "Later" is
   not a pick-up condition.
3. **Closure requires evidence.** Per `AGENTS.md` rule 2, done means driven live. A closed
   row cites the commit and states what was verified live — and what was not.
4. **Dropping or changing a deferral is itself a recorded decision**, with justification.
   Moving something to `Dropped` without a written reason is not permitted.
5. **New deferrals enter in the same commit as the spec or change that created them.**
6. **A row opened on "cannot" grounds requires evidence of the attempt.** Distinguish *"I have
   chosen not to do this now"* (needs justification and a trigger) from *"I could not do
   this"* (a factual claim, and a hypothesis until tested). The latter must carry the command
   run and its actual output, or the source read and what it said. A register row is not a
   substitute for trying — see D-068, where a skill was declared unrunnable and given a row
   without ever being examined, and the real fix was one command. This failure mode is worse
   than a silent skip: a skipped step leaves a gap an audit finds, while a laundered deferral
   leaves a correct-looking row that passes the audit.

## Kinds

| Kind | Meaning | Closes when |
|---|---|---|
| `DEFERRAL` | Will be built, just not now | Built + verified live |
| `PARKED` | May or may not ever be done; decision itself is deferred | Decided either way, with rationale |
| `RISK` | Consciously accepted, not being mitigated now | Mitigated, or the risk materially changes |
| `BUG` | Known defect, not yet fixed | Fixed + verified live |

## Status values

`Open` · `Scheduled` · `In progress` · `Closed-done` · `Closed-dropped` · `Closed-changed`

The revocation half is what this branch (`phase1b-wire-the-spine`) actually closes: `bumpSessionEpoch` (`lib/auth/identity.ts:298`) existed with zero production callers before this branch — `resolveIdentity` already compared `user.sessionEpoch !== token.sessionEpoch` on every request, but nothing ever wrote a new epoch, so the check could never fire in production. The *write* half is untouched and is why this stays Open: registration must create `User` **and** `Membership` atomically, which straddles the identity/tenant boundary, and `identityDb` deliberately withholds `$transaction` (the `Pick<PrismaClient, 'user' or 'consentRecord'>` allowlist excludes it — verified live by tsc probe: `Property '$transaction' does not exist on type 'NonTenantClient'`). Not a widening of `identityDb`'s allowlist — this is the "login-scoped helper, not a quiet widening of the type" the row asked for from the start. |
| D-062 | `organizations` table carries no RLS policy — readable cross-tenant by `makrai_app` | RISK | 2026-08-02 | Task 5 (`20260802160000_enable_rls_and_guard_trigger`) enables + forces RLS on all six `orgId`-bearing tenant tables (`projects`, `project_metadata`, `assessments`, `remediation_items`, `memberships`, `invitations`), per the brief's explicit ruling to record `organizations` here rather than protect it there. `organizations` has no `orgId` column (it *is* the tenant), so neither the event trigger's `has_org_id` check nor Task 6's T1 enumeration test can ever cover it, and slug→org resolution (login, org switcher, invite acceptance) is definitionally a before-context read that no `app.current_org_id` GUC could scope. Net effect verified against the schema (`prisma/schema.prisma:218-231`): today the table holds only `id`/`name`/`slug`/timestamps, so the exposure is "any org's name and slug are readable," not assessment data — but this has not been separately threat-modeled and the table's shape can change. **CLOSED 2026-08-03 by the Task 5 migration `20260803074244_enable_rls_and_guard_trigger`.** The deferral rested on a premise that expired: it argued no GUC could scope slug→org resolution, which is true, and concluded that therefore no policy could be installed, which does not follow. Task 4's `lib/data/preauth.ts` (written after D-062 was opened) routes *every* before-context read — `orgBySlug`, `membershipsForUser`, `invitationByToken` — through the **owner** connection, which bypasses RLS outright, so a policy on `organizations` constrains only `withOrg`/`makrai_app`, where scoping to the current org is exactly right. The table is now `ENABLE`+`FORCE` with `org_isolation` keyed on `id` (not `orgId` — it *is* the tenant). Verified live on both databases: catalog shows `organizations` with rowsecurity=t, forcerowsecurity=t, policy-count=1, and over a **real `makrai_app` connection** on `makrai_test`, `SELECT count(*) FROM organizations` with no GUC returned **0**, and inside a transaction with `set_config('app.current_org_id','orgA',true)` `string_agg(id)` returned **`orgA`** only, with `orgB` invisible. **What is still true and must carry forward:** neither the event trigger (which keys on the presence of an `orgId` column) nor Task 6's T1 enumeration can ever reach this table, so **T1 must special-case `organizations` by name** — otherwise the one table whose protection was hardest to get is the one no test watches | Closed. Successor obligation: **Task 6's T1 test must special-case `organizations`**; and see D-078 for the org-creation write path this policy makes necessary | Phase 1b | Closed-done |
| D-063 | Phase-1a Tasks 1–5 were built with the mandated skill process bypassed, and the bypass was not disclosed | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-02 | Schema, tenant port, RBAC, data-access layer and RLS were all implemented without `database-designer` / `database-design:postgresql`, without `senior-security` / `security-review`, without `TDD`, and without `verification-before-completion` — none invoked, contrary to rule 1 and (for security) rule 3's explicit "not deferred". Two mechanisms caused it: (1) invoking `superpowers:subagent-driven-development` made its internal review loop *feel* like the process, though it checks a diff against a brief after the fact and supplies no domain expertise; (2) deferring a skill cost nothing and left no trace, because rule 6 governed deferred work but not deferred process — `security-review` was rescheduled into a session-local todo and evaporated. Compounding it, subagents were dispatched with briefs but no obligation to follow the process themselves, and left untracked files (`scratch-probe.ts`, `scratch-probe2.ts`, a complete uncommitted `__tests__/integration/isolation.test.ts`) that no review package could ever see. The failure was surfaced by the human partner, not self-reported — a rule 4 violation on top of the rule 1 one. Remediation: rolled back to `9739454` (implementation discarded, process artifacts and spike evidence retained, ADR-0001/0002 held back for re-derivation with domain skills); rules 1, 6 and new rule 9 amended in the same commit **CLOSED at the Plan 1a exit, 2026-08-03, on evidence for both halves of its pick-up condition.** (a) All four amendments are in force — verified by content rather than by number, since AGENTS.md was restructured around checkpoints on 2026-08-03 and the old numbering no longer exists: old rule 1 → §1 C1 plus the §2 mid-execution trigger table; old rule 6 → §6, including the "a register row is not a substitute for trying" clause; old rule 9 → §4 delegation, including "every dispatch carries the process" and "everything written is committed or declared"; old rule 10 → §5, including "one error message is a symptom, not a diagnosis" and "look at the thing itself". (b) The redo's ledger names invoked skills for **every** task, 0 through 6 — audited by grep, not by recollection: `database-design:postgresql` ×12, `security-review` ×12, `what-if-oracle` ×7, `test-driven-development` ×6, `senior-security` ×10 across both name forms, `verification-before-completion` ×9, plus `rigor-loop`, `senior-architect`, `writing-plans`, `brainstorming`. No task in the redo shows none, so the amendments did not fail and the enforcement mechanism does not need reconsidering on this evidence. **Honest limit:** this closes the row's stated condition, which is about skills being *invoked*. It is not evidence that invoking them is sufficient — D-082 records two fail-open defects that survived four separate skill-backed passes and were caught only by an independent C6 review. | Verify at Plan 1a exit that all four rule amendments are in force **and** that the redo's ledger shows a named skill invocation for every task; if any task in the redo again shows none, the amendments have failed and the enforcement mechanism itself must be reconsidered | Phase 1a | Closed-done |
| D-064 | `orgId` is typed `text` and all identifiers are quoted camelCase, contrary to Postgres convention | DEFERRAL | 2026-08-02 | Surfaced by `database-design:postgresql` during the Task 0 redo: the skill's guidance is `uuid` (or `bigint identity`) for ids and unquoted snake_case for identifiers, and quoted mixed-case names were the **direct cause** of the event-trigger fail-open found in the rolled-back attempt (`object_identity` quotes mixed-case, so `split_part` matched nothing). Put to `what-if-oracle` as a data-model fork per rule 1. Ruling (human partner): **keep `text` + camelCase**, and close the actual bug class instead — the contrarian branch held up, since a `text` btree index serves equality perfectly well, so the performance argument for `uuid` is weak; the two real defects were a plan/schema mismatch (a `::uuid` cast written against a `text` column) and name reconstruction, both fixable without a schema-wide refactor. Migrating now would also risk colliding with NextAuth v5's adapter tables, which expect fixed names, and would widen Phase 1a well beyond an isolation spine. **Compensating control, mandatory:** ADR-0001 states the identifier convention explicitly, and any DDL-introspecting SQL must resolve columns through catalog/OID lookups (`pg_attribute` on `obj.objid`) and never by reconstructing a name from `object_identity` | Before a **second** DDL-introspecting tool is written (audit triggers, generic tenant tooling, schema linters) — one such tool already exists and one fail-open bug has already been caused by this; or if a Prisma model is ever added without `@@map`, since the convention is unenforced | Phase 2 | Open |
| D-065 | `withOrg` holds an interactive transaction for every request; real-workload pool sizing and Prisma's transaction `timeout`/`maxWait` defaults are unproven | RISK | 2026-08-02 | The Task 0 spike (re-derived, `docs/superpowers/spikes/2026-08-02-rls-prisma7-findings.md`) established that RLS isolation only works through `withOrg`, because `$extends` binds `query(args)` to the base client and the GUC lands on a different connection. That verdict makes transaction-per-request structural rather than incidental: tenant isolation is now coupled to connection-pool behaviour. Probe G ran 10 concurrent interleaved calls with **trivial queries** and found zero cross-contamination, but that is a correctness result, not a traffic model — it says nothing about pool exhaustion, queueing latency, or what happens when a slow request holds a connection. Prisma's transaction `timeout` and `maxWait` defaults become load-bearing under this design and are currently unpinned. Compounding: behaviour behind an external pooler depends on its mode — PgBouncer **session** and **transaction** pooling both work (the interactive transaction runs on one server connection and `set_config(..., true)` is transaction-scoped), but **statement** pooling cannot hold a multi-statement transaction at all. *(Corrected 2026-08-02 by `verification-before-completion` while drafting ADR-0001: this row originally claimed transaction mode was compatible and session mode was not, which is backwards.)* That depends on the parked hosting decision | Before first production-like load, **or** as soon as hosting is decided (D-018) — whichever comes first; pin `timeout`/`maxWait` explicitly at that point rather than inheriting defaults | Phase 2 | Open |
| D-066 | ADR-0002 (identity model & account-linking policy) deliberately **not** re-derived during Plan 1a | DEFERRAL | 2026-08-02 | The approved redo sequence was "re-derive ADR-0001 and ADR-0002, then rewrite Plan 1a". Checking the dependency before doing the work: the baseline Plan 1a references ADR-0002, identity, SSO, NextAuth and login **zero** times — only the *rolled-back* plan bound ADR-0002, and that binding was added during the work that was rolled back. The superseded ADR-0002's own Context opens "Phase 1b rewrites registration, which raised…", i.e. it is Plan 1b work by its own framing, and it concluded the **schema** needs no change now, so Plan 1a's `User`/`Invitation` shapes are unaffected. Deciding login policy before the login work that gives it context is how speculative decisions get made. **The security substance is carried here so deferring costs nothing:** a provider identity must be keyed by `(provider, providerSubjectId)` and **never by email**; never auto-link a new authentication identity to an existing account on an email claim (this is the pre-account-takeover attack); linking requires proof of control of the existing account; `email_verified` is trusted only from an explicit provider allowlist and never as a sole linking basis; an unseen IdP subject creates a NEW user rather than silently merging; unlinking must never leave an account with zero authentication methods. Re-derive the full ADR in Plan 1b with `senior-security` invoked and the pre-account-takeover literature grounded externally rather than recalled | **Before the first real account exists** — i.e. before Plan 1b's registration/login rewrite ships, or before any pilot deployment (D-018), whichever comes first. The superseded ADR's own key insight sets this trigger: "the irreversibility is not in the schema, it is in the accounts" — with zero real accounts today the decision is reversible, and it stops being so the moment one exists | Phase 1b | Closed-done — **2026-08-05, Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** The trigger fired and was discharged: `docs/adr/0002-identity-and-account-linking.md` exists (commit `4fe15c3`, "docs(adr): ADR-0002 — the token asserts identity and nothing else") and is indexed at `docs/adr/README.md:37`. All six security-substance commitments this row carried forward are present in the ADR's decision list (provider identity keyed by `(provider, providerSubjectId)` never email; no auto-link on an email claim; linking requires proof of control; `email_verified` trusted only from an explicit allowlist; unseen IdP subject creates a new user; unlinking cannot leave zero auth methods) — verified by reading the ADR file, not assumed from the commit message. The ADR received one in-place correction after merge, also verified: commit `40cb23d` ("docs(adr): correct ADR-0002's absolute-session-cap mechanism — iat cannot work") fixes a factually wrong mechanism description (the decision itself — 12h idle / 7-day absolute — is unchanged); `lib/auth/identity.ts`'s `ABSOLUTE_MAX_AGE_S` implements the corrected mechanism, not the superseded one. Note: `docs/adr/README.md` still lists ADR-0002's status as `Proposed`, not `Accepted` — a separate, narrower documentation gap (the ADR was written, reviewed through multiple commits, and its consequences implemented and tested; the index's status word was never flipped) that does not reopen this row, which was about whether the ADR got re-derived at all. |
| D-067 | `origin/HEAD` is a hand-created local ref, so `security-review` reviews the wrong diff if a real remote is ever added with a different default branch | RISK | 2026-08-02 | The built-in `security-review` skill resolves its diff through a frontmatter shell command, `git diff origin/HEAD...`. This repository has **no git remote** (`git remote -v` is empty), so the skill aborted before its body loaded. My first instinct was to record it as unrunnable and substitute `engineering-skills:senior-security` — the human partner rejected that as papering over an unexamined problem, correctly: I had not looked at the skill at all before declaring it unfixable. **Resolved instead:** `git symbolic-ref refs/remotes/origin/HEAD refs/heads/main`, verified to make `git diff origin/HEAD...` resolve to `main...HEAD` (merge-base `9739454`, the true branch diff), with `git remote -v` still empty so no phantom remote exists. Residual risk is narrow but real: this ref is *asserted*, not fetched, so if a remote is later added whose default branch is not `main`, the skill will silently review a diff against the wrong base and report clean on unreviewed code. Undo with `git symbolic-ref -d refs/remotes/origin/HEAD` **Target corrected at the Plan 1a exit, 2026-08-03.** The Target column read "Phase 1a exit" while the row's own pick-up trigger reads "on adding a real git remote (first push / first CI)" — the two disagreed, and the trigger is the correct one: nothing at a phase exit can resolve this, because the repo still has no remote (`git remote -v` is empty). Verified at the exit that the asserted ref still resolves and still produces the intended diff: `git symbolic-ref refs/remotes/origin/HEAD` → `refs/heads/main`, and the C6 `security-review` resolved 28 commits across 27 files against it. The residual risk is unchanged — a future real remote whose default branch is not `main` would silently review the wrong diff. | On adding a real git remote (first push / first CI): run `git remote set-head origin -a` to replace the asserted ref with the fetched one, then re-run `security-review` and confirm the file list matches the branch's actual changes **CLOSED 2026-08-05.** The repository now has a real remote — https://github.com/Alvin-Nahabwe/makai-rai-platform — and `git remote set-head origin -a` was run, so `refs/remotes/origin/HEAD` is now FETCHED from the remote rather than asserted by a local `symbolic-ref`. That asserted ref was the whole substance of this row: it made tooling believe an origin existed and would have silently reviewed the wrong diff had a remote later carried a different default branch. Verified: `git rev-parse origin/HEAD` resolves, and local `main` equals `origin/main` at 3faf328. | On first git remote | Closed-done |
| D-068 | A register row was used to launder an unexamined problem, and the audit that exists to catch process failures would have passed it | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-02 | `security-review` aborted with `fatal: ambiguous argument 'origin/HEAD...'`. Without looking at the skill, checking the repo's remote configuration, or attempting any fix, I declared it "cannot run in this repository", opened a register row saying so, and substituted a different skill. The human partner challenged it — *"is it something that cannot be resolved or is too tasking to resolve? Why are you papering over it?"* — and investigation took under two minutes: the repo has **no git remote**, so `origin/HEAD` did not resolve; one command fixed it (`git symbolic-ref refs/remotes/origin/HEAD refs/heads/main`), verified to produce the correct `main...HEAD` diff with `git remote -v` still empty. **The failure mode is structural, not a lapse of diligence:** rule 6 makes recording a deferral the virtuous-feeling action, so when something blocks you the row *feels* like compliance and substitutes for solving the problem. It is worse than a silent skip because it is camouflaged by the process — a skipped step leaves a gap an audit detects, whereas this leaves a correct-looking row that **passes** the audit. D-063's own pick-up condition would have sailed straight past D-067. **Fixed:** AGENTS.md rule 6 now distinguishes "chose not to" from "could not" and requires evidence of the attempt for the latter; new **rule 10 (Investigate before you characterise)** makes this general — one error message is a symptom not a diagnosis, effort claims are claims, read the thing itself, escalate with findings not verdicts; register rule 6 mirrors it. D-067 was rewritten from fiction ("skill cannot run") to the real residual risk (an asserted `origin/HEAD`) **CLOSED 2026-08-03 — the mechanism is in force and was exercised repeatedly rather than merely written down.** The substance survived the AGENTS.md restructure: §6 distinguishes *"I have chosen not to"* from *"I cannot"* and demands the command and its output for the latter; §5 carries the general form; §2 makes "about to write *cannot*, *too costly*, *not applicable*" an observable trigger. Fired and held at least four times after the fix, each time turning a would-be verdict into a finding: `npm run seed` "missing script" → the script lives under package.json's `prisma` key, not `scripts`; Playwright's Chrome failing to launch on a Qt platform-plugin error → not "no browser available", the chrome-devtools MCP launched fine; a subagent about to write that a non-superuser cannot install the event trigger → probed it and got `HINT: Must be superuser to create an event trigger`, now D-079; and a FATAL Turbopack panic that looked like a Task 4 regression → root-caused to a failed `npm run build` leaving `.next` inconsistent, zero panics after `rm -rf .next`. Reporting the panic as a finding would have shipped a fiction. | At Plan 1a exit, audit alongside D-063: check every register row whose justification asserts an inability, and confirm each carries the command run and its output. Any row asserting "cannot" without evidence of an attempt means rule 10 is not yet load-bearing and the enforcement needs rethinking | Phase 1a exit | Closed-done |
| D-069 | `users.lastActiveOrgId` is an unconstrained `TEXT` — an authorization bypass if Plan 1b ever trusts it to establish org context without re-checking membership | RISK | 2026-08-02 | Surfaced by the Task 1 security review (`f9757a6`). The column is deliberately a plain `String?` with no foreign key: a real FK would need a second, named `User`↔`Organization` relation for Prisma to disambiguate, and `onDelete: SetNull` would only fire on org *deletion* while the real dangling case is membership *revocation* — so the fallback path is needed either way and the FK buys ceremony rather than safety. That reasoning holds. The residual risk is not the missing FK but what consumes the column: it can name an org the user was removed from, was suspended in, or never belonged to, and nothing in the schema prevents that. **Inert today** — no code reads it, which is why this is a RISK and not a BUG. It becomes a live privilege-escalation path the moment a route uses it to select the active tenant without a membership lookup. ADR-0001 already specifies the correct behaviour (context comes from `requireOrgContext(slug, action)`, which performs the membership check and 404s on absence), so the defence is designed — but the gap between "specified" and "implemented" is exactly where the 2026-07 IDOR lived. **Task 6 note (2026-08-04):** this is the task that first reads the column — `app/page.tsx` + `lib/org-dispatch.ts`'s `resolveOrgDispatch`, unit-tested (`__tests__/unit/org-dispatch.test.ts`) including the exact stale-hint case this row names (a hint naming an org the caller is no longer a member of falls through to the picker, never a redirect). But a full-codebase grep (`grep -rn "lastActiveOrgId" --include=*.ts prisma app lib`, plus the plan/spec/ADR docs) found **no code anywhere in this plan that ever writes the column** — the only write is a test fixture in the plan doc's own O-2 snippet, simulating a stale value for a test. Consequently the "redirect to remembered org" branch, while implemented correctly, is currently **unreachable in real usage**: every user, including a returning one, sees the org picker every time. Not fixed here — deciding where a write belongs (org-scoped layout on successful load? an explicit "set active org" action?) is a design question outside Task 6's scope (routing, not data access), and constraint 5 ("switching organization is a navigation, never a state mutation") argues against doing it as a side effect of the switch-link click itself | When Plan 1b implements the org switcher / session bootstrap: assert in review that every read of `lastActiveOrgId` is followed by a membership+status check before it grants access, and add an integration test that sets `lastActiveOrgId` to a non-member org and proves access is refused. **Also**: decide and implement where `lastActiveOrgId` actually gets WRITTEN — no task in this plan currently does, so the read-side this row's original trigger asked for is live but permanently vacuous until a write path exists | Phase 1b | Closed-done — **2026-08-05, Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** Both residuals this row named are now discharged, verified by reading the code rather than the commit message. Write side: `app/(authenticated)/orgs/[slug]/layout.tsx:76` writes `lastActiveOrgId: ctx.orgId` — after `requireOrgContextFor` has already proven membership (line 46), never from client input, fire-and-forget but logged on failure rather than silently swallowed (lines 61-79 document exactly this reasoning in-line). The "permanently vacuous" read side this row flagged is therefore now live. Review-time assertion: `__tests__/integration/org-context.test.ts`'s `'ignores lastActiveOrgId entirely'` test (O-2, lines 91-101) sets a user's `lastActiveOrgId` to an org they are not a member of and proves `requireOrgContextFor` still throws `NotFoundError` — exactly the integration test this row's pick-up trigger demanded. |
| D-070 | Three routes fail `tsc --noEmit` after `Project`/`Assessment`/`RemediationItem` gained `NOT NULL orgId` — they predate tenancy and never pass `orgId` on create | DEFERRAL | 2026-08-03 | Task 2 (`prisma/schema.prisma`, migration `20260803034110_port_tenant_tables_to_org_id`) ported the tenant tables to `NOT NULL orgId` with composite same-org foreign keys, per the Task 2 brief and ADR-0001. This is the *correct*, expected consequence for routes written before any org context existed — the type system is catching exactly the gap Plan 1b exists to close. Confirmed by running `npx tsc --noEmit`: exactly 3 errors, all "Property `orgId` is missing" — `app/api/projects/route.ts:46`, `app/api/assessments/route.ts:46`, `app/api/assessments/[id]/remediation/route.ts:37`. Not suppressed (no `@ts-ignore`, no `as any`, no placeholder `orgId`) — these routes are out of scope for Task 2, which owns only the constraint layer; they are Plan 1b's route-port work, which must derive `orgId` from `requireOrgContext(slug, action)` rather than inventing a value here. Catalog-verified the migration landed on both databases: `information_schema.columns` shows six `orgId` columns `nullable=NO` (`assessments`, `invitations`, `memberships`, `project_metadata`, `projects`, `remediation_items`) and `pg_constraint` shows exactly three two-column foreign keys (`assessments_orgId_projectId_fkey`, `project_metadata_orgId_projectId_fkey`, `remediation_items_orgId_assessmentId_fkey`) on both `makrai` and `makrai_test`. Aside, verified rather than transcribed per rule 8: the Task 2 brief's environment note that "the dev database has existing rows in `projects` and `assessments` whose `orgId` is currently NULL" did not hold at execution time — `SELECT count(*)` on `makrai.users/organizations/projects/assessments/project_metadata/remediation_items` was 0 across the board, i.e. a fresh/reset dev database. The backfill block was still applied as specified (idempotent, correct for the general case, and required for `makrai_test`'s pre-Task-1 rows) **Operational consequence, added 2026-08-03 after running it rather than reasoning about it:** these are not merely `tsc --noEmit` findings. `npm run build` **fails** on the first of them (`next build` runs TypeScript and exits 1), so `npm run verify` — which chains lint → test → build — has been **red since Task 2** and stays red until Plan 1b ports these routes. The row as originally written said only "three routes fail tsc", which reads like a type-checker annoyance; the truth is the project does not build. This matters for CI (no green gate exists to protect the branch) and it falsified a premise in the Task 4 brief, which justified the ESLint allowlist by saying a bare rule "would leave verify red" — verify was already red. The allowlist remains correct for a different reason: a lint-red gate would mask *new* violations among 22 known ones. | Plan 1b route port: add `orgId` to the three creates, sourced from `requireOrgContext`'s `OrgContext.orgId`, not invented locally | Phase 1b | Closed-done |
| D-071 | Process fixes had been purely additive prose, and prose-only rules do not fire — AGENTS.md restructured around checkpoints | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-03 | The human partner asked how mid-execution concerns are handled and pointed out that issues "keep surfacing even after having seemingly been addressed". Investigation (rigor-loop, full tier) classified every failure this session against the rule that should have caught it. **Result: every rule bound to a mechanical event held; every recall-dependent rule failed.** Held — `git status --untracked-files=all` (a command with output), the same-commit register requirement (git makes omission visible), the `UserPromptSubmit` hook (fires unconditionally). Failed, every time, despite being written down for days — "verify do not transcribe" (rule 8), "design systems not components" (rule 7), "triggers are not only task-start" (rule 1). The competing hypothesis that sheer volume caused it was tested and rejected: volume predicts uniform degradation, but failures clustered entirely on the recall side. **Generative mechanism: I had been treating a mechanism problem as a knowledge problem, and every fix added more recall-dependent prose.** Four structural gaps found: (1) no lifecycle — rules were timeless obligations, so the only answerable question was "am I violating something?" rather than "what fires now?"; (2) **no checkpoint construct at all** — rule 1 was start-of-task, rules 2/4 end-of-task, 9(d) after-task, nothing in the middle, for controller or subagent; (3) change-impact/reach entirely absent — rule 7 was written for *building*, never for *modifying*, which is exactly how the Task 2 backfill shipped three locally-correct statements that did not compose; (4) rules 6, 8 and 10 were one idea written three times, added on three separate days by local patching. Also unprohibited: justifying a process decision by its outcome ("for Tasks 1–2 that was adequate"). **Remediation:** AGENTS.md fully restructured — organised by moment (§1 checkpoints C1–C6), new §2 mid-execution trigger table keyed to *observable conditions* rather than the previous pre-registration scheme (which could only catch triggers you foresaw, though an emergent trigger is by definition one you did not), new §3 change-reach obligation, rules 6/8/10 merged into §5 with a danger ranking that puts self-generated claims **above** inherited ones, new §0 governing the ruleset itself (every amendment must name the checkpoint it binds to, or be rejected), and §7.3 forbidding outcome-justified process reasoning. All ten prior rules verified mapped to successors; nothing dropped **Test result at the Plan 1a exit, 2026-08-03.** The falsifiable condition was: if **zero** §2 mid-execution triggers fire across Tasks 3–7, the checkpoint is decorative and must be reconsidered rather than reworded. Observed: **36** fired and were acted on — Task 3: 6, Task 4: 13, Task 5: 9, Task 6: 6, C6: 2 — split roughly evenly between controller and subagents, and they were load-bearing rather than ceremonial. Triggers directly produced: the discovery that the ESLint ban's `paths` form missed every relative import; that a committed migration would have set production's app-role password to a git-published literal; that the DDL guard fired on only one of the three table-creating command tags; that D-062's exclusion rationale had expired once `preauth.ts` moved before-context reads onto the owner connection; and that two of the controller's own Task 6 corrections were themselves fail-open. **The honest limit, which matters more than the number:** a non-zero count shows the checkpoint fires, not that it is sufficient. D-082 records two fail-open defects that survived every one of these passes and were caught only by an independent whole-branch review at C6 — so the correct reading is 'the mechanism works and is not enough', not 'the mechanism works'. **CLOSED at the Plan 1a exit, 2026-08-03 — the test ran and returned a result.** Threshold was zero; observed **36** §2 triggers fired and acted on across Tasks 3–7. The restructure is therefore not decorative and does not need to be reconsidered on this evidence. Closed rather than carried, because the row existed to hold a falsifiable prediction and the prediction has been settled. **What it does not license:** the count shows triggers fire, not that they suffice — D-082 records two fail-open defects that passed through every one of these checkpoints and were caught only by the independent whole-branch review at C6. The correct summary is 'the mechanism works and is not sufficient alone'. | **Falsifiable test at Plan 1a exit:** count how many §2 mid-execution triggers fired and were acted on across Tasks 3–7, by controller and subagents. If the count is **zero across five tasks**, the checkpoint is not working — the trigger conditions are either unobservable in practice or not being read — and the mechanism must be reconsidered rather than reworded. Also re-check whether the document grew again by accretion: it went 181→249 lines in this restructure, justified as new capability plus scannable tables, but that justification is a claim and should be re-tested | Phase 1a exit | Closed-done |
| D-072 | Two live authorization authorities, and RLS will **not** cover the 9 routes still using `lib/authz.ts` | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Surfaced by the Task 3 subagent (reach assessment, §2/§3) and sharpened by the controller. ADR-0001 says `lib/authz.ts` is "deleted, not extended" because its ownership premise (`assessment.userId !== user.id`) is wrong under tenancy — a colleague in the same org legitimately reads a project they did not create. It is still present and imported by **9 route files** (`app/api/{admin/users/[id]/role, assessments/[id]/complete, assessments/[id]/remediation, assessments/[id], assessments, projects/[id], projects, reports/[id]/pdf, users/me/password}/route.ts`), so `can(role, action)` from Task 3 and `lib/authz.ts` now coexist as two authorities that will drift. **The security characterisation, which had not been stated anywhere:** `lib/authz.ts:43` reads `if (!isAdmin(user) && assessment.userId !== user.id) return null`. For non-admins this is *over*-restrictive under tenancy but fail-closed. For admins it is not: `isAdmin` tests `User.role`, the **global pre-tenancy** `UserRole` enum (`admin｜assessor`), which is unrelated to `OrgRole` and not scoped to any organization — so a global admin reads any assessment in any tenant. Critically, all 9 routes import `lib/db`, which connects via `DATABASE_URL` as `makrai` — **SUPERUSER with BYPASSRLS** — so **Task 5's RLS cannot constrain them**. The isolation spine does not reach these routes until they are ported. This is pre-existing Phase-0 behaviour, not something this plan introduced, but it means "RLS shipped" must not be read as "tenant data is protected" | **Before Plan 1a is described as delivering tenant isolation** — the Plan 1a exit statement must say explicitly that 9 routes bypass RLS via the superuser connection. | D-074 | 22 files are exempted from the `no-restricted-imports` raw-client ban by an explicit ESLint allowlist | DEFERRAL | 2026-08-03 | Task 4 adds the structural guard (ADR-0001 control 6) banning `@/lib/db`, `**/lib/db`, `./db` and `../db` outside `lib/data/`. Enabling it bare would fail `npm run lint` on **22 pre-existing importers**, re-enumerated live at execution time rather than transcribed (`grep -rlE` for imports from `@/lib/db`, `../lib/db` or `./db`, run over `app lib` → 22 files: 20 under `app/`, plus `lib/auth.ts` and `lib/authz.ts` which use `./db`). Because `npm run verify` chains lint → test → build, a bare rule would leave the gate red for the whole of Plan 1b, and a permanently-red gate trains people to ignore it. The allowlist is therefore explicit and **shrinking**, not a blanket suppression: each entry names one file, Plan 1b deletes entries one at a time as each call site moves to `withOrg`/`identityDb`, and adding a new path is a review failure. Same population as D-070's 3 tsc errors and D-072's 9 `lib/authz.ts` routes. **Implementation note carried here because it is a silent-failure class:** the 8 entries containing a Next.js dynamic segment must be written `\\[id\\]`. ESLint `files` entries are globs, and unescaped `[id]` is a picomatch character class matching the single character `i` or `d`, so it never matches a directory literally named `[id]` — observed live 2026-08-03: with plain brackets those 8 files were NOT exempted and `npm run lint` reported 8 errors; escaped, 0 | **The allowlist reaches zero entries** — at which point delete the whole override block from `eslint.config.mjs`. Regression is self-detecting: unescaping a bracket or porting a file without removing its entry both surface immediately as lint errors | Phase 1b | Closed-done |
| D-075 | `makrai_app` has no privilege at all on `users` and `consent_records` | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Task 4's migration grants the app role blanket DML on `public`, then `REVOKE ALL` on `_prisma_migrations`, `users` and `consent_records`. Rationale: `users` holds `passwordHash` and will carry **no** RLS policy (it is non-tenant), so without the revoke a compromise of the app role could read or overwrite every credential in the system with nothing at the database layer to stop it; `_prisma_migrations` would let it rewrite migration history. Verified live on both databases (`has_table_privilege('makrai_app','users','SELECT')` → `f`, `..._prisma_migrations','UPDATE')` → `f`, `...'projects','INSERT')` → `t`). Nothing in Plan 1a reaches these through the app role — `identityDb` and `preauth` both use the owner connection. **The referential-integrity worry was tested, not assumed:** `projects.createdById` references `users(id)`, and an `INSERT INTO projects` as `makrai_app` returned `INSERT 0 1` while a direct `SELECT count(*) FROM users` as the same role returned `ERROR: permission denied for table users` — PostgreSQL runs the FK check with the constraint owner's privileges, so the revoke does not break writes. The accepted risk is the mirror image: the moment Plan 1b moves any identity read onto the app role it will fail with `permission denied`, and the temptation will be to re-grant the whole table | **When Plan 1b moves any part of the identity path off the owner connection onto `makrai_app`** — grant back column-by-column (`GRANT SELECT (id, email, name, isActive) ON users`), explicitly **excluding `passwordHash`**, never a bare `GRANT ... ON users` | Phase 2 | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** Verified the trigger has not fired: `grep -n "owner"` and `grep -n "superuser"` against `lib/data/identity.ts` confirms `identityDb` still connects as the schema owner, and no code in this branch grants `makrai_app` any privilege on `users`/`consent_records` — the accepted-risk posture this row describes is exactly the current state, unmaterialized. Not blocking phase exit: the privilege boundary it protects is intact and verified, not merely assumed. Aligned with D-099 (the load pressure that would tempt the exact re-grant this row warns against), which real production load testing — not Plan 1b — will surface; matches D-040's own "production connection-pool sizing" target of Phase 2. |
| D-076 | The `makrai_app` password has a well-known default and nothing enforces that production overrides it | RISK | 2026-08-03 | The migration deliberately sets **no** password (migrations are committed and run in every environment, so a literal there would publish production's app credential in git — this is the defect the first draft of Task 4 carried). The password is instead provisioned out-of-band by `scripts/provision-app-db-role.sh <db>`, which falls back to the well-known local default `app_dev_password` — matching `vitest.config.ts` — and refuses that default only when `NODE_ENV=production`. Two gaps remain, both accepted for Plan 1a: (1) the `NODE_ENV` guard is the *only* thing standing between a real deployment and a published password, and `NODE_ENV` is trivially unset or wrong in a container — a deployment that forgets it gets `app_dev_password` silently, with just a stderr line; (2) nothing runs the script automatically, so `prisma migrate deploy` alone leaves a **LOGIN role with no password set at all**, and whether that is reachable depends entirely on the server's `pg_hba.conf` auth method rather than on anything in this repo. `npm run db:provision` makes the two-step discoverable; it does not make it enforced. Not exercised in anger yet because hosting is still parked (D-018) **DOCS HALF FIXED 2026-08-12, at the Phase 1d-i C6 exit, on the human partner's ruling. The enforcement half remains open and this row stays Open for it.** The C6 whole-branch security review surfaced a concrete path this row's abstract framing did not make visible: `docs/runbooks/deployment.md` section 4 instructs `cp .env.example .env`, and its `Set production values` block listed seven variables while OMITTING `APP_DATABASE_URL` entirely. So an operator following this project's own written deployment procedure kept the well-known local default in their production `.env` and was never once prompted to think about it. That is a different and likelier failure than the one this row described -- it needs no container misconfiguration, only obedience to the runbook. **Fixed:** `APP_DATABASE_URL` added to that block with a placeholder and an inline warning naming this row. **The ruling was to also replace the working password in `.env.example` with a placeholder; that half was NOT done, and the reason was reported to the human partner rather than silently substituted (AGENTS.md section 2, an assumption in the brief turning out false).** `app_dev_password` is not a stray literal: the identical value appears in `scripts/provision-app-db-role.sh` line 19, `vitest.config.ts` line 23 and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` line 241, and all four must agree or local setup breaks -- a developer who copies a placeholder then runs `npm run db:provision` gets a database password that does not match their `.env`. Instead `.env.example` keeps the working value and gains an explicit warning block, the same shape already used for `ADMIN_PASSWORD` two lines above it. **Also corrected for the record:** the review's framing implied no production guard exists. One does -- `scripts/provision-app-db-role.sh` lines 14-18 exit 1 when `NODE_ENV=production` and `APP_DB_PASSWORD` is unset. This row's original text was right that the guard is the ONLY protection and that `NODE_ENV` is trivially unset in a container; it was the runbook omission, not the absence of a guard, that was newly found. | **When hosting is decided (D-018) or the first non-local deployment is prepared**, whichever is first: move the app-role password to the platform's secret store, make deployment fail closed if `APP_DB_PASSWORD` is unset rather than falling back, and assert in the deploy runbook that provisioning runs after every `migrate deploy` | Phase 2 | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** Neither trigger fired: D-018 (hosting/deployment model) is still Open/unparked, and Plan 1b never touched deployment tooling — verified `scripts/provision-app-db-role.sh` is unchanged from its Task-4-era form (no new commits under `scripts/`). Follows the same target convention already used by D-046, which carries the identical D-018 dependency ("Hosting decision (D-018) or first multi-instance deploy" → Phase 2) — grouping this with the other hosting-gated risk rather than leaving it alone against a closing phase. |
| D-077 | RLS contains a **forgotten filter**, not **injected SQL** — `makrai_app` can re-point `app.current_org_id` itself | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Bounds every isolation claim Plan 1a makes, so it is recorded rather than left implicit. The `org_isolation` policies read `current_setting('app.current_org_id', true)`, and `withOrg` (`lib/data/tenant.ts:70-73`) sets that GUC as the app role — it **must** be able to, or the mechanism cannot work. There is no Postgres facility to make a GUC writable once per transaction, so the same capability is available to anything else running on that connection. Verified live 2026-08-03 over a **real `makrai_app` connection** (`docker exec -e PGPASSWORD=… psql -U makrai_app -d makrai_test`): `SELECT set_config('app.current_org_id','orgA',true)` returned `orgA` and the subsequent `SELECT` scoped to that org — i.e. the role freely chooses its own tenant. **Consequence:** an attacker who achieves SQL injection on the tenant path is not contained by RLS; they simply set the GUC to the victim's org. RLS defends against the *omission* failure (a forgotten `where`), which is the IDOR class that motivated this rebuild — it does not defend against injection. Parameterised queries therefore remain fully load-bearing, and `withOrg` deliberately uses parameterised `set_config(...)` rather than `SET LOCAL` (which cannot be parameterised at all); `__tests__/integration/tenant-layer.test.ts:51-59` pins that a hostile orgId is stored literally. Not mitigable at the database layer with this design; recorded as an accepted, bounded risk | **Any review of query construction on the tenant path** — specifically: the first use of `$queryRawUnsafe`/`$executeRawUnsafe` inside a `withOrg` callback, or any code that builds a tenant-path SQL string by concatenation. At that point the reviewer must treat injection as an *isolation* bug, not merely an integrity one. Also re-read when ADR-0001's "RLS is the authoritative tenant filter" is quoted in any exit statement | — | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** This is a standing structural risk, not phase-bound work — its own trigger is "any review of query construction on the tenant path," an ongoing condition rather than a milestone, so it belongs in the same no-phase bucket as D-006/D-012/D-015/D-018/D-036 rather than against a closing phase. Checked the trigger has not fired in this branch: grepping `lib app` (excluding tests) for both `$queryRawUnsafe` and `$executeRawUnsafe` finds only `lib/data/preauth.ts:477-487`, three fixed-literal `SAVEPOINT`/`ROLLBACK` statements on the **owner** connection (not `withOrg`, not tenant-scoped, no interpolated input) — not the case this row warns about. |
| D-078 | Creating an organization can no longer go through `withOrg` — Plan 1b needs a sanctioned before-context **write** path | DEFERRAL | 2026-08-03 | A deliberate and foreseen consequence of the D-062 fix, recorded before it is discovered as a bug. `organizations` now carries `WITH CHECK (id = NULLIF(current_setting('app.current_org_id', true), ''))`, and a *new* organization is by definition not yet the current org — so `withOrg(ctx, tx => tx.organization.create(...))` cannot succeed for any `ctx`. Verified live over a real `makrai_app` connection on `makrai_test`: the analogous cross-org insert `INSERT INTO projects (…,"orgId",…) VALUES ('pX','orgB',…)` under `app.current_org_id='orgA'` failed with `ERROR: new row violates row-level security policy for table "projects"`; the `organizations` policy has the identical shape keyed on `id`. Nothing is broken today — no code path creates an organization outside tests, and the tests create orgs through `testDb`, which connects as the superuser owner (`vitest.config.ts` `DATABASE_URL`), so all 182 tests pass unchanged. This is a *forced checkpoint*, the same device as D-061: org creation is a before-context write and must be designed as one, in `lib/data/preauth.ts` alongside the before-context reads, rather than discovered mid-feature and "fixed" by weakening or dropping the `organizations` policy — which would silently re-open D-062. **CLOSED 2026-08-03 by the Task 3 commit that adds `bootstrapOrgWithOwner` and `createOrgForUser` to `lib/data/preauth.ts`.** Both write on the owner (BYPASSRLS) connection inside a single `$transaction`, exactly as this row specified, and neither routes through `withOrg` nor weakens `WITH CHECK`. `__tests__/integration/preauth-surface.test.ts`'s export pin was extended deliberately, and its failure was observed live before the fix landed, confirming the forced checkpoint fired as designed. Verified live: `__tests__/integration/bootstrap.test.ts` (12 assertions, including atomicity and "second org for an existing user") and the full suite (`npm test`, 254/254) pass against a real Postgres `organizations` table whose `WITH CHECK` policy is unmodified | **The first feature that creates an organization** (registration-with-new-org, or an admin "create organization" action in Plan 1b). Add the write to `lib/data/preauth.ts` on the owner connection and extend `__tests__/integration/preauth-surface.test.ts`'s export pin, which will otherwise fail and force the decision. **Explicitly forbidden as the fix:** removing `WITH CHECK` from `organizations`, or routing org creation through `withOrg` with a self-referential GUC | Phase 1b | Closed-done |
| D-079 | This migration is **superuser-only** to apply — `CREATE EVENT TRIGGER` cannot be run by a non-superuser | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Found during Task 5 execution, not present in the plan. ADR-0001 control 4 (the DDL guard) is implemented as an event trigger, and PostgreSQL restricts `CREATE EVENT TRIGGER` to superusers with no `GRANT`able alternative. Verified live rather than asserted — attempted as the app role: `docker exec -e PGPASSWORD=app_dev_password -i docker-postgres-1 psql -U makrai_app -d makrai_test` running `CREATE EVENT TRIGGER probe_trg ON ddl_command_end EXECUTE FUNCTION probe_et();` returned `ERROR: permission denied to create event trigger "probe_trg"` / `HINT: Must be superuser to create an event trigger.` (the preceding `CREATE FUNCTION` had already failed with `permission denied for schema public`, so no residue was left; confirmed by `SELECT proname FROM pg_proc WHERE proname='probe_et'` → 0 rows). Locally this is invisible: `makrai` is a superuser and `prisma migrate deploy` succeeded on both databases. It becomes a real constraint on any managed Postgres that withholds superuser — several managed providers do — where this migration would fail outright, or would have to be applied out-of-band by an operator. Accepted for Plan 1a because hosting is parked (D-018) and the local topology satisfies it; recorded because the alternative is discovering it during a first deploy, when the tempting fix is to delete the guard | **When hosting is decided (D-018) or the first non-local deployment is prepared**, whichever is first: confirm the target offers a superuser-or-equivalent role for migrations. If it does not, do **not** drop the event trigger — Task 6's T1 enumeration test is the compensating control and would then become the *only* one, which must be stated explicitly in the exit statement rather than assumed | Phase 2 | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** Same D-018 (hosting) dependency as D-076/D-046, and it has not fired — D-018 is still Open. Grouped with D-076 rather than left alone: both are dormant-until-a-real-deployment risks discovered during Plan 1b's own database work, neither touched by anything Plan 1b actually built. The compensating control this row names (Task 6's T1 enumeration test) is confirmed still present and passing as of this branch. |
| D-080 | Prisma silently DROPS a `where` key whose value is `undefined`, and nothing project-wide prevents it | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Found by the C6 whole-branch `security-review` and reproduced live before fixing: `preauth.invitationByToken(undefined)` returned a **live pending invitation including its token and an `owner` role**; `membershipsForUser(undefined)` returned the platform-wide membership graph; `orgBySlug(undefined)` returned an arbitrary tenant. Cause is `strictUndefinedChecks` being a Prisma preview feature that `prisma/schema.prisma` does not enable, so `undefined` elides the filter instead of raising. The declared parameter types say `string`, which does not help: callers are route handlers reading `await req.json()`, typed `any`. **Fixed at the module boundary** in `lib/data/preauth.ts` (all three functions validate the key and return `null`/`[]`), pinned by 5 tests in `__tests__/integration/data-boundary.test.ts`, and proven non-vacuous — reverting the fix turns 3 of them red. Not blocking: the two live-reproduced exploits this row documents were fixed at the module boundary in `lib/data/preauth.ts` and are pinned non-vacuously by `__tests__/integration/data-boundary.test.ts`; route-level `params` values (e.g. `[id]`, `[slug]`) are Next.js-guaranteed strings, not the `req.json()`-derived `any` that caused the original exploit, so the remaining exposure is defense-in-depth rather than a known live path. Precisely because the blocking condition (9 unreviewed routes) it cited for NOT flipping the flag has since closed, this is now cheap to actually do and worth scheduling rather than leaving open against Phase 1b indefinitely. **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** `grep -n "strictUndefinedChecks" prisma/schema.prisma` still returns nothing -- the global flag remains unenabled. Phase 1c's new routes take `evidenceId`/`assessmentId` from Next.js route params (guaranteed strings, not `req.json()`-derived `any`), so the residual exposure this row already described as "defense-in-depth rather than a known live path" is unchanged, not worsened. Still not blocking; still cheap; still worth scheduling. |
| D-081 | `identityDb`'s type boundary did not stop Prisma relation traversal, and its own comment claimed it did | BUG | 2026-08-03 | Found by the C6 whole-branch `security-review`. `Pick<PrismaClient, 'user' or 'consentRecord'>` closes `$queryRaw*`, `$executeRaw*`, `$transaction` and future models, but hands over the **entire** `user` delegate — and `User` has relations to five orgId-bearing models. Reproduced live: `identityDb.user.findMany({ include: { memberships: { include: { org: true } }, projects: true, sentInvitations: true } })` type-checks, passes lint (`lib/data/**` is exempt from the import ban), passes every RLS test because it never touches `makrai_app`, and returns other organizations' projects, the full membership graph and live invitation tokens over the SUPERUSER/BYPASSRLS owner connection. The nested-write form (`data.memberships.updateMany`) performed an unauthorized cross-tenant role change with `can()` never consulted. **The docblock asserted the boundary was closed**, which is worse than silence — a reviewer trusts it. Fixed with a runtime guard (`assertNoTenantRelation`, applied via `$extends` argument inspection only — the Task-0 `$extends` NO-GO concerned connection state and does not apply) plus a corrected comment; 4 tests pin it, proven non-vacuous. Residual: `TENANT_RELATIONS` is a **literal list**, so a tenant relation added to `User` in Plan 1b widens the bypass unless the list is updated **Reopened and re-fixed 2026-08-03 after an adversarial review BLOCKED the first two fixes.** The Proxy that replaced the type-only narrowing was itself escaped: `identityDb.user.$parent` is an own property of Prisma's delegate pointing back at the **unproxied** client, and the reviewer reproduced `.project.findMany()`, `.$queryRawUnsafe('SELECT … FROM projects')`, `.$transaction(tx => tx.invitation.findMany())` returning a live `owner` invitation token, and a cross-org `membership.create` granting itself `owner` in a victim organization. Symbol keys skipped the trap outright (`typeof prop === 'string'`). Separately, `_count: true` — the shorthand, with no child keys for the recursive walk to reach — expanded to every relation and leaked cross-org project/membership/invitation counts as a per-user oracle. **Now fixed by CONSTRUCTION rather than interception**: `identityDb` is a frozen object built from an explicit operation allowlist, so `$parent` is absent because nothing copies it, not because something denies it. Verified after the fix: `Object.keys` is exactly `['$connect','$disconnect','consentRecord','user']`, `getOwnPropertySymbols` is empty, `user.$parent` is `undefined`, both `_count` forms are rejected, legitimate identity counts still work, and `$connect`/`util.inspect`/spread/`JSON.stringify` — all of which the Proxy had broken — work again. | **Whenever a relation is added to `User` or `ConsentRecord`** — add it to `TENANT_RELATIONS` in `lib/data/identity.ts` in the same commit. Also re-evaluate whether a hand-written delegate interface (no `include`) should replace the guard once Plan 1b knows which operations identity actually needs | Phase 1b | Closed-done — **2026-08-05, Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** The row's own text already narrates the CONSTRUCTION fix in full; this closure verifies it live rather than trusting the prose. `lib/data/identity.ts:68` defines `TENANT_RELATIONS`, `:229`/`:232` build the exposed surface via `Object.freeze`, matching the "frozen object from an explicit allowlist" description. `__tests__/integration/data-boundary.test.ts` (16 tests) asserts exactly the claims the row makes — `Object.keys(surface)` is the closed set, `$parent` is `undefined`, `getOwnPropertySymbols` is empty, both `_count` forms (shorthand and nested) are rejected — and ran clean on this tree just now: `npx vitest run __tests__/integration/data-boundary.test.ts` → 16/16 passed. Residual named by the row itself (`TENANT_RELATIONS` is a literal list, must be updated when a relation is added) is not a defect, it is the row's own documented maintenance obligation, carried forward in the file's comments rather than needing its own register row. |

Two compounding causes: none was bound to a moment, and v0.1 of the inventory **listed `silent-failure-hunter` among skills when it is an Agent** (`subagent_type`), so "invoke the skill" would have failed even if someone had tried. **Fixed:** §2 now binds it to "about to write error handling, a fallback, a catch, or a default", and inventory §4 separates skills from agents from MCP tools **CLOSED 2026-08-03 — dispatched, and it earned the row.** `pr-review-toolkit:silent-failure-hunter` ran over the branch and found the two most serious defects in it: `identityDb`'s narrowing was type-only, so the runtime object still carried `project`, `$queryRawUnsafe` and `$transaction` (all confirmed working against the owner connection); and no client validated its connection string, so an unset `APP_DATABASE_URL` lets `pg` fall through to `PG*` and libpq defaults and could silently connect as the SUPERUSER owner with every control still appearing to function. | D-086 | No connection-string validation: an unset `APP_DATABASE_URL` could silently become the **owner** connection | BUG | 2026-08-03 | Found by `silent-failure-hunter` in the C6 re-drive and reproduced by the controller. `new Pool({ connectionString: undefined })` does **not** throw — `pg/lib/connection-parameters.js` tests the value for truthiness and otherwise falls through to `PG*` env vars and libpq defaults (`host: 'localhost'`, `user: process.env.USER`). `PGUSER`/`PGPASSWORD` are conventional in containers and CI, and `.env.example` hands an operator the OWNER credential, so a missing or misspelled `APP_DATABASE_URL` could make `appClient` a SUPERUSER/BYPASSRLS connection: `withOrg` still sets the GUC, every policy still exists, every test still passes, and RLS is bypassed for every query with a correct-looking HTTP 200. **The suite structurally cannot catch this** — `vitest.config.ts` hardcodes both URLs, so it proves the test config, not the deployed one. **Fixed** by `lib/data/connection.ts` (load-time refusal on all three clients) plus a once-per-process assertion inside `withOrg` that the live connection is neither `rolsuper` nor `rolbypassrls` — the only check that verifies the architecture's central assumption against reality rather than against configuration | **When a deployment target exists**, confirm the identity assertion runs on a real connection there and is not disabled for startup performance | Phase 1b | Closed-done — **2026-08-05, Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** Verified live rather than trusting the row's own "Fixed" claim: `lib/data/connection.ts:23-56` defines `requireDatabaseUrl`, refusing an unset connection string at load time rather than falling through to `PG*`/libpq defaults, with an in-line comment naming exactly this row's threat. `lib/data/tenant.ts:122-148` implements the once-per-process assertion, querying `current_user`/`rolsuper`/`rolbypassrls` on the live connection and throwing if either flag is true. `npx vitest run __tests__/integration/isolation.test.ts` → 14/14 passing on this tree. The pick-up trigger ("when a deployment target exists, confirm the assertion runs there") cannot be discharged — no deployment target exists yet (D-018 still Open) — so it is not carried forward as a new row; whoever picks up D-018 inherits it as an implicit part of standing the app up for real. |
| D-087 | T1 could not see a tenant table with RLS enabled but **no policy** — the exact state its own event trigger creates | BUG | 2026-08-03 | Found by `silent-failure-hunter`. Reproduced live: `CREATE TABLE "trigger_made_me"(id text primary key, "orgId" text not null)` yields `rls=true force=true policies=0` and the old T1 returned green, so a table that reads empty for every user in every organization would ship with a green suite and a `RAISE NOTICE` surfaced nowhere. The same blind spot hid a **dropped** policy: only `projects` and `memberships` were covered behaviourally, so removing `org_isolation` from `assessments` left the whole suite green while every assessment read returned zero rows. T1 was also vacuously satisfiable — `toEqual([])` passes on an empty or unmigrated schema. **Fixed**: the predicate now also fails on a missing `org_isolation` policy, and a second test asserts the protected population is exactly the six expected tables. | D-088 | `withOrg`'s transaction handle still exposes `$executeRaw`, so a callback can re-scope the org GUC mid-transaction | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Found by `type-design-analyzer`; mechanism verified by the controller. Prisma 7's `ITXClientDenyList` is `["$connect","$disconnect","$on","$use","$extends"]` — lifecycle methods only — so `Prisma.TransactionClient` retains `$executeRaw`/`$queryRaw`. A callback can therefore run `set_config('app.current_org_id', <any org>, true)` and re-scope itself for the remainder of the transaction; RLS cannot prevent it, since setting a GUC is unprivileged (the same limit as D-077). **Chosen not to fix now, with reasons:** the callback is first-party code, so this is a footgun rather than an attacker path, and narrowing the type to a `Pick` over model delegates would break the tenant-layer and isolation tests, which legitimately use `tx.$queryRaw` to assert the GUC and would need a separately-provided raw handle. What *was* done: the hand-rolled `Parameters<Parameters<PrismaClient['$transaction']>[0]>[0]` — which resolved correctly only because `Parameters<>` picks the **last** overload and the interactive form happens to be last — is replaced by the generated `Prisma.TransactionClient`, and the docblock now states exactly what is and is not excluded | **When Plan 1b ports its first route to `withOrg`**, decide then whether to narrow `TenantTx` to model delegates and give the GUC tests their own handle | Phase 1b+ | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation, with justification, not silently carried.** The trigger fired long ago — Task 8 ported every remaining call site onto `withOrg` (commit `01179fa`) — but the narrowing decision was never revisited: verified `lib/data/tenant.ts:64` still defines `export type TenantTx = Prisma.TransactionClient` unchanged, no narrower delegate type exists. | D-091 | The provisioning script passes the production password as an `argv` element, readable via `ps` | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Found by `silent-failure-hunter`, and it undercuts the reason the password was moved out of the migration in the first place. `scripts/provision-app-db-role.sh` invokes `docker exec … psql -v pw="$APP_DB_PASSWORD"`, so the value appears in the process table and any user on the host can read it while the command runs. Also noted: `docker exec -i docker-postgres-1` is hardcoded, so the `NODE_ENV=production` refusal branch is written for an environment in which the rest of the script cannot execute at all — the guard and the transport disagree about what the script is for. | D-092 | Guards on this branch have repeatedly closed the bypass shapes their author enumerated and left the class open — four times, and three of those were fixes for the previous one | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-03 | Recorded because it is the branch's dominant defect mechanism and it predicts the next one. The instances: (1) the ESLint ban caught `@/lib/db`, `../../lib/db` and `./db` and missed `../../db`, then after widening still missed `await import()` and `require()` because `no-restricted-imports` sees only static declarations; (2) the DDL-trigger pin caught the trigger's existence and all three command tags and missed `evtenabled`, so `ALTER EVENT TRIGGER … DISABLE` passed it; (3) `assertNoTenantRelation` v1 checked five named clauses at depth 1 and missed nesting, `where` combinators, `_count`, array `orderBy` and `upsert`; (4) the Proxy that replaced the type-only narrowing was escaped via `user.$parent` and symbol keys, and the recursive walk still missed `_count: true`. **In every case a test pinned exactly the shapes the author had thought of and passed.** The common root is that each guard *intercepted* — it enumerated what to deny — and an interception guard is only as good as the reachability graph its author imagined. What actually worked: building the surface by construction (nothing to escape through), deriving the denied set from generated types (`keyof Prisma.UserInclude`) so the compiler maintains it, and asserting a control's *behaviour* rather than its *presence* (T1 now checks the policy predicate, not that a policy exists). **The mechanical lesson, now in AGENTS.md C4: an independent reviewer found all four; the author's own re-reading found none of them** | **At the C1 of any task that writes a guard**: state the *class* it must close, then have someone other than the author try to escape it — `engineering-skills:adversarial-reviewer` or `pr-review-toolkit:silent-failure-hunter`, both now bound in §2. If the only evidence is a test written by the guard's author, the guard is unreviewed | — | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** Standing process trigger ("at the C1 of any task that writes a guard"), not phase-bound — belongs with D-082/D-077/D-036 rather than against a closing phase. This row's own prediction proved true rather than being a one-off: Task 12's fix-round-2 commit (`6cffe94`) names itself "guard number six in this project's D-092 pattern (five prior guards each closed the shape their author enumerated and left the class open)" — the pattern recurred twice more after this row was written. Mixed evidence, recorded honestly rather than rounded to closed or reopened-as-worse: round 2 DID apply the row's own "what actually worked" prescription — a Control registry plus a completeness census that walks every rendered control and fails on a missing registry entry (`e2e/role-matrix.spec.ts`), asserting behaviour rather than enumerating shapes — but `grep -n "adversarial-reviewer" task-12-report.md` shows no independent adversarial review was dispatched for that round, only `silent-failure-hunter` (not new). The lesson is being applied unevenly: the construction fix, yes; the independent-reviewer discipline, not consistently. |

(1) There is **no** CI workflow, pre-commit hook, husky config, gitleaks config or detect-secrets baseline in the repository — the skill's central recommendation ("enforce detection in CI before merge") has nothing to attach to, and `npm run verify` is red anyway (D-070), so no gate protects this branch at all. (2) The skill's own `env_auditor.py` returned **10 HIGH findings, every one a false positive** (a React `onChange` handler, test fixtures, documented example passwords in spec files) while missing **every** real committed credential — six tracked files carry `postgresql://user:password@host` pairs and its patterns do not match connection strings. Wiring it in as-is would produce pure noise and zero signal here. Also noted: `.env.example` contains working local credentials rather than placeholders, which the skill lists as its first common pitfall — benign today because the values are local-only, but it means copying the example yields a live config instead of a template that forces a decision | **When CI is first set up** (Phase 4, or earlier if a remote is added): add secret scanning with patterns that actually match connection strings, and verify it flags a deliberately planted `postgresql://u:p@h/db` before trusting it | Phase 4 | Open |
| D-094 | A VIEW over a tenant table bypassed RLS entirely, and every control on the branch missed it | BUG | 2026-08-03 | Found by the final C6 security review and reproduced by the controller before fixing. Three mechanisms combined: the DDL guard filters `object_type = 'table'`, so `CREATE VIEW` skipped it; `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES … ON TABLES` covers views, so `makrai_app` was auto-granted `arwd`; and because the view is owned by the SUPERUSER `makrai` and PostgreSQL's `security_invoker` defaults to **false**, the base table's RLS was evaluated with the *view owner's* rights. Measured as `makrai_app` with **no org context at all**: `SELECT count(*) FROM projects` → **0** (fails closed, correct) while the same data through a view → **2 rows across two organizations**. That inverts the invariant the whole architecture rests on, which ADR-0001 states as "forgetting to use withOrg fails CLOSED". T1 could not see it either — it filtered `relkind = 'r'`. **Fixed** by migration `20260803140500`: a second event trigger raises on any `public` view or materialized view created without `security_invoker = true`, the default privilege grant is revoked, and T1's filter widens to `('r','p','v','m')`. Pinned by two tests. **The lesson is D-092's again**: ADR-0001 control 4 claimed a forgotten policy was "structurally impossible", and it was — for the one relation kind its author had in mind | **If a reporting view, materialized view or partitioned tenant table is added**, confirm the guard still covers it; `security_invoker` is PG15+, so a downgrade below that would silently remove this control | Phase 1b | Closed-done — **2026-08-05, Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** Verified live: `prisma/migrations/20260803140500_guard_views_and_narrow_default_privileges` exists on disk, and `__tests__/integration/isolation.test.ts` + `data-boundary.test.ts` (30 tests combined) pass clean on this tree (`npx vitest run` just run). No reporting view, materialized view or partitioned tenant table has been added on this branch since, so the pick-up trigger has not fired and there is nothing further to check. |
| D-095 | Database referential actions reach tenant tables on the owner connection, and an argument-inspecting guard cannot see them | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Found by the final C6 review. `identityDb.user.delete({ where: { id } })` names no relation, so `assertNoTenantRelation` passes it — and `memberships_userId_fkey ON DELETE CASCADE` then deletes that user's memberships across every organization, on the BYPASSRLS owner connection, with `can()` never consulted. Reproduced live: deleting one user silently removed two memberships in two different orgs. `Project`/`Assessment`/`Invitation` FKs to `users` are `RESTRICT`, so they are protected by accident rather than by design. **Fixed for now by removing `delete` and `deleteMany` from `EXPOSED_OPERATIONS`** — nothing in Plan 1a deletes users — but the *class* is open: any future operation whose effect is produced by the database rather than by the query arguments is invisible to a guard that inspects arguments | **When Plan 1b needs to delete a user**, add a named `deleteUser()` that tears down memberships explicitly through `withOrg`, rather than re-adding `delete` and relying on a referential action | Phase 1b | Closed-done — **2026-08-05, Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** The trigger fired — `DELETE /api/users/me` exists (`app/api/users/me/route.ts`) — and was discharged exactly as prescribed, not by re-adding `delete`/`deleteMany`: verified `EXPOSED_OPERATIONS` in `lib/data/identity.ts:133-144` still excludes both, with the exclusion comment naming this exact cascade risk; `scrubUserOnDeactivation` (`lib/data/identity.ts:290-338`) is the named function, running on `base` as a documented singular reviewed exception rather than a widened allowlist, and never issues a `User` delete (deactivate-and-scrub, not delete); the route (`app/api/users/me/route.ts:70-101`) tears down every membership explicitly through `withOrg`, one org at a time inside a `FOR UPDATE`-guarded owner-count check, BEFORE calling `scrubUserOnDeactivation` — matching this row's own prescription verbatim. |
| D-096 | `requireDatabaseUrl` covers three PG* fields, not the PG* class — and `appClient` is exported, so `withOrg` is not the only path | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Two residuals from the final C6 review, kept together because they compose. (1) `pg` resolves `PG*` **per field**; the URL check covers `user`, `hostname` and `database` only, leaving `port`, `password`, `ssl` and — the security-relevant one — `options`. `PGOPTIONS='-c app.current_org_id=<org>'` pre-sets the isolation GUC on every pooled connection, so a query that never called `withOrg` returns that org's rows, inverting the fail-closed property T2 asserts.
