| D-097 | Invitation tokens are stored **plaintext**, where the design spec required a sha256 digest | BUG | 2026-08-03 | `prisma/schema.prisma` ships `token String @unique`; `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-08-02-phase1-foundation-design.md` §2.1 specifies `tokenHash` (sha256) with the raw token existing "only in the email", and also specifies an `acceptedAt` column that is likewise absent. The divergence was introduced in Plan 1a Task 1 and reviewed by nobody against the spec, because every review compared the diff to its own task brief rather than to the design. Consequence, stated concretely: **any read of the `invitations` table yields working capabilities** — a database backup, a log line, a `SELECT` by an operator, or an ORM bypass hands over the ability to join an organization at a role the row itself names. This is not hypothetical on this branch: the C6 review reproduced `invitationByToken(undefined)` returning a live pending invitation *with its token and `owner` role* (D-080), and the adversarial review separately reached live invitation tokens through `identityDb.user.$parent` (D-081). Hashing would not have prevented either bug; it would have made both leaks worthless. Note the raw token is still needed once — to build the delivered link — so the change is generate → hash → store digest → return the raw value to the sender exactly once  **CLOSED, Task 8 (commit 71aea44).** `prisma/schema.prisma` already shipped `tokenHash TEXT UNIQUE` with the `invitations_tokenHash_is_sha256_hex` CHECK (Task 2); Task 8 adds the write side that actually exercises it: `createInvitation` (lib/data/members.ts) generates `randomBytes(32)`, hashes with sha256 before the `invitation.create` write, and returns the raw token to the caller exactly once. Verified live: `__tests__/integration/invitations.test.ts` "stores only a digest — no stored value works as a token (O-7)" asserts every persisted `tokenHash` matches `/^[0-9a-f]{64}$/`; full suite green (`npm run verify` exit 0).| **Plan 1b's invitation work, before any invitation is created outside a test.** Migration adds `tokenHash` + `acceptedAt` and drops `token`; `preauth.invitationByToken` looks up by digest; assert in test that no stored value functions as a token (proof obligation O-7). Both databases hold zero invitation rows today, so the migration needs no backfill — a property that expires the moment a real invitation exists | Phase 1b | Closed-done |
| D-098 | Invitation acceptance has no **email binding** designed, so whoever holds the link joins the organization | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Surfaced by the ADR-0002 threat model (STRIDE / Q3), and recorded now because the acceptance flow does not exist yet — this is a design obligation, not a shipped defect, and it is exactly the kind that is cheap now and expensive after the flow is written. `invitationByToken` authenticates the *token*, not the *person*. With email delivery the link is forwardable; with the copy-link fallback (D-030) it is pasteable into the wrong channel. Either way an invitation addressed to one person becomes organizational access for another, at the role the invitation names, and the resulting `Membership` is indistinguishable from a legitimate one. The binding must differ by case: for an email that already has an account, the accepting session's email must match; for a new account, the email is fixed from the invitation and not editable on the registration form. Neither rule is expressible in the schema, so both need tests. Interacts with ADR-0002 decision 9: never create a second `User` for an email that already has one, and never authenticate anyone as a side effect of acceptance  **CLOSED, Task 8 (commit 71aea44).** Both binding rules implemented in `acceptInvitation` (lib/data/preauth.ts): existing-account case compares `identity.email` (read fresh from the database by `requireIdentityForApi`, never the JWT claim) against `invitation.email`, case-insensitively; new-account case derives the email from the invitation row server-side in `POST /api/v1/invitations/[token]/register` — the form has no editable email field at all. `role` is read from `inv.role` inside the same transaction, never from the caller's input (O-5). Replay is refused by a conditional `updateMany` gated on `status: 'pending'`, proven non-vacuous live (see the Task 8 report). Tests: "refuses a forwarded link for a different account", "remains acceptable by the correct invitee after a mismatched attempt", "takes the role from the row, ignoring anything the caller supplies (O-5)", "creates exactly one membership when two different users race the same token" — all green.| **The task that implements invitation acceptance.** Ships with tests for: forwarded link refused for a different authenticated user; invited email non-editable on the new-account form; `role` taken from the row and not the request body (O-5); replay creates no second membership (O-6) | Phase 1b | Closed-done |
| D-099 | Per-request identity reads concentrate load on the **owner** connection, and the obvious performance fix re-opens D-075 | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Second-order consequence of ADR-0002 decisions 3–5, found by the `what-if-oracle` ∞ branch. Moving `role` and `mustChangePassword` out of the token means every authenticated request performs an identity read — and identity reads run on the owner connection (SUPERUSER, BYPASSRLS) because `makrai_app` deliberately holds no privilege on `users` (D-075). So **hardening session revocation increases traffic against the one pool in the system that bypasses tenant isolation**, currently `max: 5`. Under load the pool saturates and the natural-looking remedy is `GRANT SELECT ON users TO makrai_app`, which is precisely what D-075's own pick-up trigger predicts in writing: *"the temptation will be to re-grant the whole table."* The danger is the disguise — the pressure arrives as a latency graph, so the decision gets taken as a performance fix by whoever is on call, without the security review that a privilege grant would otherwise attract | **Any of: p95 latency on the owner pool, `identityDb` connection timeouts, or a proposal to grant `makrai_app` privilege on `users`.** Sanctioned responses in order: per-request memoisation (`cache()`), then pool sizing. If a grant ever becomes genuinely necessary it is column-scoped and excludes `passwordHash` (`GRANT SELECT (id, email, name, isActive, sessionEpoch) ON users`), never a bare table grant | Phase 2 | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** None of the three named triggers (owner-pool p95 latency, `identityDb` connection timeouts, a proposal to grant `makrai_app` privilege on `users`) has fired — no load test has been run against this branch (matches D-040/D-065's own unproven-pool-sizing rows, both already targeting Phase 2). Real per-request load is a production-traffic question this phase's synthetic spike (D-005) never modeled; sits with D-040/D-065 rather than blocking a phase that never load-tested anything. |
| D-100 | `docs/adr/README.md` indexed a **nonexistent** ADR-0002 as `Accepted`, and nothing checks cross-document references | BUG | 2026-08-03 | Found while deriving ADR-0002. The index on `main` linked `0002-identity-and-account-linking.md`, status `Accepted`; the file existed only on `rollback/phase1a-unadherent`. So the ADR index asserted the identity decision was settled while D-066 recorded that it was deliberately not derived — two project records in direct contradiction, with a reader meeting the wrong one first. Fixed in the same commit that adds the real ADR. **The instance is trivial; the class is not, and the class is the finding.** It survived a rollback, a full C6 phase-exit audit and a merge, because every mechanism this project has checks *code* against reality — `tsc`, ESLint, the test suite, `git status --untracked-files=all` — and not one checks whether a document's references resolve. A dangling link is invisible to all of them. This is also the documentation-deficit failure the product itself exists to diagnose (framework areas PO-03, PO-07), committed in our own repo, which AGENTS.md §6 says we do not get to do. Per §0.4, the fix is a mechanism and not a reminder | **Plan 1b's first documentation task.** Add a test that walks every relative markdown link under `docs/` and fails on a missing target, and wire it into `npm run verify`. Cheap (one test, no new dependency) and self-detecting thereafter | Phase 1b | Closed-done — **2026-08-05, Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** The specific instance this BUG names is fixed: verified `docs/adr/README.md:37` now indexes the real `0002-identity-and-account-linking.md`, and the file exists (commit `4fe15c3`). **The pick-up trigger's mechanism did NOT ship** — checked directly: `grep -rl "link.*check" __tests__ scripts` returns nothing, and `package.json`'s `"verify"` script is `npm run lint && npm run test && npm run build`, no link-checker anywhere in the chain. Closing the instance rather than leaving it open against a mechanism nobody in this plan was ever assigned to build; the mechanism itself is spun off as its own row, D-132, rather than closed silently — see D-132. |
| D-101 | Organization existence is disclosed by slug collision at creation, and the two 404 branches are timing-distinguishable | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Two residuals accepted by ADR-0002 decision 10, recorded rather than solved. (1) An org-creation form that reports *"that slug is taken"* is a plain existence oracle: it answers "does organization X use this platform?" for any guessed slug, defeating the 404 policy that the rest of the decision establishes. Server-side slug derivation (accept the display name, derive a unique slug, never report a collision) avoids it entirely and is the intended fix. (2) The two branches that both return 404 — "slug unknown" and "slug exists but caller is not a member" — perform different numbers of database queries and are therefore distinguishable by response time, which recovers the same information the status code withholds. Mitigated by always performing both lookups regardless of the first result. Severity is low in isolation and material in aggregate: for an assurance tool, *which institutions are running AI risk assessments* is competitively and reputationally sensitive, and it is the customer list. **Fix 1 done 2026-08-03 by Task 3.** `deriveSlug` + `createOrgInTx` in `lib/data/preauth.ts` derive the slug server-side and never surface a collision — `bootstrap.test.ts`'s "never reports a slug collision to the caller" test proves the second of two same-named orgs gets a random suffix, not an error. **Residual even with fix 1 done, worth carrying rather than closing quietly:** a registrant who submits "Acme Corp" and gets back `acme-corp-3f2b` still learns that `acme-corp` was already taken — one registration attempt per probe, slower than a direct oracle but not eliminated. **Fix 2 (the timing side-channel on `requireOrgContext`'s two 404 branches) remains entirely open** — Task 3 does not touch that code path | **Fix 1: done, this row is half-closed.** **Fix 2 remains: the task that implements `requireOrgContext`** (unconditional double lookup so both 404 branches cost the same). Neither is a follow-up: it is a one-line-shaped decision that is free at implementation time and awkward afterwards | Phase 1d | Open — **Fix 2 done, Task 5; one narrower residual remains, re-verified 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation (the Status column previously held this narrative with no leading status word, a formatting defect fixed here without changing its substance).** `requireOrgContextFor` (`lib/auth/context.ts`) runs `orgBySlug(slug)` and `membershipsForUser(userId)` via `Promise.all`, unconditionally, before either result is inspected — confirmed by `silent-failure-hunter` review (task-5-report.md) that no code path short-circuits one lookup on the other's outcome, and pinned by `org-context.test.ts`'s same-error-class assertion. **What has changed since this text was written: the premise "no route calls `requireOrgContext` yet" is now false.** Verified live: `requireOrgContext`/`requireOrgContextFor` are now called from many real routes (`app/api/v1/orgs/[slug]/projects/route.ts`, `.../assessments/route.ts`, `.../reports/[id]/pdf/route.ts`, the `/orgs/[slug]` layout, and others), and `e2e/org-routing.spec.ts`/`e2e/two-orgs.spec.ts` assert both the unknown-slug and not-a-member cases return 404 through real HTTP requests. **What remains genuinely unmeasured**: a dedicated timing-side-channel measurement (comparing actual response latency between the two 404 branches over real HTTP) does not exist — the live tests assert same status code and same error class, not response-time symmetry. Low severity (this is defense-in-depth against a slow, noisy timing oracle, not a live exploit), not blocking phase exit. Target moved to Phase 1c for this narrower residual rather than left against a closing phase. **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** `lib/auth/context.ts` was touched once this phase (commit 8e29222, Task 11), but only additively -- a new `orgContextsForUser` function for the evidence export route. Read `requireOrgContextFor`'s body directly: it is unchanged in structure, still an unconditional `Promise.all([orgBySlug, membershipsForUser])` with both branches throwing the same `NotFoundError`. The dedicated timing measurement this row asks for still does not exist. |
| D-102 | Playwright's Chromium does not launch on this machine, and Plan 1b's definition of done now depends on it | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Observed during Plan 1a's C5 verification: Playwright's bundled Chrome failed on a Qt platform-plugin error while the `chrome-devtools` MCP drove the same app successfully, so the work was verified live through the MCP instead and the incident was recorded **only** as a footnote in `docs/SKILLS_INVENTORY.md` §3 — never as a register row, because at the time an alternative launcher existed and the obligation was satisfied. That reasoning expired at the Plan 1b spec review: the human partner ruled the role × permission matrix must be **exhaustive live** as well as exhaustive at the integration level (2 orgs × 5 roles × 2 members, every role walking every screen), and a 20-user browser matrix driven through an MCP one call at a time is not a realistic substitute for a Playwright suite with `storageState` reuse and parallel workers. So a footnote about a tool that "has a workaround" has become a hard dependency of the phase exit. Note the shape of the failure this row exists to prevent: the launcher is needed at step 12, so the natural sequencing discovers it last, at the exact moment when the tempting response is to weaken the exit criterion rather than fix the tooling | **Step 0 of Plan 1b, before the fixture is built.** Timeboxed investigation with `superpowers:systematic-debugging` (D-084's lesson — one error message is a symptom, not a diagnosis; the Qt error most likely names a missing system library that `npx playwright install-deps` supplies). If Playwright cannot be made to launch, decide the verification vehicle explicitly and record it, rather than discovering the constraint at step 12 **CLOSED 2026-08-03 at Plan 1b Task 0 — the failure does not reproduce.** Verified by the controller independently of the implementer's report, per C4: `npm run test:e2e` launched 3 parallel Chromium workers and ran all 12 specs — **11 passed, 1 failed on an application defect (see below), 0 launcher failures**. No code change was required; the task produced zero commits and left the tree clean. **Residual, deliberately not closed with it:** the *reason* the Qt platform-plugin error stopped reproducing is inferred (most likely a system Qt/GTK `t64` library transition on this Ubuntu 24.04 host) and was not proven inside the 60-minute timebox. Since the exhaustive-live exit criterion depends entirely on this launcher, **re-verify at the start of Task 12 rather than trusting this closure indefinitely** — a working tool today is a derived conclusion that silently ages (§5). **Byproduct worth more than the closure:** the single failing spec, `e2e/project.spec.ts:33 "create a new project after login"`, is *not* an unrelated bug as first reported — it is D-070's third type error manifesting at runtime (`app/api/projects/route.ts(46,5)`: a `Project` built with no `orgId`). It is therefore the first **live, in-browser** proof that this application cannot create a project, a claim previously resting on static analysis alone, and it is a ready-made regression test that Task 7 must turn green | Phase 1b | Closed-done |
| D-103 | Design-time completeness has no checkpoint: three defects in one session all occurred **inside enumerations**, and self-review is 0-for-7 on this class | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-03 | Raised by the human partner at the Plan 1b spec review: *"systems thinking and separation of concerns is consistently getting lost… the impact has a radius."* Investigated with `rigor-loop` rather than conceded, and the claim holds with one sharpening that makes it fixable. **The three defects share a shape:** (1) an exhaustive proof-obligation list and an exhaustive route port, with nothing asking whether the RBAC matrix connects to the routes — so `can()` could be perfect while every route consulted the wrong action; (2) a 20-file inventory built from one lens ("imports `prisma`") that missed 8 pages including project creation; (3) a definition-of-done gate claiming an invitation into the *same* org demonstrated *two-org isolation*. In every case **each element was correct and the relation was wrong** — connection, completeness, correspondence. **The sharpening:** relation-thinking is not absent, it is *predictably* absent. The same session produced D-099, D-100 and the PDF-transaction finding, all relation findings. It fires when a skill mandates a slot for it or when an anomaly is encountered; it fails while producing an enumeration, because attending to N correct elements generates a completion signal proportional to N that substitutes for the completeness question. **The sharpest evidence is the cross-check that "passed":** a 22-file inventory matched Plan 1a's ESLint allowlist *exactly* and was reported as validation — but the allowlist enumerates files importing `lib/db` and the inventory was built by finding files importing `lib/db`. The same measurement twice. A loose match would have prompted a closer look; the exactness disarmed it. **Why nothing caught it:** AGENTS.md §3 had two obligations, both about a *unit* — a new unit's interactions, a changed unit's dependents. Neither asks whether a **set** of parts is complete or whether the parts compose, and C1–C6 contains no checkpoint that fires on "I have just decomposed something". Decomposition is where this is most needed and least likely to fire, because breaking a thing into parts *feels* like understanding the whole. **A correction the human partner made mid-investigation, kept because it improved the fix:** I claimed nothing mechanical catches conceptual absence and only independent review has a record. That was too absolute — `what-if-oracle`'s **Ψ Wild Card** ("a variable nobody is tracking") and **∞ Second Order** are built for exactly this and are 2-for-2 here (D-099 from ∞; the shared-lab-machine finding that set session `maxAge` from Ψ). The limit that survives is narrower and more useful: **the oracle finds absence within a decision you have named, and cannot reach a decision you never named**, because its Phase 1 requires naming the variable. §2 bound it only to "you reach a hard-to-reverse fork", which requires *recognising* the fork — the same recall dependency §0 exists to eliminate | **Fixed in this commit, in three layers, and the fix carries its own falsifiable test.** Layer 1 (mechanism, §3): where a list must be complete, generate it from the source of truth — `ROUTE_ACTIONS` enumerated from disk, the page checklist from `app/**/page.tsx`, D-100's link checker; precedent is `Record<keyof Prisma.UserInclude, …>`, the generated RBAC matrix, and T1's `pg_class` enumeration. Layer 2 (§2 trigger + §3 obligation): on producing any enumeration, name the generating property, regenerate under a different property and diff, state what connects the items, state the claim each verification item proves, and run `what-if-oracle` on the decomposition itself. Layer 3 (§2): `adversarial-reviewer` on every design artifact before it reaches the human partner, briefed to attack the decomposition rather than review the content. **Falsifiable test, as D-071 did it:** if across Plan 1b the enumeration trigger fires and the second lens never produces a diff, **or** the human partner again finds a completeness defect the trigger should have caught, then the mechanism is not working and must be *reconsidered rather than reworded*. Honest limit recorded rather than hidden: Layer 1 caught only 1 of the 3 retrospective defects; Layer 2 caught 3 of 3 but is prose with a trigger; Layer 3 caught 3 of 3 and costs an invocation per artifact | — | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation** (this row's own falsifiable test is a standing trigger over the rest of the project's life, not phase-bound, so it belongs with D-006/D-012/D-015/D-018/D-036/D-077/D-082/D-092 rather than against a closing phase); re-verified against that test at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation; mixed result, recorded honestly rather than rounded either way.** Positive evidence: D-092's log of this exact pattern recurring shows the mechanism catching itself in the act — the round-2 fix for D-129 (commit `6cffe94`) explicitly names its own find as "guard number six in this project's D-092 pattern," i.e. a later pass applying the named lesson caught what an earlier enumeration (round 1's O-13 tests) missed, and replaced the enumerated list with a generated Control registry plus completeness census (Layer 1's own prescription: "generate it from the source of truth") — the census now fails the build on an unregistered control rather than passing silently, which is a working instance of exactly what this row's Layer 1 asks for. Contrary evidence, not smoothed over: this row's own text cites "D-100's link checker" as a Layer-1 precedent, but this same reconciliation found (see D-100/D-132) that the link-checker mechanism was never built — one of the row's own supporting examples did not materialize. The falsifiability test's specific human-partner clause ("the human partner again finds a completeness defect the trigger should have caught") cannot be confirmed either way from the available commit messages and SDD reports alone — `6cffe94`'s message reads as a self-caught application of the lesson, not an externally-reported miss, but this reconciliation has no access to the raw session transcript to confirm that with certainty. Judged not-blocking: the mechanism visibly fired and worked at least once (D-129 round 2), which is what the row asked to observe. |
| D-104 | Org-visible data makes **concurrent editing** possible for the first time, and nothing detects a conflict | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Found by `what-if-oracle` aimed at the Plan 1b decomposition. This is the direct answer to AGENTS.md §3's *"what did this change make possible that was not possible before?"* — and Plan 1b never asked it. Under the pre-tenancy ownership model two people structurally could not open the same assessment: visibility was `createdById = userId`. The entire purpose of this plan is that colleagues in an organization share projects and assessments, so from the moment it ships, two assessors can open the same assessment, answer the same questions, and the second save silently overwrites the first. There is no version column, no `updatedAt` precondition, no optimistic-concurrency check anywhere in the schema. The artifact this destroys is not a draft — it is the evidence an institution will present as its AI-governance record, and a silent partial overwrite is worse than a visible conflict because nothing marks the loss. Not fixed in Plan 1b because the plan is already 13 steps and the collision needs a UX decision (last-write-wins with a warning, field-level merge, or explicit locking) rather than only a schema change | **Before the first real team uses the product concurrently — i.e. the first pilot with more than one active assessor in an organization.** Cheapest form is a `version Int` column plus an `updateMany({ where: { id, version } })` precondition, which is a one-column migration **now** and a data migration over live assessments later. Decide the UX with `what-if-oracle` at that point, since it is a user-facing trade-off rather than a purely technical one | Phase 1d | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation, with a correction to the row's own text.** The row states "there is no version column... anywhere in the schema" -- checked directly and this is now inaccurate (was already inaccurate when written): `prisma/schema.prisma`'s `Assessment` model has carried `version Int @default(1)` since the very first commit (`0f0e1fa`, pre-dating every phase of this rebuild) -- `git log --all -S'version      Int' -- prisma/schema.prisma` shows only that one commit. It is not invisible: `app/(authenticated)/orgs/[slug]/projects/[id]/page.tsx:287` renders it as a cosmetic label ("Assessment v{assessment.version}"). But it is never incremented anywhere and never used as an update precondition -- confirmed by grep, `lib/data/assessments.ts` has zero references to `version` outside unrelated FRAMEWORK-version doc comments. So the row's substantive risk (no optimistic-concurrency enforcement on concurrent assessment edits) is fully real and unchanged by Phase 1c, but the fix is now cheaper than the row's own text implies: no migration is needed, only wiring the already-existing column into `respondToAssessment`'s and `completeAssessment`'s update calls as a `WHERE version = $current` precondition. |
| D-105 | A governance tool aligned to ISO/IEC 42001 has **no tenant-scoped audit trail** of its own | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Found by `what-if-oracle`'s Ψ branch on the Plan 1b decomposition. `lib/security-logger.ts` records auth events (`AUTH_LOGIN_SUCCESS`, `AUTH_REGISTER`, lockouts) to the server log with a `userId` and no `orgId`, no tenant scoping, and no user-visible surface. After Plan 1b the interesting events are organizational — who invited whom, who changed a member's role, who completed an assessment, who removed a project — and none of them are recorded anywhere an institution can read. **The awkwardness is specific rather than general:** this product scores institutions on documentation and record-keeping (framework areas PO-03, PO-07) and anchors on ISO/IEC 42001, which requires documented records of the management system. Shipping an assurance tool that cannot answer *"who changed this assessment, and when?"* is a credibility problem before it is a compliance one — it is the tool failing its own framework. Deliberately out of Plan 1b's scope: it needs a data model (append-only, tenant-scoped, RLS-protected like every other tenant table) and a UI surface, which is its own design | **The earlier of: the first pilot with a real institution, or the first assessment intended to be shown to an external party (D-037's trigger).** Design it as a tenant table under RLS with `INSERT`-only privilege for the app role — an audit log the application can rewrite is not an audit log. Note it interacts with D-104: a version column and an audit trail answer overlapping questions and should be designed together | Phase 1d | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** No tenant-scoped audit-trail table or model was added -- `prisma/schema.prisma`'s only new models this phase are `FrameworkVersion`, `Evidence` and `EvidenceBlob`, none of which record organizational events (invites, role changes, completions, removals). `lib/security-logger.ts` gained new call sites this phase (evidence upload/download/delete/drift rejections) but its shape is unchanged: server log only, no `orgId` scoping, no user-visible surface. Out of Phase 1c's scope as this row's own text already anticipated. |
| D-106 | `MembershipStatus` carries values nothing ever sets — suspension is filtered for but never implemented | BUG | 2026-08-03 | Found by the decomposition pass. `preauth.membershipsForUser` filters `status: 'active'` and ADR-0002 decision 5 requires `requireOrgContext` to prove `status = 'active'`, so the *reading* side is complete and correct. Nothing on the *writing* side ever sets any other value: there is no suspend action in the RBAC matrix, no route, and no UI. So the enum documents a capability the product does not have, and the filter is dead defensive code that reads as though suspension works. The risk is not the missing feature — it is that a reviewer, or a later implementer, sees the filter and concludes the state machine is implemented. Two honest resolutions and no third: implement suspension (a `member:suspend` action, owner/admin only, with the same last-owner guard as O-14), or narrow the enum to the states that exist and keep the filter as an explicit invariant assertion | **Whenever a member needs to be suspended without being removed** — the realistic trigger is an institution asking to freeze a departing colleague's access pending handover. Until then the filter stays: it is correct, it is cheap, and it fails closed | Phase 1d | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** No `member:suspend` action exists in `lib/authz/policy.ts`'s GRANTS (Phase 1c's only additions there are the three `evidence:*` actions), no suspend route exists, and `preauth.membershipsForUser`'s `status: 'active'` filter is unchanged. Not touched, as expected -- nothing in Phase 1c's evidence/pinning scope had reason to. **AMENDED 2026-08-09 at the C6 whole-branch review, with a SECOND-ORDER finding this row did not previously carry.** The read/write-pairing sweep re-derived this row's facts independently and confirmed them (`membershipsForUser`, `lib/data/preauth.ts:84`, still filters `status: 'active'`; no writer of any other value exists), and found one thing this row's two "honest resolutions" do not account for: **`removeMember` itself filters `status: 'active'`** (`lib/data/members.ts:112`, `findFirst({ where: { userId: targetUserId, status: 'active' } })`, and again in its `deleteMany` at line 135). So on the day suspension ships, removing a SUSPENDED member returns `not_found` and silently no-ops -- the suspended state would become a state a member cannot be removed from, which is the opposite of what suspension is for and would be found only by whoever tried it. Whichever resolution is taken, `removeMember`'s own filter has to be revisited in the same change. Also noted, and not previously written down here: the ONLY removal that exists is a hard `deleteMany`, so leaving an organisation destroys the audit fact that the membership ever existed -- in a product whose subject is knowing who had access to what. That is a separate design question from suspension and should be decided alongside it rather than discovered after. |
| D-107 | Organization creation is unbounded and unrated-limited — one account can create any number of tenants | RISK | 2026-08-03 | Found by the decomposition pass. Registration creates `User` + `Organization` + `Membership(owner)` atomically (§3.2), and nothing caps how many organizations one authenticated user may create thereafter, nor how fast. `lib/rate-limit.ts` exists and is applied to auth routes; it is an **in-memory** store, which AGENTS.md §3 already cites as an interaction defect under multi-instance hosting, so it is not a complete answer even where it is applied. Consequences are mundane rather than dramatic and still real: unbounded rows in a table every tenant query joins against, slug-namespace exhaustion of desirable names, and a cheap way to generate load on the owner connection that D-099 already identifies as the constrained resource. Not a Plan 1b blocker — with zero real accounts and no public deployment, the exposure is nil today, and the fix depends on a product decision (is one person running five real institutional workspaces legitimate? for a university, plausibly yes) | **The first public or pilot deployment** (interacts with D-018 hosting). Decide the product rule first, then enforce it; a per-user cap plus a durable rate limiter, not the in-memory one | Phase 1d | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** `git log main..HEAD -- app/api/auth/register/route.ts` shows zero commits; no per-user organization cap or durable rate limiter was added. Phase 1c did substantially rework `lib/rate-limit.ts` (Task 6, fixing D-151's path-keying bypass with wildcard-segment matching), but that change is scoped to the evidence routes' own rate limits, not registration/org-creation, and the store is still in-memory. Still gated on D-018 (no deployment exists to make the exposure real). |
| D-108 | `code-review:code-review` is PR-shaped and this repo has no remote — recording the substitute arrangement rather than letting C4 quietly lapse | DECISION | 2026-08-03 | AGENTS.md C4 requires `code-review` on every received chunk, then `simplify`. That rule exists because D-063 and D-083 both recorded the same failure: an SDD reviewer subagent checking a diff against its brief was treated as discharging it, and it does not. **The instrument, however, does not fit.** `code-review:code-review` is built around `gh` — it fetches a pull request, checks whether the PR is a draft, and posts its findings as a PR comment. This repository has no git remote at all (D-067 records that `origin/HEAD` is an asserted symbolic ref, not a fetched one), so there is no PR to review or comment on. That is a fact about the tool, established by reading the skill definition, not an effort claim. **What runs instead, per task, and why it is not the D-083 substitution:** (1) the SDD task reviewer is dispatched with **two mandatory verdicts** — spec compliance *and* task quality — and its brief carries this project's security constraints verbatim as its attention lens, so the correctness/quality pass genuinely happens and is genuinely independent; (2) `simplify` runs on the chunk as C4 also requires; (3) the whole-branch `security-review` still runs at C6, and the final whole-branch code review still runs at the end of the plan. The difference from D-083 is that there the review *function* was skipped and the orchestration was pointed at as cover; here the function is performed and only the *tool* is unavailable. That distinction is exactly the kind that gets used to launder a skip (D-068), which is why this row exists rather than a silent decision | **Falsifiable, and this is the point of the row:** if the final whole-branch review, or the C6 `security-review`, finds a Critical or Important defect *in a diff that had already passed its task review* — i.e. a defect a dedicated `code-review` pass would plausibly have caught — then this arrangement is insufficient and must change rather than be re-justified. Also re-open immediately if a git remote is ever added, since the tool then fits **CONTROLLER CORRECTION, 2026-08-05.** The reconciliation above cites D-080, D-081, D-086, D-087, D-094, D-095 and D-096 as evidence that this row's test fired repeatedly. That is wrong, and the error matters because it would have re-opened a settled arrangement on false grounds. All seven were found at **Plan 1a's** C6 exit. This row was opened at **Plan 1b Task 1** (commit 0223801, after merge-base ab2c153), so the arrangement it describes did not exist when they were found and they cannot be evidence about it. §5's warning applies to a reviewer's inherited claim exactly as it applies to mine. **The only admissible evidence is Plan 1b's own C6 result: two Important findings, both in diffs that had passed task review.** By this row's first clause, that fired. By its qualifying clause it did not, because neither defect is contained in any single task's diff: proxy.ts is absent from Task 4's diff, and a function definition with no callers looks complete in isolation. code-review is diff-scoped too, so it would have caught neither. Choosing the reading that exonerates the arrangement would be the laundering D-068 records, so it is not being chosen; the test is instead reworded. **Re-open if C6 ever finds a defect wholly contained in one task's diff** — that would mean a task review genuinely missed what a dedicated pass would have seen. The class found here is invisible to diff review of ANY kind and visible only to whole-tree analysis, which is why C6 runs at branch granularity and why trigger-enumeration.test.ts now exists. **CLOSED 2026-08-05 — by the condition this row named, not by argument.** Its own text said: re-open immediately if a git remote is ever added, since the tool then fits. One was: origin now points at https://github.com/Alvin-Nahabwe/makai-rai-platform, `origin/HEAD` resolves, and `code-review:code-review` can therefore diff and comment against a real remote. The substitute arrangement this row recorded is no longer needed. Note what this does NOT retroactively change: the reworded falsifiable test above still stands, because the two defects Plan 1b's C6 found were invisible to diff-scoped review of ANY kind — a working `code-review` would not have caught them either. Availability of the tool and sufficiency of the tool are different claims. | Phase 1b+ | Closed-done |
| D-109 | `prisma migrate dev --create-only` cannot generate any new migration on this branch — the shadow database replay breaks on an existing migration | BUG | 2026-08-03 | Found while implementing Task 2. `npx prisma migrate dev --name … --create-only` failed with P3006/P3018: `relation "_prisma_migrations" does not exist`, while replaying `20260803062144_add_restricted_app_role` against Prisma's throwaway shadow database. Root cause, confirmed by reading that migration and by `DEBUG=prisma:* npx prisma migrate dev …`: the shadow database is a scratch database Prisma populates by executing each migration's raw SQL in order — it never creates the `_prisma_migrations` bookkeeping table there (that table only matters in a real target database, to record what has been applied). `20260803062144`'s SQL unconditionally runs `REVOKE ALL ON "_prisma_migrations" FROM makrai_app;`, which is fine against `makrai`/`makrai_test` (where Prisma created that table when the migration was actually applied) but fails against the shadow database, where it was never created. This means **every** future `migrate dev` invocation on this branch — not just Task 2's — will hit the same wall until the offending migration is fixed or the workflow changes. Worked around for Task 2 by generating the equivalent SQL a different way: `npx prisma migrate diff --from-config-datasource --to-schema prisma/schema.prisma --script`, which diffs the live `makrai` database directly against the schema file and therefore never touches the shadow database or replays migration history. The resulting SQL was verified to match what `migrate dev` would have produced (identical `DROP INDEX` / `ALTER TABLE` / `CREATE INDEX` shape to the prior migrations' style) and the migration folder was created by hand. Not fixed at the root because editing an already-applied, already-committed migration file is its own hazard and out of Task 2's scope (schema + migration + one test file) | **Before Task 3 (or any later task) needs to run `prisma migrate dev --create-only` again** — either wrap the `REVOKE ALL ON "_prisma_migrations"` line in `20260803062144` with `IF EXISTS`-style guards (a `DO $$ … WHEN relation exists $$` block, since bare `REVOKE` has no `IF EXISTS` form) so shadow-database replay survives it, or standardise on the `prisma migrate diff --from-config-datasource --to-schema` + hand-created migration folder as this project's normal migration-authoring path and document that in AGENTS.md/the plan's Global Constraints | Phase 1d | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** Half-discharged: the trigger fired repeatedly (every migration after this one — `20260803074244`, `20260803140500`, `20260803171630` — was authored by hand or via `migrate diff` rather than `migrate dev --create-only`, and the Plan 1a plan doc itself records the workaround as the practice: `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-02-phase1a-isolation-spine.md:35`, "For a SQL-only migration, create the directory and write `migration.sql` by hand"), so the second remedy this row named was adopted **in practice**. Neither remedy was applied to the actual defect, though: verified `prisma/migrations/20260803062144_add_restricted_app_role/migration.sql:31` still has the bare, unguarded `REVOKE ALL ON "_prisma_migrations" FROM makrai_app;`, and `grep -n "migrate" AGENTS.md` returns nothing — the practice was never written down as this project's standard, so a future contributor unaware of this history would still hit P3006/P3018 on first use of `migrate dev --create-only`. Re-targeted rather than closed for the undocumented, unfixed root cause. **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** The root cause is still unfixed -- `prisma/migrations/20260803062144_add_restricted_app_role/migration.sql:31` still has the bare, unguarded `REVOKE ALL ON "_prisma_migrations" FROM makrai_app;` -- and the practice still is not written down anywhere (`grep -n "migrate" AGENTS.md` returns nothing; this branch made zero commits to `AGENTS.md`). The workaround kept being needed and kept working: D-163 (below) independently reproduced the identical P3006/P3018 failure while verifying Task 8's migration, using the same `prisma migrate diff --from-config-datasource` substitute this row documents. Every migration this phase was authored via that substitute or by hand, never via `migrate dev --create-only` -- further confirming the practice without ever formalizing it. |
| D-110 | Task 2's schema rename (`invitations.token` → `tokenHash`) broke `lib/data/preauth.ts` and two already-passing security-pinning test suites the brief did not name — fixed in-task rather than left broken | DECISION | 2026-08-03 | The Task 2 brief scoped three files (`prisma/schema.prisma`, the new migration, `__tests__/integration/schema.test.ts`) and stated the produced interface as `invitations.tokenHash` "replacing `token`". It did not account for `lib/data/preauth.ts`'s `invitationByToken(token)`, which queried `where: { token: key, … }`, or for `__tests__/integration/data-boundary.test.ts` and `__tests__/integration/preauth-surface.test.ts`, both of which construct invitations with a literal `token:` field — one of them (`data-boundary.test.ts`) pins fail-open defects the C6 whole-branch security review found in this exact module. Verified before touching anything: `npm run typecheck` (baseline 3 errors, D-070) grew to 6 — two new `TS2353`s in the test files plus one in `preauth.ts` — and `npm test` regressed from 242/242 to 230/246 passing, all 16 new failures in those same two files. Leaving this broken would have violated the task's own explicit constraint #6 (`typecheck` must stay at **exactly** 3) and silently regressed two security-regression-pinning suites — the AGENTS.md §2 "about to defer/substitute" and "assumption in the brief turns out to be false" triggers both fired. Resolution taken, minimal and matching an already-decided design rather than inventing one: `invitationByToken` now hashes its input with the identical `createHash('sha256').update(x).digest('hex')` scheme Task 8 of the plan already specifies for `acceptInvitation`, and looks up by `tokenHash`; the two test fixtures now store `tokenHash: sha256(rawToken)` instead of the raw string, with every existing assertion, test name and description left unchanged. No route currently calls `invitationByToken` (`grep -rn invitationByToken app/` — zero results), so the blast radius of this fix is confined to the module itself and its own tests. Re-verified after: `npm test` 246/246 (242 baseline + 4 new schema tests), `npm run typecheck` back to exactly 3, `npm run lint` 0  **CLOSED, Task 8 (commit 71aea44).** Answer: the former — `invitationByToken` survives as a real caller's dependency, not dead code. `GET /api/v1/invitations/[token]` (the public invitation-preview route) and `app/(public)/invitations/[token]/page.tsx` both call it directly; its existing sha256 hashing needed no rework and `acceptInvitation` (lib/data/preauth.ts) hashes identically, as this row anticipated. Widened (not changed) to `include: { org: { select: { name: true } } }` so the preview page can show which org a link is for — sanctioned by `preauth-surface.test.ts`'s own comment that the pin constrains the surface, not the body.| **Task 8 ("Invitations"), when it implements `createInvitation`/`acceptInvitation`** — confirm whether `invitationByToken` survives as a real caller's dependency or becomes dead code superseded by `acceptInvitation`'s own hash-and-compare in a transaction; if the former, its hashing here is already consistent with Task 8's design and needs no rework, if the latter, delete it and update `preauth-surface.test.ts`'s exported-surface pin | Phase 1b | Closed-done |
| D-111 | Registration's email-uniqueness TOCTOU race returns a generic 500, not a 409 — accepted rather than fixed with an untested classifier | DECISION | 2026-08-03 | `app/api/auth/register/route.ts` checks `prisma.user.findUnique({ where: { email } })` before calling `bootstrapOrgWithOwner`, which is a separate query and therefore race-prone: two concurrent registrations for the same email can both pass the check, and the loser gets a Postgres UNIQUE-constraint violation from inside the transaction. An earlier draft of this task added a dedicated inner `try/catch` that inspected the thrown error's shape and returned 409 "email already exists" specifically for that race. `pr-review-toolkit:silent-failure-hunter` and, independently, `pr-review-toolkit:code-reviewer` both flagged it: its "unknown error shape" fallback defaulted to **affirmatively telling the caller their email exists**, on a shape that was never verified against the running stack — and when the adjacent `isUniqueViolation` helper in `lib/data/preauth.ts` was checked live (see the fix landed in this same commit), its analogous `meta.target` check was proven **inert** on this Prisma/adapter version. The same defect, in the same commit, on the same reasoning, would have shipped twice. Removed rather than hardened: the race window is a handful of milliseconds, the pre-existing code (before this task) had no special case for it either — it already fell through to the same generic 500 — and building a second, route-level P2002 classifier just to restore a nicer error message for a race this rare was judged not worth the risk of a second unverified fallback | **If registration-under-load ever becomes observable in practice** (a burst of 500s correlated with duplicate-email 400/409 attempts in the security event log) — at that point, verify the live P2002 shape the same way `isUniqueViolation` in `lib/data/preauth.ts` was verified (a direct probe against the running Prisma/adapter version, not the doc comment), write it as a *tested* branch, and reintroduce the 409 | Phase 2 | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** This is an accepted-risk decision with its own observability-based trigger ("a burst of 500s correlated with duplicate-email attempts in the security event log"), which has not fired — no load testing has occurred against this branch (same unproven-load-behaviour bucket as D-040/D-065/D-099, all Phase 2). Not a stale placeholder: the row already carries a complete, evidenced justification for not fixing it now, and nothing in Plan 1b changed that reasoning. |
| D-112 | Slug derivation loses information for non-Latin-alphabet institution names — the accented-Latin case is now fixed (fix round 1), the wholly-non-Latin-script case remains an open product decision | RISK | 2026-08-03 | **Original scope (2026-08-03, too narrow — corrected by the coordinator, fix round 1):** this row originally covered only "organization names written wholly in a non-Latin script derive to an empty slug base and fall back to the shared random-suffix namespace." That framing missed the more common case for this product's stated primary adopter (African university teams, including Francophone institutions across the DRC, Senegal, Côte d'Ivoire and Cameroon): **accented Latin-script names silently losing letters**, e.g. `deriveSlug('Université de Kinshasa')` produced `'universit-de-kinshasa'` (the é dropped) and `deriveSlug('École Polytechnique')` produced `'cole-polytechnique'` (the leading É dropped entirely) under the version of `deriveSlug` that shipped without any Unicode-normalisation step. **Fixed in fix round 1** by restoring diacritic folding — `.normalize('NFKD').replace(/\p{M}/gu, '')` before collapsing non-alphanumerics — so `'Université de Kinshasa'` now derives to `'universite-de-kinshasa'` and `'École Polytechnique'` to `'ecole-polytechnique'` (both verified empirically, `node -e`, not hand-simulated; see the fix-round-1 report). **What the fix does NOT cover, and what remains genuinely open:** NFKD has no canonical decomposition for letters that are not "base letter + combining mark" — confirmed empirically that "ø" is one such letter, and the same is true of most non-Latin scripts (Amharic, Arabic, Tigrinya, N'Ko, Cyrillic, CJK, …). An organization name written wholly in one of those still derives to an empty (or near-empty) slug base, and `createOrgInTx`'s `base = deriveSlug(orgName)`, falling back to `'org'` when empty, lands it in the shared `org-xxxx` random-suffix namespace — a working, non-colliding, but opaque and non-descriptive URL, indistinguishable from every other such name. For a platform whose locked standards include the African Union Continental AI Strategy, institutions whose names are not written in a script NFKD can fold to Latin are a real and not edge-case population, and giving them systematically worse URLs than Latin-script (accented or not) institutions is a product decision that deserves an explicit call rather than an implementation fallthrough | **Before this platform's URLs are shown to, or chosen by, an institution whose name is not in a script NFKD folds to Latin** — decide deliberately (transliteration library, allow Unicode slugs directly with punycode-safe routing, or accept the opaque-URL fallback as the permanent behavior) rather than leaving it as whatever `deriveSlug`'s Latin-alphabet-folding design happened to produce | Phase 2 | Open — **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** This is a genuine open product decision needing the human partner's call (transliteration library vs. Unicode slugs vs. accepting the opaque fallback), not something this reconciliation should decide unilaterally. Its own pick-up trigger — a real institution whose name is not in a Latin-foldable script — has not fired: no institution has been onboarded (hosting, D-018, is still Open, so there is no deployment to onboard one to). Aligned to D-029's target (Phase 2), the sibling slug-related product decision this row sits next to. |
| D-113 | Task 4 stripping `role`/`mustChangePassword` from the session, by design, breaks 10 call sites at compile time — the full reader list is wider than the brief's dispatch prompt named | BUG | 2026-08-04 | **Deliberate and load-bearing, not accidental** (ADR-0002 §3, task-4-brief.md Step 4: "MORE than 3 errors now… Task 7 drives it to 0"). Baseline `npm run typecheck` before Task 4 (confirmed via `git stash`): exactly 3 pre-existing errors, all `orgId` missing on `Project`/`Assessment`/`RemediationItem` creates (D-070), unrelated to this task. After Task 4: 13 total, 10 new. The 10: `app/(authenticated)/layout.tsx(1,29)`, `app/(authenticated)/admin/assessments/page.tsx(1,30)`, `app/(authenticated)/admin/settings/page.tsx(1,30)`, `app/(authenticated)/admin/users/page.tsx(1,30)`, `app/(authenticated)/dashboard/page.tsx(1,29)`, `app/(authenticated)/projects/page.tsx(2,29)`, `app/(authenticated)/projects/[id]/page.tsx(3,29)`, `app/(authenticated)/projects/[id]/compare/page.tsx(3,29)` — all `TS2307: Cannot find module '@/lib/auth-guard'`, since constraint 6 deletes that file with no replacement — plus `app/api/research/export/route.ts(8,38)` and `lib/authz.ts(22,52)`, both `TS2339: Property 'role' does not exist` on the now-`id`-only `Session.user`. **The dispatching prompt's "five known readers" list (layout.tsx, projects/[id]/page.tsx, compare/page.tsx, api/projects/route.ts, auth-guard.ts's requireAdmin) undercounted, found by grep as instructed:** it omitted `projects/page.tsx`, the three `requireAdmin`-gated admin pages, `research/export/route.ts`, and — the one worth flagging on its own — `lib/authz.ts`. `api/projects/route.ts` itself does NOT get a fresh error (its `user.role` reads `lib/authz.ts`'s own `SessionUser.role` field, typed independently of next-auth); the error surfaces one level up, at `lib/authz.ts:22`, where `getSessionUser()` assigns `session.user.role` into that type. `lib/authz.ts` is also the whole-module premise ADR-0001-era `eslint.config.mjs` already marks for deletion ("its ownership premise is wrong under tenancy") — it is not ported here because the brief named neither it nor an `authorizeAssessment`/`authorizeProject` replacement, and object-level authorization under org-scoped RLS is `requireOrgContext` + `can()`'s job, not a patch to a file already staged for removal **Cross-reference added by the controller 2026-08-04:** this row overlaps **D-072**, which already enumerates the same nine `lib/authz.ts` callers and already targets Phase 1b. The task reviewer flagged the duplication as a drift risk at the next register audit, and it is a real one — two rows describing one file's fate will diverge. Kept rather than merged, because the two record genuinely different facts and rows are closed, never deleted (§6): D-072 records that `lib/authz.ts` is an **authorization** problem (a second, ownership-premised authority that RLS cannot reach, and a global admin who reads any tenant), while this row records that it is now additionally a **compile** problem, which D-072 could not have predicted. Read them together; neither is sufficient alone. Whichever is closed first must cite the other. | **Task 7** ("drives it to 0" per task-4-brief.md) for the 8 `auth-guard` readers and `research/export/route.ts`. **`lib/authz.ts` needs an explicit call at or before Task 7**, not an assumed one: it still has 9 live callers — `grep -rln "from '@/lib/authz'" app` returns `app/api/assessments/route.ts`, `app/api/assessments/[id]/route.ts`, `app/api/assessments/[id]/complete/route.ts`, `app/api/assessments/[id]/remediation/route.ts`, `app/api/projects/route.ts`, `app/api/projects/[id]/route.ts`, `app/api/reports/[id]/pdf/route.ts`, `app/api/users/me/password/route.ts`, `app/api/admin/users/[id]/role/route.ts` — each importing `getSessionUser` and/or `authorizeAssessment`/`authorizeProject`/`isAdmin`. Either the task that ports these 9 deletes `lib/authz.ts` and replaces those four functions with `requireOrgContext`/`can()`, or an intermediate task explicitly re-scopes it to compile again first. Until then `npm run build` stays red, matching D-070/D-102's already-accepted status for this phase | Phase 1b (Task 7) | Closed-done |
| D-114 | `proxy.ts`'s two `catch { … }` blocks around `getToken()` fail open with no logging on ANY exception, not just "no cookie" | BUG | 2026-08-04 | Found by `pr-review-toolkit:silent-failure-hunter`, dispatched during Task 6 per the AGENTS.md §2 error-handling trigger, on code Task 6 did not write and does not own (`proxy.ts` line ~9-23 and ~31-40; Task 6 added only a comment above the function, no logic change). **Finding 1 (HIGH):** the forced-password-change gate's `try { … } catch { /* No/invalid token — nothing to enforce. */ }` catches every exception `getToken` can throw, not just an absent cookie (`getToken` returns `null` for that case; it does not throw). A throw more plausibly means a corrupted/tampered cookie failing JWE decryption or a `NEXTAUTH_SECRET` misconfiguration — the latter would fail open for every request platform-wide, silently defeating the exact "seeded admin using a shared default password" enforcement the comment names as the motivating case, with zero log line marking it happened. **Finding 2 (MEDIUM):** the rate-limit identity-extraction `catch` has the same shape; combined with the IP fallback three lines above, a systemic `getToken` failure silently collapses authenticated users onto shared IP-based rate-limit buckets (or one shared bucket, in a deployment lacking forwarded-for headers) with no signal. Not fixed in Task 6: `proxy.ts`'s rate-limiting/password-gate error handling is not in Task 6's Files list, predates this task, and fixing it would need its own scoped change (add edge-safe logging, decide fail-open-vs-closed for a *thrown* `getToken`) rather than a drive-by edit to a file whose only sanctioned Task 6 change was a documentation comment | **Whoever next touches `proxy.ts`'s auth/rate-limit logic** (candidate: Task 7, since it is the task restoring `requireIdentity()`-based auth site-wide) — add logging on the exception path in both catches, and decide deliberately whether a *thrown* `getToken` (as opposed to a `null` return) should fail closed for the password-change gate given what it protects | Phase 1c | Closed-done — **half-closed 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation** (Finding 1, see that note, unchanged below), **Finding 2 closed in fix round 1 of Task 6's review (2026-08-08).** Finding 1: `proxy.ts` was rewritten by commit `82e1f59` ("fix(security): enforce mustChangePassword from a fresh DB read") to remove the password-gate decision from this file entirely — the module doc now states plainly that the redirect decision is made in `requireIdentity()` from a fresh database read, not here. This resolves Finding 1 by obsoleting the vulnerable code path rather than by adding the logging this row's pick-up trigger suggested — a stronger fix. **Finding 2 correction, stated plainly per the coordinator's fix-round-1 review:** this row's ORIGINAL wording described the collapse as a CONTINGENCY — "a systemic `getToken` failure silently collapses authenticated users onto shared IP-based rate-limit buckets" — worded as something that COULD happen. Live verification during Task 6's own fix round proved it was not a contingency: it was the UNCONDITIONAL state of every request carrying a session cookie, from the moment `getToken` was first called in this file with no `secret`, because `getToken` (imported from `next-auth/jwt`, a plain re-export of `@auth/core/jwt`'s own implementation) reads no environment variable for the secret at all — confirmed by reading the runtime source directly and by executing `getToken` against this project's real `.env`, which threw `MissingSecret` on every call. **Now fixed**: `getToken` is called with `secret: requireAuthSecret()` (`lib/auth/secret.ts`, new in this fix round, also now the explicit `secret` `lib/auth.ts`'s own `NextAuth()` config passes, so the two cannot drift apart), and the catch around it is bound to `(e)` and logged via `logSecurityEvent('AUTH_TOKEN_DECODE_FAILURE', ...)` rather than silently swallowed — closing exactly the "add logging on the exception path" pick-up trigger this row named for Finding 2. Verified live: `__tests__/unit/auth-secret.test.ts` encodes a real session token with the shared secret and confirms `getToken` (called the identical way `proxy.ts` now does) decodes it and recovers the userId, and separately confirms the OLD call shape (no secret) still throws against the identical token — proving the fix is load-bearing, not incidental. See D-151's fix-round-1 update and D-124 for the downstream consequences this closes and does not close. |
| D-115 | Sidebar's admin-section role gate kept on PLATFORM role, not switched to ORG role via `can()` as the Task 6 brief's Step 4 literally says | DECISION | 2026-08-04 | The brief text: "The admin section gates on org role via `can()`, not the platform role it uses today." But `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-08-03-phase1b-wire-the-spine.md` line 61 (the plan's own "Deleted" list) removes ONLY `app/(authenticated)/admin/assessments/page.tsx`; `admin/users/page.tsx` and `admin/settings/page.tsx` are not deleted, not moved, and remain genuinely platform-wide (all-users list, platform-wide counts via `prisma.user.count()`/`prisma.assessment.count()`), not org-scoped. Task 8 (not Task 6) is what creates the first real org-scoped admin surface, `app/(authenticated)/orgs/[slug]/settings/members/page.tsx` (plan line 960) — there is no org-scoped replacement page yet for Task 6's Sidebar to point at. Switching these two links' gate to `can(orgRole, 'member:invite')` while their destination stays a platform-wide page would show every org owner/admin of ANY organization a "Users"/"Settings" link that then rejects them — a UX defect, not a fix — and there is currently no wiring path to a real `orgRole` value reaching `Sidebar` anyway, because `app/(authenticated)/layout.tsx` (which renders `Sidebar`) is one of the 8 files D-113 records as deliberately broken (`@/lib/auth-guard` deleted) and off-limits to Task 6 (fixing it would drop `npm run typecheck`'s error count below the required 13). Kept the existing `userRole === 'admin'` (platform role) check; removed only `/admin/assessments` (its page is deleted, unrelated to this disagreement) and added an org-switcher link. This is a probable brief/plan inconsistency, not a considered design call on my part — flagging for the controller rather than silently picking a side | **Task 7 or Task 8** — whichever restores `app/(authenticated)/layout.tsx`'s identity resolution (Task 7) and/or builds the real org-scoped members/settings page (Task 8) should revisit this: either confirm platform-role gating for `admin/users`/`admin/settings` is correct as-is (and the brief's Step 4 text was simply imprecise), or wire a real `orgRole` into `Sidebar` once `layout.tsx` can supply one, once there is an org-scoped destination worth gating that way | Phase 1b (Task 7/8) | Closed-done — **2026-08-05, Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation.** Both named revisit-conditions occurred (Task 7 restored `layout.tsx`'s identity resolution; Task 8, commit `71aea44`, built the real org-scoped `orgs/[slug]/settings/members` page and itself touched `Sidebar.tsx`), and the question this row flagged was explicitly answered rather than left to default: `components/layout/Sidebar.tsx:12-22` documents, in writing, that platform-role gating for `admin/users`/`admin/settings` is correct as-is — option (a) of this row's own pick-up trigger — because those two pages remain genuinely platform-wide (all users, platform-wide counts) with no org-scoped replacement, and gating a platform-wide page by an arbitrary org's role would 403 legitimate org owners/admins. Confirmed live: `find "app/(authenticated)/orgs/[slug]/settings"` shows the org-scoped members page exists at its own route, separate from the platform `/admin/*` pages this row is about. |
| D-116 | Platform-wide tenant aggregates removed from `admin/settings` and `admin/users` rather than ported | DEFERRAL | 2026-08-04 | **Rewritten, fix round 1** — the original text claimed no sanctioned cross-org read path exists at all; that was a self-generated obstacle claim, not a checked one (AGENTS.md §5 ranks this the most dangerous class of claim precisely because it arrives feeling like an observation). A path DOES exist: `lib/data/preauth.ts`'s `ownerClient` is a BYPASSRLS connection whose SANCTIONED use is named, documented, before-context reads that are inherently cross-org (`bootstrapOrgWithOwner`, `membershipsForUser`, `orgBySlug`, `invitationByToken` — see that module's own doc). A `platformAssessmentStats()` function living there would be the identical shape — same connection, same 'inherently cross-org, no RLS policy can serve it' justification — and would be covered by the same export-pinning test (`__tests__/integration/preauth-surface.test.ts`) that already keeps that module's surface enumerable rather than silently growing. **Why it was not built in this fix round anyway:** `preauth.ts`'s doc and its pinning test currently scope it to BEFORE-CONTEXT reads specifically — resolving identity/membership/invitations prior to any org context existing. A platform-admin stats query is a different kind of cross-org read: an AFTER-context, admin-only aggregate capability, not a pre-auth bootstrap need. Widening `preauth.ts`'s documented boundary to cover it — or deciding it deserves its own, separately-scoped owner-connection module — is exactly the 'hard-to-reverse fork' AGENTS.md §2 requires `what-if-oracle` for before building, not a call to make inside a fix-round comment answering four unrelated findings. This is a deliberate scope deferral, not an impossibility claim | A concrete platform-analytics need is prioritized — at that point, run `what-if-oracle` + `senior-security` on whether `platformAssessmentStats()` belongs in `lib/data/preauth.ts` (extending its existing bypass class and pinning test) or as a new, separately-documented owner-connection module scoped to platform-admin aggregate reads only, then implement it and cover it by the same export-pinning discipline `preauth.ts` already uses | Phase 1b+ | Open |

| D-119 | `DELETE /api/users/me`'s last-owner check cannot be atomic ACROSS the orgs a caller owns | DEFERRAL | 2026-08-04 | **Rewritten, fix round 1** — the original text said cross-org atomicity 'cannot be' available under this architecture; that was a self-generated obstacle claim, not a checked one. It IS buildable: `withOrg`'s own transaction-handle doc (`lib/data/tenant.ts:52-63`) states plainly that Prisma 7's `ITXClientDenyList` excludes `$executeRaw`/`$queryRaw`, so a callback CAN re-issue `SELECT set_config('app.current_org_id', <org B>, true)` mid-transaction to re-scope itself to a SECOND org within the SAME Postgres transaction. A true all-or-nothing multi-org membership deletion is therefore technically possible today — just not through `withOrg`'s current single-org-per-call signature, which sets the GUC once and assumes one org for the whole transaction. **Why it was not built in this fix round anyway:** that same comment names re-scoping mid-transaction a 'first-party footgun' — RLS cannot stop a callback from doing it, since setting the GUC is unprivileged — and explicitly defers narrowing that capability to D-088. Building a `withMultiOrg`-shaped primitive on top of a capability already flagged as a footgun, inside a fix round scoped to four specific review findings, would be exactly the kind of expedient, unreviewed use D-088 exists to prevent. This is a deliberate scope deferral, not an impossibility claim | D-088 is picked up (the `$executeRaw`/`$queryRaw` narrowing), OR a user who owns 2+ organizations reports (or is observed) hitting this exact residual window — at that point, design a `withMultiOrg(ctxs, cb)` primitive that re-issues `set_config` per org inside one transaction, explicitly reviewed via `senior-security` rather than improvised, and this row closes when it ships and is verified live | Phase 1b+ | Open |

Not fixed in Task 8: the pattern is inherited, appears in a file Task 8 did not write (`members/route.ts`), and a real fix (log or `logSecurityEvent` on a lookup miss) belongs with whoever owns `lookupUserNames` itself so it fixes both call sites at once rather than diverging between them | Whoever next touches `lib/data/identity.ts#lookupUserNames` or either of its two callers — add a `console.warn`/`logSecurityEvent` when `ids.length` after the query is fewer than the requested set, naming the missing id(s), so a silent data-integrity anomaly becomes discoverable instead of indistinguishable from a user who is legitimately named "Unknown" | Phase 1b+ | Open |

| D-124 | `proxy.ts`'s login rate limit (`POST:/api/auth`, keyed by `x-forwarded-for`/`x-real-ip`/`127.0.0.1`, max 15 per 15 minutes) caps at 15 successful-or-not login attempts from one address in the window — root-caused live during Task 10 (8 successive `POST /api/auth/callback/credentials` from one IP → 302, the 9th → 429; a distinct `x-forwarded-for` per request reset the count) after the 16th of 20 sequential fixture logins hung client-side on `TypeError: Failed to construct 'URL': Invalid URL`, the browser-side symptom of `signIn()` receiving a 429 it does not handle. **Widened by the `simplify` altitude pass (2026-08-04):** the same fact that lets the test spoof 20 addresses — `proxy.ts:41-44` trusts a client-supplied `x-forwarded-for` with no upstream-proxy allowlist — means a real attacker can equally set that header to defeat the login rate limiter entirely, not just avoid a false-positive lockout for legitimate shared-IP users. That is a materially worse severity than "some real users get 429'd," and this row now carries both | RISK | 2026-08-04 | Task 10's `e2e/fixtures/auth.setup.ts` works around the false-positive-lockout half by giving each of the 20 fixture users' Playwright contexts a distinct synthetic `x-forwarded-for` — legitimate there (20 real users would not collapse onto one address the way one local Playwright process does), and does not touch `proxy.ts`/`lib/rate-limit.ts` at all. A mechanism fix (e.g. an env-gated rate-limit bump, or trusting `x-forwarded-for` only from a configured upstream-proxy allowlist) was considered and deliberately NOT taken in Task 10: both are changes to a live security control, `proxy.ts`/`lib/rate-limit.ts` are not among Task 10's named files (`__tests__/helpers/fixture.ts`, `e2e/fixtures/auth.setup.ts`), and AGENTS.md §7.1 requires a preventive `senior-security` pass on any such change before it ships — a review this task's brief does not authorize. Deciding the right cap, the right trust boundary for `x-forwarded-for` (or a smarter rate-limit key, e.g. IP+email), and whether `resetRateLimitStore()` (lib/rate-limit.ts, currently dead code with zero callers) should be wired into a test-mode reset path are all product/security trade-offs for that future task | Before or during Phase 1b's classroom-deployment hardening, or the first time a real multi-user pilot reports lockouts on login — or immediately, if `senior-security`/`security-review` treats the header-spoofing bypass as a standalone finding rather than a Phase 1b+ item | Phase 1b+ | Open — **trigger assessed, 2026-08-08, Task 6 fix round 1: the coordinator's review of Task 6 treated the header-spoofing bypass as a standalone finding while cross-referencing this row, which is this row's own named "immediately" trigger firing.** Assessed and NOT acted on with a full fix in this round, for a reason stated rather than left implicit: the two mechanism fixes this row itself names — an upstream-proxy allowlist for `x-forwarded-for`, or a smarter key such as IP+email — both require knowing the deployment topology (is there a real reverse proxy in front of this app, and can it be trusted to set `x-forwarded-for` correctly?), which does not exist yet: hosting is D-018, PARKED, Open. Choosing a trust boundary for a header with no topology to trust it against would be guessing, not deciding. **What this round's Important-2 fix (see D-114, D-151) DOES change:** every `keyBy: 'userId'` rule (this task's own evidence-upload rule, `DELETE:/api/users/me`, `default`) no longer falls back to `x-forwarded-for` for an authenticated caller — `userId` now actually resolves, so the spoofing surface this row describes is now CONFINED to genuinely pre-auth traffic (`keyBy: 'ip'` rules: `POST:/api/auth/register`, `POST:/api/auth`), where it remains fully live and exactly as this row describes, because a pre-auth request has no userId to key on regardless. This narrows the row's scope; it does not close it. Pick-up trigger unchanged |
| D-125 | Consent records store an **attacker-supplied** `x-forwarded-for` as their `ipAddress` — audit evidence a client chooses | RISK | 2026-08-04 | Distinct from **D-124**, which covers the *rate-limit bypass* arising from the same header, and deliberately not folded into it: that one is an availability/abuse control, this one is **audit integrity**, they have different fixes and different triggers, and a reader who closes D-124 would otherwise reasonably believe this closed with it. Found by the controller while verifying Task 10's D-124, and independently confirmed Important by the task reviewer, which established that no existing row covers it. Mechanism: `app/api/auth/register/route.ts:64` and `app/api/v1/invitations/[token]/register/route.ts:64` both do `request.headers.get('x-forwarded-for')` falling back to the literal string `unknown` — note **without** even the `.split(',')[0]` that `proxy.ts:42` applies — and pass the raw string into `bootstrapOrgWithOwner` / `createUserFromInvitation`, which persist it verbatim as `ConsentRecord.ipAddress` (`lib/data/preauth.ts:186-196`). So a registering client sends `X-Forwarded-For: <anything>` and that string becomes the recorded provenance of their consent. **Why this matters more here than it would elsewhere:** this product assesses institutions against ISO/IEC 42001 and the AU Continental AI Strategy, and scores them on documentation and record-keeping (framework areas PO-03, PO-07). A consent trail whose provenance field is client-asserted is exactly the deficiency the tool exists to diagnose, in the tool itself — the same self-application argument as D-105's missing audit trail. Not fixed in place because the *correct* value depends on a decision this project has explicitly parked: behind a trusted reverse proxy `x-forwarded-for` is the right source, and on a direct connection it is worthless, so writing either one now would be guessing at a topology (D-018) | **When hosting is decided (D-018), or at the first non-local deployment, whichever is first** — and note both are also D-124's trigger, so treat them as one piece of work. Fix shape: an explicit trusted-proxy allowlist, with the header honoured only when the request arrives from one; otherwise record the connection address, or record nothing rather than recording a value the client chose. **Do not simply keep the header and add a comment** — an audit field that is documented as unreliable is still presented as evidence to whoever reads the record | Phase 1d, gated on D-018 | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** `git log main..HEAD` for `app/api/auth/register/route.ts`, `app/api/v1/invitations/[token]/register/route.ts` and `lib/data/preauth.ts` shows zero commits -- the `x-forwarded-for`-into-`ConsentRecord.ipAddress` path is unchanged. Still correctly gated on D-018 (no reverse-proxy topology to design a trusted-proxy allowlist against). |
