| D-130 | The debounced assessment autosave races the explicit stage-completion write, and a lost race fails SILENTLY (no `res.ok` check on either) | RISK | 2026-08-04 | Found live via `superpowers:systematic-debugging` while verifying D-127/128/129's fix: `e2e/assessment-flow.spec.ts`'s full end-to-end walk (create → answer → complete → report → PDF) failed reproducibly at the SAME step across three consecutive single-worker reruns, with two different-looking symptoms (once appearing stuck on module 1's own validation banner, once landing back on the assessment page with a "not completed" bounce from `/report`) — both traced to one mechanism. `AssessmentPageClient.tsx`'s `autoSave` debounces a `PATCH .../assessments/[id]` by 1000ms after every response change, cancelling any prior pending timer; `handleGenerateReport` (fired by "View Report Now"/"View Full Report") separately does its OWN explicit `PATCH` (with the stage marked `completed` in `engineState`) immediately followed by `POST .../complete`, sequentially awaited, then `router.push` to the report. If the LAST answered question's debounced timer is still pending when completion is triggered (near-certain for a fast, automated answer-then-immediately-complete sequence, and plausible for a real user who answers a final question and clicks "Complete" within a second), its STALE (pre-completion) `PATCH` can commit to the database AFTER the explicit "completed" `PATCH` — `respondToAssessment` (`lib/data/assessments.ts`) has no version/timestamp precondition, so whichever write lands last in Postgres wins regardless of which was issued first client-side. If the stale write wins, `POST .../complete` (which reads `engineState` fresh inside its own transaction) finds `pre-processing.status` NOT `completed` and returns `no_stages` (400) — which `handleGenerateReport` never checks (`fetch` does not throw on a non-2xx response, and the code has no `res.ok` guard on either call), so the client navigates to `/report` regardless, which correctly finds `assessment.status !== 'completed'` and bounces back, with NO error ever surfaced to the user. This is a variant of D-104's already-recorded "no optimistic concurrency" gap — D-104 frames it as two DIFFERENT users editing the same assessment; this is the SAME missing precondition triggered by one user's own sequential-but-async actions, and is arguably more likely to occur in practice (every "answer the last question, then immediately complete" click sequence is a live instance of this race, not a rare multi-user collision). Not fixed here: out of this task's authorized scope (a fix round for D-127/128/129's role-gating specifically), and the correct fix is the same one D-104 already names (a version/`updatedAt` precondition on the write) — implementing it here would be solving D-104 as a side effect of an unrelated task, which is exactly the kind of undecided-scope expansion AGENTS.md §2 asks to be reported rather than absorbed. Worked around in `e2e/assessment-flow.spec.ts` with a 1100ms wait before triggering completion (documented inline, cites this row) — a real user's own natural pause already does this most of the time, which is also why this race is intermittent rather than constant in practice | Same trigger as D-104 (the first pilot with concurrent/rapid real usage, or before any concurrency work ships) — fix both together: a `version`/`updatedAt` precondition on `respondToAssessment`'s update (and `completeAssessment`'s, which has the identical shape), OR at minimum have `handleGenerateReport` check `res.ok` on both calls and surface a retry to the user instead of silently navigating to a report that isn't there yet | Phase 1d | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** Same correction as D-104 (its named fix target): the row states there is "no version column"; there is one (`Assessment.version`, present since the initial schema, never incremented -- see D-104's corrected text), so the fix this row and D-104 share is wiring an existing column rather than a schema migration. Substantively unaddressed: `lib/data/assessments.ts`'s `respondToAssessment` still has no update precondition and the client callers this row names still do not check `res.ok`. Worth noting Phase 1c did fix the analogous race on a different write path -- Task 7 closed a TOCTOU race between evidence deletion and assessment completion with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` -- encouraging precedent for the same technique here, but that does not itself close this row, which is about the answer-autosave path specifically. |

A feature this reachable, with a real UI entry point and its own engine module, is not "flow unreachable" by any reading. **What was done here, and why it forecloses nothing:** gated every mutating control in `QuickAssessment.tsx` (inputs, submit) behind `can(ctx.role, 'assessment:respond')`, the identical pattern D-127/128/129 already established elsewhere — strictly safer regardless of which way this resolves. | D-133 | Register drift is bound to TASK completion, not CONTROLLER completion — every row closed by a task's own commit got marked; rows closed by the controller's own work (deriving an ADR, fixing a doc index, running this reconciliation itself) did not, until an explicit phase-exit audit caught them | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-05 | Diagnosed while performing the Plan 1b phase-exit register reconciliation (AGENTS.md C6) that this row's own commit is part of. Each SDD task's brief ends with a register update as one of its deliverables, so task-driven closures fire reliably — every `Closed-done` row citing a task number in this file was closed on schedule. The controller's own work carries no equivalent obligation: nothing in AGENTS.md binds "the controller derived an ADR" or "the controller fixed a doc index" to a register check, so those closures either never happened (D-066: ADR-0002 was derived in commit `4fe15c3`, twelve commits and two days before this reconciliation closed the row) or happened but were never written back (D-100: the ADR-index bug was fixed in the same commit that created the real ADR, yet the row stayed Open until now; D-069: Task 6/7 wrote the `lastActiveOrgId` write path and a review-time test, discharging the row's residual, with nothing closing it). Three confirmed instances (D-066, D-069, D-100) plus this reconciliation's own broader count — of the 47 Open rows targeted at Phase 1b (in its various spellings) this reconciliation examined, 14 were substantively resolved by the time of exit and needed only the register to catch up, not new work (D-022, D-052, D-061, D-066, D-069, D-073, D-081, D-084, D-086, D-087, D-094, D-095, D-100, D-115) — establish this as a pattern, not a one-off. **A mechanical check is possible and is narrower, and therefore more likely to actually fire, than a broad "controller must remember" rule:** a test asserting that no register row whose Target names a phase remains `Open` once that phase's branch is merged to `main` would have caught every instance here, because it does not depend on anyone remembering to update the row — it depends only on the merge event, which is observable and already gates on `npm run verify` passing. | D-134 | ESLint's `no-restricted-imports` `lib/db` (D-074) and `auth` (ADR-0002) bans were silently unenforced from Task 5 to 2026-08-05, defeated by a same-rule config collision D-074's own closure did not detect | BUG | 2026-08-05 | Found at the final Plan 1b whole-branch review (CRITICAL-1). `eslint.config.mjs` carried two `no-restricted-imports` config objects on overlapping `files` globs (`app/**/*.{ts,tsx}`, `lib/**/*.{ts,tsx}`): one from Task 4/D-074 banning `@/lib/db` and `auth`, a second from Task 5/D-089 banning `createOrgContext`. ESLint flat config replaces a rule's options per matching object rather than merging them, so the second silently deleted the first's patterns for every file both applied to — and `npm run lint` stayed 0 problems throughout, because the deletion is a valid config, not a lint error. Verified two ways before fixing: `npx eslint --print-config "app/(public)/login/page.tsx"` returned `no-restricted-imports` containing ONLY the `createOrgContext` pattern; a probe file importing `auth` from `@/lib/auth` linted with exit 0. One live violator existed: `app/(public)/invitations/[token]/page.tsx:2` imported `auth` directly (benign in that instance — it fed `resolveIdentity` — but nothing structurally prevented the next caller from treating `session.user.id` as authenticated and skipping the isActive/sessionEpoch/absolute-age checks ADR-0002 exists to force). Fixed: `eslint.config.mjs` rewritten as ONE base config object carrying all three ban groups, with per-file overrides (`lib/data/**`, `lib/data/tenant.ts`, `lib/auth/identity.ts`, `lib/auth/context.ts`) that each restate the subset still needed rather than using an object-level `ignores`; the invitation page now uses a new `tryResolveIdentity()` export (lib/auth/identity.ts) instead of importing `auth`. `__tests__/lint/effective-config.test.ts` pins the RESOLVED config (via `eslint --print-config`), not this file's source text — reading the source would not have caught the original bug, since each individual block, read alone, was and is correct. Verified live: all five of that suite's general-case assertions fail when `eslint.config.mjs` is reverted to the pre-fix two-block form (confirmed by temporarily restoring it and re-running), and pass against the fix; `npm run lint` stays 0 problems; probe files confirm all three bans (`lib/db`, `auth`, `createOrgContext`) fire on an ordinary `app/**` file and correctly do not fire on each file's sanctioned exemption | Closed by this same commit — nothing further to pick up | Phase 1b | Closed-done |
| D-135 | No mechanism stops a FUTURE `no-restricted-imports` (or any other rule) collision of the same shape D-134 just fixed — the fix closes this instance, not the failure class | DEFERRAL | 2026-08-05 | Raised by the `simplify` pass on the D-134 fix (altitude review). `eslint.config.mjs` now derives each override's pattern subset from `ALL_IMPORT_BAN_PATTERNS` via `.filter()` rather than hand-listing it, and `__tests__/lint/effective-config.test.ts` pins the resolved config for all five files (general case + four overrides) that matter TODAY — but nothing mechanical stops a future contributor from adding a sixth config object on an overlapping `files` glob that sets `no-restricted-imports` (or any other rule) again, reintroducing the exact collision shape D-134 just fixed. Two candidate mechanisms were named and neither was built here, as out of this fix wave's scope (four named findings, not an open-ended hardening pass): (a) a small script, run alongside `npm run lint`, that parses `eslint.config.mjs`'s resolved config objects and fails if two objects set the same rule key on overlapping `files` globs; (b) restructuring the ban definitions into a single source-of-truth exemption map (file -> excluded ban groups) that GENERATES the config objects, making a hand-written colliding block structurally impossible rather than merely discouraged by comments. Comments alone are not the fix — this file's own history is the counter-example | Whoever next adds a `no-restricted-imports` (or any rule) config object to `eslint.config.mjs`, or at the start of Phase 1c | Phase 1d | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** `git log main..HEAD -- eslint.config.mjs` shows zero commits; neither candidate mechanism was built. | D-136 | The planning stage has no execution step, and every defect the controller introduced in Plan 1b was an unexecuted claim about behaviour | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-05 | Raised by the human partner at the Plan 1b close: *"If this is a clear and consistent pattern, do we just keep letting it happen and hope a sub agent will pick up on it?"* — a fair challenge, since every serious Plan 1b defect was in the plan rather than the implementations, and each was caught by a subagent that happened to check. **The diagnosis is specific rather than general.** Four of the five were claims about how a dependency behaves that were never executed: ADR-0002 asserted the absolute session cap could be enforced against the JWT `iat`, and @auth/core resets `iat` on every re-encode; the plan's slug-collision retry could not work, because Postgres aborts a transaction on any statement error and a retry needs SAVEPOINT; the ESLint config was assumed to merge two `no-restricted-imports` blocks, and flat config REPLACES rule options, so the import bans did not exist at all; and a non-vacuity instruction told an implementer to swap two actions that turned out to have identical grant sets, so nothing would have gone red. Each was seconds to verify — one `node -e`, one psql query, one `--print-config`, one grep — and none was verified. **The structural reason:** implementers hit reality constantly because code either runs or does not; planners never do unless something forces it. So plan-stage defects are systematically of the unexecutable-claim kind, and are systematically invisible to every review instrument the controller owns — STRIDE, `what-if-oracle` and `adversarial-reviewer` are all reading instruments pointed at a reading-shaped artifact. The fifth defect (`lastActiveOrgId` read by one task and written by none) is a variant: not a false claim, but an unpaired one | **PROPOSED, NOT YET INSTALLED — awaiting the human partner's ruling, which is why this is a row and not an AGENTS.md amendment.** Two mechanical checks bound to the observable moment *"I am about to hand over a plan or a task brief"*: **(1) Executable-claim probe** — any claim in a plan about how a library, database or tool BEHAVES is executed before the plan ships, and the plan cites the command and its output; if it cannot be executed it is marked in the brief as an assumption for the implementer to check rather than inherit. This would have caught all four above. **(2) Read/write pairing** — every value a plan says is read must have a named writer and vice versa, verified by grep. This catches `lastActiveOrgId`, `bumpSessionEpoch` and `mustChangePassword` — three of the phase's worst defects, all the same shape. Honest limit, stated so the fix is not oversold: neither check touches JUDGMENT errors. The representative-versus-exhaustive live-matrix call was wrong and was not an unexecuted claim; the only instrument with a record against that class is an independent reviewer who disagrees, and in that instance it was the human partner. If installed, the falsifiable test is: if a later phase's C6 finds a controller-introduced defect that IS an unexecuted behavioural claim, the checks are not firing and must be reconsidered rather than reworded | Phase 1d (pending human partner decision) | **Closed-done, 2026-08-10.** Human partner ruled to install. Both checks are now an AGENTS.md §2 trigger row bound to the observable moment *you are about to hand over a plan or a task brief* -- the executable-claim probe and read/write pairing together, since they fire at the same moment. The falsifiable test this row proposed stands unchanged: if a later phase's C6 finds a controller-introduced defect that IS an unexecuted behavioural claim, the check is not firing and must be reconsidered rather than reworded. |
| D-137 | Roadmap resequenced: domain specificity becomes Plan 2a, and four harvest-audit rows depend on its outcome | DECISION | 2026-08-05 | Human partner's ruling after reviewing AIHET-A (AI in Healthcare Ethics Toolkit for Africa, Chukwu 2026, CC BY 4.0, DOI 10.5281/zenodo.21696642): Plan 1c stands as scoped — evidence attachment (D-001) plus framework-as-versioned-data (D-003) — and closes Phase 1; domain specificity becomes **Plan 2a**. **AIHET-A is a COMPARATOR to learn structure from, explicitly NOT a framework to incorporate** — adopting it wholesale would mean shipping another author's framework under this product's name, when the product's own framework is the thing being assessed. What is worth taking is structural rather than content: its four lifecycle phases with GATES (a phase can return *do not deploy*), its items tiered Mandatory / As-Applicable / Recommended so resource-constrained sites can defer the *should* without touching the *must*, and the fact that its output is a decision where ours is a percentage. **The dependency that matters runs the other way from how the question was posed, and it argues FOR keeping 1c first:** D-003 is already in Plan 1c and IS the machinery domain specificity needs — if question banks, areas and controls become versioned records an assessment pins to, a domain is plausibly another axis of the same model rather than a parallel system | **Binding constraint on Plan 1c, and the reason this row exists rather than a note:** design `FrameworkVersion` KNOWING Plan 2a exists — not building for it, but not foreclosing it. If 1c models a single linear version chain, which is the obvious shape when domains are not in view, then 2a must bolt a second axis onto a shipped schema. That is the D-103 reach class at the schema layer, where it is most expensive to unwind. **Harvest-audit rows split by whether the domain decision changes their scope:** D-049 and D-050 (UI/UX, design system) are independent — do them whenever convenient. D-032 (content currency), D-033 (standards crosswalk), D-034 (engine mechanics: staging, gating, cross-stage propagation) and D-035 (report and PDF) all have scope that changes if content and workflow become domain-partitioned, and D-034 is the sharpest — the engine's staging and gating is precisely what would have to become domain-aware. Re-target those four to after Plan 2a's design settles, individually rather than as a sweep. **Plan 2a opens with `what-if-oracle`**, mandatory under AGENTS.md §2: it is a scoring and data-model fork, hard to reverse once real assessments exist | Phase 2a | Open |
| D-138 | D-003 splits: Plan 1c closes the **pinning** half, and framework content as database rows moves to Plan 2a | DECISION | 2026-08-06 | Human partner's ruling during the Plan 1c design, taken over the two alternatives (content rows now, or files-plus-registry permanently). Recorded as its own row rather than an edit to D-003 because it changes D-003's *scope*, and a scope change buried in an existing row's status column is exactly the silent re-targeting §6 forbids. The reasoning that decided it: D-137 binds Plan 1c not to foreclose a domain axis, and the cheapest way to honour that is not to guess the content model at all — Plan 2a designs content rows and the domain axis together, once, with both requirements in view. What Plan 1c ships instead is the part that is a **defect** rather than a feature: no assessment in this database records which content it was answered against, and the field that appears to (`engineState.version`) is the engine's version, verified as `const VERSION = '4.0.0'` at `lib/engine/AssessmentEngine.js:23` while the content files carry `3.0.0` | Plan 2a design opens | Phase 2a | Open |
| D-139 | OOXML uploads are validated only as far as the ZIP container, not as documents | RISK | 2026-08-06 | `.docx`, `.xlsx` and `.pptx` are ZIP archives whose magic bytes are `PK\x03\x04`, identical to any ZIP, JAR or APK. Plan 1c's `inspect()` therefore establishes *this is a zip*, not *this is a Word document*, for the formats most likely to be used as institutional evidence. Recorded rather than fixed because the residual risk is bounded by the download path: O-8 requires `application/octet-stream` plus `Content-Disposition: attachment` plus `nosniff`, so a mislabelled archive is never rendered or executed. The fix, when triggered, is to read `[Content_Types].xml` from the archive's central directory. Stated in spec §3.2 as a known limit so an implementer cannot discover it as a surprise | A format-confusion finding, or the first hosted deployment | Phase 2 | Open |
| D-140 | No malware scanning on uploaded evidence | RISK | 2026-08-06 | Plan 1c adds the first bytes a user can put into this database, and ships with no AV scanning. Recorded explicitly because silence on this in a design that introduces file upload reads as an oversight to any reviewer and to an auditor, whereas the accepted-risk case is defensible and belongs on the record: there is no hosting and no real account (D-018), uploads are confined to authenticated members of an organisation they already belong to, and the download path never serves anything inline or executable (O-8). What is NOT claimed: that scanning stays unnecessary once real institutions upload real documents | First deployment reachable by users outside the developing team | Phase 2 | Open |
| D-141 | An uploaded evidence document may itself contain third-party personal data, and user erasure does not reach it | RISK | 2026-08-06 | The human partner's ruling on erasure — keep the bytes as an organisational record, scrub `uploadedById` — is correct for the artifact-as-org-record case and does not address the case where the artifact's *contents* are personal data about someone who is not the uploader. That is a genuine conflict between the right to erasure and the integrity of a governance record, and it cannot be resolved by a technical default. Deferred deliberately rather than guessed: with zero real accounts there is no data subject, no controller and no DPO to answer to, and inventing a takedown policy now would harden an answer nobody has been asked for | First pilot holding real institutional data | Phase 2 | Open |
| D-142 | A `pg_dump` of this database now contains arbitrary user-uploaded documents | RISK | 2026-08-06 | Storing evidence as `bytea` changes the sensitivity class of every backup: what was previously schema plus text is now schema plus text plus whatever institutions upload. Anyone holding a dump holds the evidence. Recorded at design time rather than at first incident because the mitigations all belong to a decision that has not been made — backup encryption, retention and access control are properties of the hosting model | Hosting decision (D-018) | Phase 2 | Open |

| D-145 | A pre-existing, client-wide silent failure: a bare `catch` discards specific server error detail and replaces it with a fixed generic message, at 8 fetch call sites across 6 client component files -- `resolvePublishedVersionId`'s throw (Task 1) is one more path into it, not its origin | BUG | 2026-08-08 | Verified live, full chain: `lib/http/toResponse.ts:41` rethrows any error class it does not recognise (by design), so an unmapped error surfaces as Next.js's own unhandled-exception 500 instead of a laundered 4xx. `resolvePublishedVersionId` (`lib/data/framework.ts`) throws a plain `Error` when no published `framework_versions` row matches -- deliberate, no fallback (AGENTS.md §2) -- so that becomes exactly such a 500. `components/assessment/StartAssessmentButton.tsx:33` calls `await res.json()` unconditionally, before checking `res.ok`, so a non-JSON error body (Next's default 500 page is not JSON) throws inside the `try`; the bare `catch` at line 39 replaces it with 'Something went wrong. Please try again.', discarding whatever the server actually said. PRE-EXISTING and CLIENT-WIDE, not Plan 1c's doing -- Task 1 only added one more production path that can reach an already-existing client defect. Re-derived the prevalence count myself rather than inheriting the figure a prior implementer reported (AGENTS.md §5): the same shape -- `res.json()` called unconditionally regardless of `res.ok`, inside a try, with a bare/generic catch that discards the real error -- appears at 8 call sites across 6 component files: `app/(authenticated)/orgs/new/page.tsx` (NewOrgPage.handleSubmit), `app/(authenticated)/orgs/[slug]/projects/new/NewProjectForm.tsx` (handleSubmit), `components/assessment/StartAssessmentButton.tsx` (start), `app/(public)/register/page.tsx` (handleSubmit), `app/(public)/invitations/[token]/AcceptInvitationClient.tsx` (2 sites: AcceptAction.handleAccept, RegisterForInvitationForm.handleSubmit), `app/(authenticated)/orgs/[slug]/settings/members/MembersManager.tsx` (2 sites: handleLeave, InviteForm.handleSubmit). Verified by reading every `'use client'` file that calls `fetch(` repo-wide (29 client files total; 9 call `fetch(`; no axios, react-query, useSWR or XMLHttpRequest anywhere in the client tree) and checking each call site's exact ordering by hand -- reproduces the prior "6 components, 8 call sites" figure exactly. A related but narrower-risk shape -- `res.ok` checked first, `res.json()` attempted only inside the error branch -- exists at 5 more sites (`change-password/page.tsx`; `AssessmentPageClient.tsx`'s fetchAssessment; `report/page.tsx`; `MembersManager.tsx`'s refresh/handleRemove/handleGrantOwner) and was deliberately excluded: the ok-check happens before any parse is attempted, the safer half of this same failure family. NOT fixing in Plan 1c: the throws (both `toResponse.ts` and `resolvePublishedVersionId`) are CORRECT and deliberate -- fail loud, no silent fallback, exactly what this plan requires. The SWALLOW is the bug, it is client-wide and pre-dates this plan, and it is shared by 8 near-identical call sites -- it deserves one deliberate, consistent fix (e.g. a shared fetch-and-parse helper that surfaces `res.status` and the parsed error even when the body is not JSON) rather than a drive-by patch to whichever single call site Task 1 happens to touch | **RE-TARGETED, Task 10 fix round 1 (found by `pr-review-toolkit:silent-failure-hunter`, not silently left stale): both originally-named tasks have now passed without the shared fix.** Task 9 did not touch client fetch error handling. Task 10 explicitly declined the shared-helper fix by name (task-10-report.md), and its fix round 1 added three MORE call sites to the safer 5-site group instead of reproducing this bug's shape or fixing it repo-wide (`AssessmentPageClient.tsx`'s `fetchEvidence`, `handleEvidenceUpload`, `handleEvidenceDelete` -- all check `res.ok` before parsing). New trigger: the C6 phase-exit review, or whichever future task next does a deliberate pass over client-side fetch error handling for an unrelated reason -- not a third named task, since naming individual tasks has now failed twice to produce the fix | Phase 1d -- first task, not an incidental pickup | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** This row's own trigger named "the C6 phase-exit review" -- this document is that review, and the fix (a shared fetch-and-parse helper touching roughly 13 call sites across 8 files) is genuine implementation work, not a register-reconciliation action; writing it here without its own TDD/review cycle would repeat the exact shortcut AGENTS.md exists to prevent. Spot-checked that the description still holds: `StartAssessmentButton.tsx:33` still calls `res.json()` before checking `res.ok` inside a bare `catch`. Given two previously-named tasks (Task 9, Task 10) and now a phase-exit review have all passed without the shared fix landing, re-targeting to "whichever task touches this next" a third time would repeat the exact failure this row already flagged about itself. Recommending explicitly: this should be a named, first-class task early in Phase 1d, not a rider on an unrelated task. |
| D-146 | There is no CI in this repository, so every mechanical guard this project built on the principle *mechanism beats prose* fires only when a human remembers to run it on a laptop | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-08 | Found by the `adversarial-reviewer` altitude pass during Plan 1c Task 1, and verified by the controller rather than accepted on report: there is no `.github`, no `.gitlab-ci.yml`, no `.circleci`, no `Jenkinsfile` and no `azure-pipelines.yml`; the only YAML in the tree is `docker/docker-compose.yml` and `docker/docker-compose.prod.yml`; `docker/Dockerfile`'s builder stage runs `npm run build` (= `next build`) and no tests; and `npm run verify` (`lint && test && build`) is a manual local script. **The reach is the whole project, not Plan 1c.** Three phases of work have been spent converting prose rules into executable guards on the explicit doctrine that a rule bound to no observable trigger will not fire -- `__tests__/integration/trigger-enumeration.test.ts`, `port-completeness.test.ts`, `preauth-surface.test.ts`, `__tests__/lint/effective-config.test.ts`, the 726-cell RBAC matrix, the RLS non-vacuity proofs. Every one of them is a test. Nothing runs the tests except a person choosing to. That is AGENTS.md §0's own law applied one level up and failing: the guards ARE the mechanism, and the mechanism that triggers the mechanism does not exist. Concretely visible in this task: the controller told an implementer that a drift test would convert a future production 500 into a red build, and there is no build to go red **BOUNDARY REMOVED, 2026-08-10 (Phase 1d-i Task 5) -- this closure's own text is now partly stale and is corrected here rather than left to mislead.** The closure above records, as a standing limit, that 'Playwright/e2e is deliberately excluded (D-185/D-188 flakiness ...) -- a guard living only in a Playwright spec is not covered here.' That is no longer true. Task 5 added a `test-e2e` job to `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (commits 4be4524, 70e00c0) which runs the full suite against a production build on every push and pull request, green: 48 passed, 1 skipped. The two blockers that justified the exclusion were resolved rather than waived -- D-188 was closed by Task 3 (the live Resend call removed), and D-185 was QUARANTINED rather than fixed, via `test.fixme` with its coverage cost stated in that row. **What remains true from the original text:** a regression visible only to the quarantined assessment-flow spec is still not covered, and the required-check list is still `lint, typecheck, test, build` alone until the promotion task lands. Recorded because a closed row reads as finished, so its stated limits get inherited as current fact -- the precise lens an adversarial pass named as unsearched in this phase's own design review (the register's recently-CLOSED rows), and the second instance in this phase after D-185's own 'CI gate' overclaim. **[Status-field narrative relocated here 2026-08-10, Phase 1d-i C6 register review. It had been written into the STATUS column, which left the row displaying 'Open' while its own text said 'Closed-done'. No text was deleted; only moved.]** **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation. Status preserved, not resolved unilaterally.** Still true: no `.github`, `.gitlab-ci.yml`, `.circleci`, `Jenkinsfile` or `azure-pipelines.yml` exists; `npm run verify` remains a manual local script. This reconciliation is itself a data point for the row's own thesis -- it is the human running the mechanism the mechanism depends on. **Closed-done, 2026-08-10.** Human partner ruled to close now. Installed `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (commit 80288d8, with a same-commit D-091 amendment and D-192 for a Node-version mismatch this work surfaced), then fixed a real workflow-file validation error found by actually running it, not by reading it (commit 2b71e82 -- confirmed with actionlint before the second push: the `env` context is not allowed inside `services.<id>.options`). Green run: https://github.com/Alvin-Nahabwe/makai-rai-platform/actions/runs/31357119297 (`gh run view --json status,conclusion`: status=completed, conclusion=success, head 2b71e82). Verified live in that run's own log, not inferred from the green checkmark alone: Test Files 50 passed (50), Tests 1567 passed (1567), zero skipped; every guard this row names by filename ran with its own assertion count -- trigger-enumeration.test.ts (14 tests), port-completeness.test.ts (2 tests), preauth-surface.test.ts (4 tests), effective-config.test.ts (10 tests) -- plus permission-matrix.test.ts (the RBAC matrix). Applying the D-191 check this closure is itself subject to: the PROPERTY is 'these guards fire on every push, not only when a human remembers'; what the check causes is GitHub Actions running the full `npm test` (all of `__tests__/**`, including every file just named) plus lint/typecheck/build on every push and PR, failing the job on any non-zero exit; the second entails the first for the vitest-based guards this row's own text named, which is what it was actually about. Two honest boundaries, named rather than folded in silently: (1) this proves FIRING, not GATING -- `main` has no branch protection, so a red run does not yet block a merge or push; opened as D-193 rather than assumed covered by this closure. (2) Playwright/e2e is deliberately excluded (D-185/D-188 flakiness; the workflow's own trailing comment states the re-enablement condition) -- a guard living only in a Playwright spec is not covered here. Both directions of the report-status mechanism were independently observed, not just the happy path: the first push genuinely failed (a real invalid workflow file) and was correctly reported `failure`; the second genuinely passed and was correctly reported `success`. Two real gaps the pipeline found by being run rather than read: `scripts/provision-app-db-role.sh` assumed a `docker exec` target that does not exist in CI (fixed; D-091 amended accordingly), and this Prisma 7 install has no `postinstall` auto-generate hook (confirmed by reading the installed `prisma`/`@prisma/client` package.json files directly, not assumed from older Prisma behaviour, and not named in the original task brief) -- both are exactly the class of thing D-146 existed to make routine rather than exceptional. | **Needs the human partner's decision, which is why this is a row and not a unilateral fix.** The repo now has a real GitHub remote (D-067 closed 2026-08-05), so GitHub Actions is available at no infrastructure cost, but a CI pipeline is a standing commitment with its own maintenance surface and the integration suite needs a live Postgres service container plus both `makrai` and `makrai_test` migrated -- that is a real design task, not a config paste. Installing it unilaterally while claiming it closes the controller's own process gap is the move D-136 was explicitly held back from making. Candidate for the Plan 1c C6 phase exit | Phase 1d (pending human partner decision) | Closed-done |
| D-147 | Registering a new framework version is not designed as an operation -- every future `meta.version` bump must remember to ship a paired migration inserting its registry row, and nothing forces that pairing | RISK | 2026-08-08 | Distinct from D-143, which is Closed-done and scoped to the single historical instance (framework 3.0.0 existed in content with no writer for its pin). This row is the generalised shape D-143 leaves behind. `resolvePublishedVersionId` resolves `data/assessmentAreas.json`'s `meta.version` to a `framework_versions` row by exact string equality, and throws when none matches -- correct, deliberate, no fallback. But the two artifacts that must agree live in different places and ship by different means: content is a JSON file edited in a normal commit, the registry row is inserted by a hand-written migration (the 3.0.0 row was inserted by hand in `20260807102031_framework_registry_and_pin`, the only precedent). A content-only edit that bumps the version therefore type-checks, builds, and deploys green, then 500s every `POST .../assessments` platform-wide from the moment it ships. **Deliberately not fixed by promoting the concept into the schema.** The obvious fix is a currency marker -- `status` today distinguishes `published` from `deprecated`, a validity state, so the database cannot answer *which version should a new assessment get*. Adding that column is a framework data-model change, and D-138 gives the framework data model to Plan 2a so content rows and the domain axis are designed once together; doing it in 1c is exactly the foreclosure D-137 binds 1c against. What Plan 1c does instead: Task 1 fix round 2 added a test asserting the seeded row's `semver` equals the content file's `meta.version`, so the drift is visible the moment it is introduced -- subject to D-146, since nothing automatic runs that test. **AMENDED 2026-08-09 at the C6 fix wave -- this row's BLAST RADIUS widened, and the amendment is the point.** C6 finding F5 added a creation-time drift guard: `POST .../assessments` now resolves the current version AND compares the registry row's `contentHash` against `computeBundleHash()`, refusing with 409 when they differ. That closes a real hole (creation used to succeed by semver alone and mint an assessment whose first autosave 409s forever, D-167). But it changes what this row is about. Before, only a `meta.version` BUMP broke creation. Now ANY byte-level change to the four hashed `data/*.json` files does -- a whitespace or key-order change from a formatter, a one-word typo fix in a question, a trailing-newline normalisation -- because `contentHash` is a byte hash and the sole `framework_versions` row is a hardcoded literal in `20260807102031_framework_registry_and_pin/migration.sql`. Confirmed there is no remedy short of a migration and redeploy: no registration script, route or seed path for a new hash exists anywhere in `prisma/` or `scripts/` (checked directly, not assumed). **This is still the right trade** -- the alternative is minting assessments nobody can answer -- but the pre-existing gap this row names is no longer a papercut for content authors, it is a product-wide creation outage triggerable by a formatter. That raises its priority rather than changing its fix | Plan 2a design, where the framework data model is settled and *publish a framework version* can be designed as a single operation covering content, hash and registry row together | Phase 2a | Open |
| D-148 | `resetDb()`'s (`__tests__/helpers/db.ts:45-52`) truncate-exclusion list is hand-maintained, and the decision not to generalise it lived only in a code comment, not a register row, despite the comment stating both an explicit conscious decision and an explicit pick-up trigger -- everything AGENTS.md §6 requires of a row | RISK | 2026-08-08 | The exclusion list (`NOT IN ('_prisma_migrations', 'framework_versions')`) is a 2-item hand-written list, exactly the shape AGENTS.md §3 warns is "a latent defect with a timestamp on it" when a list must be complete -- and this one must be complete, because a reference table left off it gets truncated by every test file's `beforeEach`, breaking every later fixture that depends on the row surviving (the same failure class the comment itself documents finding, live, for `framework_versions`, in Task 1's own base commit). The comment already states the conscious decision (generalise via e.g. a `COMMENT ON TABLE` marker queried from `pg_description`, versus leave it hand-maintained) and the reasoning (the failure mode is self-diagnosing -- a loud FK violation on the next fixture insert, not a silent leak -- and Plan 2a's shape, which reference tables it adds if any, is not yet designed) and an explicit trigger ("Revisit when Plan 2a actually adds a second non-tenant reference table") -- §6 does not accept a comment as a substitute for a row no matter how complete the comment reads, and this project has been burned by exactly that substitution twice (D-063, D-068). Whether to generate the list from disk is a Plan 2a question and is NOT decided by opening this row -- opening it only makes the already-made decision (not now) auditable rather than only-in-prose. The code comment stays where it is, useful in place, and now points at this row instead of re-stating the full reasoning | Plan 2a actually adds a second non-tenant reference table | Phase 2a | Open |
| D-149 | Turbopack's `output: 'standalone'` tracer copies `.env` into `.next/standalone/.env` for a route whose module graph calls `fs.readFileSync` at all (any resolvability, any timing), through a path that `outputFileTracingIncludes`/`-Excludes` do not govern and that never appears in any generated `.nft.json`. Task 2 fix round 1 closes the ROOT CAUSE (no `.dockerignore` existed, so `.env` entered the Docker build context unconditionally) alongside the symptom (the `postbuild` removal); the residual, still-open risk is narrower than before but not zero -- see fix-round-1 update below | RISK | 2026-08-08 | Found live while verifying Task 2's `computeBundleHash()` (byte-hashes 4 files under `data/` for O-13/O-14): a clean build with `bundleHash.ts` wired into `lib/data/framework.ts` (imported by `app/api/v1/orgs/[slug]/assessments/route.ts`) produced `.next/standalone/.env`, byte-identical to this repo's real `.env` (containing a live-looking `RESEND_API_KEY`), confirmed ABSENT from a clean build of the same tree at Task 1 HEAD (no `computeBundleHash` anywhere). `docker/Dockerfile:31-35` copies `.next/standalone` wholesale into the shipped image, so unmitigated this reaches production. Five distinct remediations were tried and measured, not assumed, before landing on the one that worked: (1) a Turbopack ignore-comment on the `join(...)` call -- no effect; (2) scoping the dynamic path segment under a literal `'data'` folder -- removed Turbopack's "whole project traced unintentionally" warning and most incidental bloat (`docs/`, `lib/`, `public/`), but not `.env`; (3) `outputFileTracingExcludes: { '/*': ['.env*'] }` -- verified twice, under two different `bundleHash.ts` implementations, to have zero effect; (4) fully unrolling the 4 reads to all-string-literal paths -- removed the remaining incidental bloat entirely, but not `.env`; (5) making the hash computation lazy instead of eager at module load, to rule out build-time execution as the trigger -- no effect. `.env` never appears in any of the 91 generated `.nft.json` trace files (grepped directly), which is why options governing that list (2)-(3) cannot reach it -- the copy goes through a separate, undocumented path inside Turbopack's native tracer that this task did not have reason or scope to decompile further. The fix that worked: `package.json`'s `postbuild` script (`rm -rf .next/standalone/.env*`), which runs automatically after every `next build`, including the one `docker/Dockerfile` invokes via `RUN npm run build` -- verified live by a from-clean rebuild showing zero warnings, zero `.env*` anywhere under `.next/standalone` (recursive check, not just top-level), and all four `data/*.json` files present. **FIX ROUND 1 UPDATE (2026-08-08), coordinator-directed:** the original entry above closed the SYMPTOM (`.env` reaching `.next/standalone`) without asking how `.env` reached the Docker BUILD CONTEXT in the first place -- the coordinator checked what the original entry did not mention: there was no `.dockerignore` anywhere in the repository, and `docker/Dockerfile:18` is `COPY . .`, so `.env` entered the builder stage unconditionally regardless of the Turbopack quirk -- the SAME leak would recur via any other future mechanism that sweeps build-context files into a directory the runner stage copies, because the actual root cause was never "Turbopack does something odd," it was "nothing stops secrets from entering the build context at all." `.gitignore` gives no protection here: it governs git, and Docker never reads it -- `.env` being gitignored was true throughout and irrelevant. Closed with a `.dockerignore` at the repository root (verified against `docker/docker-compose.prod.yml:9`'s `context: ..` and `docs/runbooks/troubleshooting.md:214`'s `docker build -f docker/Dockerfile .`, both confirming the build context is the repo root), excluding `.env`/`.env.*` (keeping `.env.example`), `*.pem`, `e2e/fixtures/.auth/` (live session cookies per that file's own doc comment), `.superpowers/` and `.remember/` (a second local tooling workspace found during this round, not named in `.gitignore` at all, self-excluding via its own nested `.gitignore`, same class of risk), plus the standard `node_modules`/`.next`/`app/generated/prisma`/`.git`/test-artifact entries. Sanity-checked via `git ls-files --others --ignored --exclude-from=.dockerignore` (an approximation of Docker's matcher using git's, not a Docker-native check) confirming `.env` matches, `.env.example` and every spot-checked source file do not. **Honest limit, stated rather than glossed over:** pattern-matching correctness was NOT verified with an actual `docker build` -- the coordinator's scope boundary for this round explicitly excluded building the image, so this rests on syntax review and the git-engine approximation, not an executed Docker build. Also checked and cleared, empirically not by reasoning: whether `next build` inlines any `.env` value into the client bundle, which would have meant build-time and runtime configuration were entangled. No `NEXT_PUBLIC_`-prefixed var exists in `.env`, no `env:` field exists in `next.config.ts`, no source file references `NEXT_PUBLIC_` at all, and grepping the actual secret value (and every `.env` variable name) against the real compiled `.next/static/` output (and the broader `.next/standalone/`) found zero matches -- no entanglement, nothing to report as a blocker. The two fixes are now explicitly two independent layers, documented as such in `.dockerignore`'s own header comment (`.dockerignore` cannot hold documentation in `package.json` itself, which has no comment syntax) so a future reader does not delete one believing the other makes it redundant. **What remains open and why this row stays Open, not Closed:** `.dockerignore` closes the specific path this investigation found (`.env` via the build context) but not the general mechanism -- a general post-build assertion that `.next/standalone`'s contents match an expected allowlist, catching ANY future file Turbopack's tracer might similarly sweep in, or any future file that misses this `.dockerignore` list, is still not built. The pick-up trigger is unchanged. **Cross-reference, added when D-156 closed:** excluding `.env` from the Docker BUILDER stage here (correct, and the point of this row) removed `DATABASE_URL` from the build environment along with the secret -- and `next build` had, until then, accidentally kept working only because the leak this row fixed was also supplying the connection string `requireDatabaseUrl()` (`lib/data/connection.ts`) checks for at module-import time. Fixing the leak here is what made that latent, unrelated-looking eager-evaluation shape start breaking the build three steps later. See D-156 for the fix (the eager check is now lazy) and for why its own original framing ("pre-existing, independent of anything Task 6 touched") was wrong and was corrected rather than left standing | Before Plan 1c's C6 phase exit, or the next task that adds a `fs`/`readFileSync` call to any traced route (App Router page or route handler) -- whichever comes first. Add a `postbuild` (or CI, subject to D-146) step that lists `.next/standalone`'s top-level entries and fails loudly on anything outside an explicit allowlist, rather than continuing to special-case individual filenames as they are discovered | Phase 1d | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** The specific leak stays closed (`.dockerignore` present, `postbuild` script unchanged: `rm -rf .next/standalone/.env*`). The general mechanism -- a post-build assertion listing `.next/standalone`'s contents against an explicit allowlist -- was not built; `find scripts -iname "*standalone*" -o -iname "*allowlist*"` returns nothing. No task after Task 2 added a new traced `fs`/`readFileSync` call reachable from a route, so the specific risk window this row worries about did not reopen during Phase 1c, but the generic guard remains absent. |
| D-150 | Plain-text and CSV evidence cannot be uploaded, because text has no magic bytes and this product identifies file types by signature only | DECISION | 2026-08-08 | Found by Task 5's implementer as a spec/implementation divergence: design spec §3.2 listed "plain text, CSV" among accepted formats while `lib/evidence/inspect.ts` implements neither, and no row reconciled the two. The spec was wrong and has been corrected in place. **Why the implementation is right and the spec was not:** identifying text requires a heuristic rather than a signature — "the bytes look printable" — and every file this allowlist exists to reject also looks printable under one. HTML is text, SVG is text, a script payload is text. Accepting `text/plain` by exclusion therefore accepts everything, makes the allowlist decorative, and re-opens precisely the case O-7 exists to close (an HTML document declared as `image/png`). `inspect()` returns not-ok for anything it cannot positively name, with no fallthrough — the AGENTS.md §2 fail-open trigger, decided deliberately rather than reached for | **The product gap is real and deliberately left open, not dismissed.** A team wanting to attach a CSV of fairness-test results, or a plain-text model card, has no path today and must convert to PDF first — plausible institutional artifacts, and the friction falls hardest on resource-constrained sites, which cuts against this product's own stated adopter. Three options when it is picked up, none decidable now: accept the formats bound to a declared extension and rely on the download path's octet-stream/attachment/nosniff response (O-8) for safety rather than on identification; accept them only inside a zip, which has a signature; or keep the restriction and say so in the upload UI, which today just rejects with "unrecognised file type" and no guidance. Pick-up trigger: the first pilot where a team reports evidence it cannot upload, or Task 10 if the attach UI needs to explain the restriction to a user | Phase 2 | Open |
| D-151 | The O-18 rate limit on evidence upload (`lib/rate-limit.ts`) was keyed, in its STORE (not its config lookup), by `${identifier}:${method}:${path}` using the REQUEST's literal path -- so "10 uploads per 60s" was actually 10 per (user, exact assessment id) pair, not 10 total | RISK | 2026-08-08 | Found twice, independently: first during this task's own fresh C1 senior-security pass while deriving obligations for the upload route; then confirmed live, with concrete counts, by a `pr-review-toolkit:silent-failure-hunter` pass over the finished implementation before this task's commit (task-6-report.md) -- 20 POSTs split across 2 assessment ids (10 each) both succeeded against a configured max of 10; 50 POSTs across 50 distinct ids all succeeded. The CONFIG lookup half (which RATE_LIMITS entry applies) was already correctly scoped by the `'*'`-wildcard key and was never what this row was about -- **FIXED, same task, same commit.** `checkRateLimit` now buckets its store key by the MATCHED RATE_LIMITS key rather than the raw request path, but ONLY for keys containing `'*'` -- every pre-existing key (`POST:/api/auth/register`, `POST:/api/auth`, `DELETE:/api/users/me`, `default`) contains no `'*'` and keeps bucketing by the raw literal path, BYTE-FOR-BYTE unchanged (verified by a dedicated regression block in `__tests__/unit/rate-limit.test.ts`: `'default'` still gives each distinct path its own budget; the `POST:/api/auth` prefix still gives each distinct sub-path -- e.g. a different OAuth callback provider -- its own budget). Verified live: `__tests__/unit/rate-limit.test.ts`'s two new aggregation tests (varying the assessment id, and separately the org slug, across 10+1 requests for one user) now correctly cap at 10, and a same-rule-different-user test confirms the fix does not merge budgets ACROSS users. **What stays open, and why this row is not simply deleted:** the underlying mechanism -- bucketing by raw literal path -- is unchanged for every NON-wildcard key, including `'default'`, which every OTHER dynamic-segment route in this product (e.g. `PATCH .../assessments/[id]`) still falls under. Collapsing THAT bucketing the same way would merge rate-limit budgets across every route sharing `'default'` or the `'POST:/api/auth'` prefix for a given user -- a broad behaviour change spanning routes this task has no reach-assessment for (see D-153, the new row carrying that general, lower-urgency remainder) | Closed for the evidence-upload instance; see D-153 for the general pattern | Phase 1c | Closed-done — **correction, fix round 1 of Task 6's review, 2026-08-08: the original closure above was TRUE at the `checkRateLimit`-function level and FALSE at the system level, and the row did not say so.** The aggregation tests cited as "verified live" call `checkRateLimit` directly with an explicit `userId` argument — they do prove the STORE-KEY fix works given a real userId. What they do not prove, and what the original closure did not distinguish, is whether a real request flowing through `proxy.ts` ever SUPPLIES that userId. It did not: D-114 Finding 2 (a separate, pre-existing bug in code this task does not own) meant `getToken` threw on every request carrying a session cookie, so `identifier` in `checkRateLimit` was ALWAYS the caller's IP, never their userId, for every route including this one — the fix this row closed was real but had never yet had an opportunity to bind in production or through any live-pipeline test. Closing a row on a claim true only of the unit under test, while the row's own language ("10 uploads per user per minute") describes system behaviour, is the exact gap a reviewer independent of the author exists to catch — confirmed by the coordinator verifying the collapse live through the real proxy pipeline (six POSTs with six different `X-Forwarded-For` values each returning `remaining=9`) before writing to say so. Now genuinely true end-to-end: D-114's fix (`requireAuthSecret()`, `proxy.ts` now passing it to `getToken`) makes `userId` actually populate for authenticated requests, so THIS row's store-key fix now has a real userId to bucket by outside of tests, not only inside them |
| D-152 | The evidence upload route's pre-buffer size gate (`Content-Length` checked before `req.formData()`) has nothing to check when `Content-Length` is absent or understated (e.g. chunked transfer encoding, or a client that simply lies low) -- the request still reaches `req.formData()`, which buffers the full body regardless, before `inspect()`'s OWN post-buffer size check (`lib/evidence/inspect.ts`) is reached | RISK | 2026-08-08 | `lib/evidence/inspect.ts`'s own doc comment states this is "a size LIMIT, not a DoS control ... resource exhaustion is tracked separately in the register, not claimed as solved by this constant" -- checked directly rather than trusted (AGENTS.md §5, "look at the thing itself"), and as of Task 5's commit that claim was FALSE: no register row existed for it (`grep -n -e "resource exhaustion" -e "DoS" docs/DEFERRED_REGISTER.md` returned nothing before this row). A comment asserting something is tracked elsewhere, when it is not, is the exact "laundered deferral" AGENTS.md §6 warns is worse than a silent skip, because it reads as handled on inspection. Also confirmed live: Next.js's `experimental.serverActions.bodySizeLimit` (`next.config.ts`, currently `'2mb'`) governs Server Actions only, not App Router Route Handlers (`app/api/**/route.ts`) -- there is no platform-level backstop for this route at all, so the Content-Length gate plus `inspect()`'s post-buffer rejection are the ONLY two lines of defence, and both can be bypassed by a body with no declared length. The failure mode is memory pressure from buffering an oversized body before rejection, not data corruption or a security bypass (the row is still never created). **NARROWED, same task:** a related but DISTINCT code-level gap -- a non-numeric or negative `Content-Length` (e.g. `"abc"`) made `Number(declaredLength) > MAX_BYTES` evaluate `NaN > MAX_BYTES`, i.e. `false`, silently PASSING the gate rather than being absent from it -- was found by the same silent-failure-hunter pass and FIXED directly (not left to this row): the comparison now requires `Number.isFinite(...)` and non-negative, with tests (`__tests__/integration/evidence-upload.test.ts`) proving the pre-fix behaviour would have gone red for the WRONG reason (masked by the `req.formData()` catch below it) before asserting the fix's specific rejection message. This row remains open ONLY for the absent/understated-header case, which is inherent to HTTP rather than a local code defect | The first observed resource-exhaustion incident, a hosted (non-local) deployment (D-018), or a task that adds a streaming size guard reading the body incrementally and aborting once MAX_BYTES is exceeded rather than buffering it whole via `req.formData()` | Phase 2 | Open |
| D-153 | The general pattern D-151 found evidence upload's instance of: `checkRateLimit`'s store key (`lib/rate-limit.ts`) is built from the RAW request path for every NON-wildcard `RATE_LIMITS` entry, so `'default'` (and the `'POST:/api/auth'` prefix) give every distinct path its own independent budget rather than bounding a user's total activity across the routes/sub-paths sharing that rule | RISK | 2026-08-08 | Split out of D-151 when that row's evidence-upload-specific instance was fixed in this same task (see D-151's closure). This is the remainder: EVERY other dynamic-segment route in the product (e.g. `PATCH .../assessments/[id]`, `PATCH .../remediation`) still has the identical property, and always has -- this is not new. Deliberately left open rather than fixed alongside D-151, for the reason D-151's closure note states: collapsing `'default'`'s bucketing would merge rate-limit budgets across every route currently falling under it for a given user, and collapsing the auth prefix's would merge budgets across different OAuth callback providers -- both broad, product-wide behaviour changes with no reach-assessment done here. Lower urgency than D-151 was: no OTHER route currently under `'default'` does the buffer-and-hash-and-blob-write work that made the evidence-upload instance dangerous enough to fix immediately -- they are cheap JSON CRUD, where a per-path budget is a materially smaller gap | A second byte-accepting or otherwise expensive endpoint added under `'default'` (rather than getting its own wildcard-scoped entry the way evidence upload did), an observed abuse pattern exploiting per-path budgets on an EXISTING route, or a task that redesigns `checkRateLimit`'s store key generally (e.g. having `findConfig` return enough information to bucket by "route shape" for every entry, not just wildcard ones) | Phase 2 | Open |
| D-154 | `createEvidence`'s O-23 idempotency check (`lib/data/evidence.ts`: `findFirst` then `create`) does not hold under TRUE concurrency -- two near-simultaneous requests for the same claim can both pass `findFirst` (seeing no existing row) before either commits, and the LOSING transaction's `create()` then throws a raw Postgres unique-constraint violation (`evidence_dedup_key`) that `toResponse()` does not recognise and rethrows as an unhandled 500 | RISK | 2026-08-08 | Found by a `pr-review-toolkit:silent-failure-hunter` pass over the finished Task 6 implementation (task-6-report.md). The failure mode is LOUD, not silent -- no duplicate row is created (the database constraint genuinely prevents it) and no wrong data is returned -- so this is a robustness/UX gap, not a security or data-integrity one: a user who "loses" a genuine double-submission race sees a generic server error even though their upload, in effect, succeeded (someone else's identical request created the row). **Deliberately NOT fixed inline in this task**, unlike D-151/the Content-Length NaN gap: the fix shape (catch the specific Prisma `P2002` error and retry the `findFirst` once, since `evidence_dedup_key`'s `UNIQUE ... NULLS NOT DISTINCT` form is not mirrored in `schema.prisma`'s DSL -- confirmed by reading the schema directly -- so Prisma's `upsert` cannot target it by name) is understood, but proving it non-vacuously needs TRUE concurrent execution, which this project's existing test conventions do not yet have a pattern for (a `Promise.all` of two `withOrg` calls does not guarantee the interleaving the race depends on, and a flaky concurrency test is worse than none). Attempting this under this task's own time pressure, without a reliable way to prove the fix actually closes the race rather than just LOOKING like it does, was judged a worse trade than registering it precisely | The first observed instance in practice (a genuine double-submission producing a visible 500 to a user whose upload actually succeeded), or a task that establishes a reliable pattern for testing genuine transaction-level races in this suite (e.g. a synchronisation point that pauses one transaction until a second reaches a controlled point) | Phase 1d | Open **Re-targeted 2026-08-09 at the Phase 1c phase-exit register reconciliation.** `grep -n "P2002" lib/data/evidence.ts` returns nothing -- the retry is not implemented; `createEvidence`'s `findFirst` then `create` shape is unchanged. Worth flagging: this row's own target ("whichever next touches `lib/data/evidence.ts`") fired repeatedly within this very phase -- Tasks 7, 8, 9 and 11 all touched that file -- without picking this up, the same shape as D-145's repeated miss. Tightened rather than repeated: the real trigger is not "touches the file", it is "a task that also establishes this project's first true-concurrency test pattern", which is a capability gap, not a scheduling one. |
