| D-191 | An obligation names a PROPERTY while its implementation and test name a MECHANISM, and nothing checks the two refer to the same thing -- eight instances in one phase | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-10 | **PROPOSED AMENDMENT, NOT INSTALLED -- awaiting the human partner's ruling, for the same reason D-136 was held back: installing my own process fix for my own failure pattern, while asserting it closes that pattern, is the move that needs a second opinion.** The eight, all found by review rather than by the mechanism that should catch them: Task 5's size fixture was rejected by the no-signature fallthrough, not the size guard, so deleting the guard left the suite green; Task 7's concurrency test proved the ROLLBACK direction while its docstring claimed both, leaving untested the direction where evidence silently vanishes; O-14's in-progress half was implemented on the evidence-upload route, and uploading an artifact is not answering a question; then again on `respondToAssessment` while `completeAssessment` -- the path that permanently bakes a score into an immutable record -- had no check at all; the PDF route stamped a pinned hash on content it had not used, with the banner suppressed by construction for exactly that case; O-24's second clause was recorded as satisfied while the grant model made it unimplementable; the drift banner asserted the report is not recalculated while the largest section recalculates on every view; and creation could mint an assessment that can never be answered. Every one passed its own tests. **Why the existing mechanism cannot catch this class:** `__tests__/integration/trigger-enumeration.test.ts` pins MECHANISMS -- it greps for `if (!resolution.matches)` and for named call sites. A guard implemented on the wrong surface, or a test asserting the wrong direction, satisfies the grep exactly. The C6 whole-branch reviewer put it plainly: a grep-for-the-mechanism test cannot verify a property-to-mechanism translation, by construction | **The proposed trigger, bound to an observable moment per §0.1:** *you are about to record an obligation as discharged* -- in a report, a commit message, a coverage map, or a register closure. The check is two sentences and a question. State the obligation's property in its own words. State what the test actually causes to happen. Then ask whether the second entails the first, and specifically: could the property be violated by some other mechanism while this test still passes? If yes, the test does not discharge the obligation and the gap is named rather than closed. Falsifiable test of the amendment itself, stated so it can be judged rather than defended: if a later phase's C6 finds an obligation recorded as met that was satisfied on a neighbouring mechanism, the check is not firing and must be reconsidered rather than reworded -- Phase 1d, with the human partner's ruling on D-136 -- the two are the same shape (a planning-stage check bound to a handover moment) and should be decided together rather than one at a time | Phase 1d | **Closed-done, 2026-08-10.** Human partner ruled to install. Now an AGENTS.md §2 trigger row bound to *you are about to record an obligation as discharged*, carrying the property-vs-mechanism question and the eight instances. Deliberately a READING obligation rather than another test, because the existing mechanism greps for mechanisms and so cannot catch this class -- stated in the row itself so nobody later improves it into a grep. |
| D-192 | `docker/Dockerfile:5` pins `FROM node:20-alpine`; local dev and the CI workflow just installed for D-146 both run Node 22 (`node --version` on the dev machine: v22.22.3) | RISK | 2026-08-10 | Found while building `.github/workflows/ci.yml` for D-146. The task's verified fact to work from was 'Node v22.22.3 locally, package.json has no engines field', so CI was pinned to `node-version: '22'` to match what this project is actually developed and tested against -- `docker/Dockerfile` was deliberately NOT touched to match: editing it is outside D-146's scope (installing CI, not reconciling deployment config), and picking a canonical version unilaterally, inside a task about something else, risks exactly the D-136/D-191 shape (a unilateral process fix asserted to be correct rather than put to the human partner). Not a hard break today: `node_modules/next/package.json` declares `engines.node: '>=20.9.0'`, which `node:20-alpine` satisfies, and nothing in this repo enforces any Node version anywhere -- `package.json` has no `engines` field and there is no `.nvmrc`. So three numbers now describe one application (20 in the Dockerfile, 22 locally, 22 in CI) with nothing keeping them aligned, and CI passing is no longer evidence that the Dockerfile's Node 20 build path was exercised at all **Closed-done, 2026-08-10.** Human partner ruled Node 22 canonical. `.nvmrc` (containing `22`) is now the single source of truth and is genuinely READ, not copied: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` replaced `node-version: '22'` with `node-version-file: '.nvmrc'`, so CI resolves the same file a developer's `nvm use` does. `docker/Dockerfile` cannot do that -- a `FROM` line takes no file input -- so it restates the version as `ARG NODE_VERSION=22` with `FROM node:${NODE_VERSION}-alpine`; that restatement is irreducible, which is precisely why it needed a mechanical pin rather than a convention. `package.json` gained `engines.node: '>=22'`, which is ADVISORY and not enforcing: npm does not check it without `engine-strict`, deliberately not set here because it also applies to transitive dependencies' engines fields and is a known source of spurious install failures. The enforcement is `__tests__/lint/node-version-alignment.test.ts` (5 tests), which DISCOVERS declaration sites from `git ls-files` rather than listing them, so a second Dockerfile or a new workflow is in scope the moment it is tracked -- AGENTS.md section 3's generate-don't-write rule, applied to the exact defect this row describes. **Non-vacuity proven in two stages, because the first red was the WRONG red:** written against the divergent tree the suite failed 4 of 5, but the Dockerfile assertion failed with ENOENT on the absent `.nvmrc`, not on the version mismatch it exists to catch -- a green after fixing everything at once would have proven only that the test detects a missing file. So `.nvmrc` was added ALONE and the suite re-run, producing the intended failure `docker/Dockerfile:5 builds on node:20-alpine: expected 20 to be 22`, and only then was the Dockerfile changed. Applying the D-191 check to this closure: the PROPERTY is 'the declared Node major cannot silently diverge across the places that declare it'; what the test CAUSES is every tracked workflow and Dockerfile being parsed and compared against `.nvmrc`, with discovery itself asserted non-empty so a broken scan fails loudly instead of passing vacuously; the second entails the first FOR DECLARED VERSIONS only -- it cannot make any runtime OBEY the declaration, so a developer running Node 20 with no version manager stays out of its reach, and that gap is named rather than folded in. Verified beyond the test, per section 5: `docker build --check -f docker/Dockerfile .` resolved `docker.io/library/node:22-alpine` metadata and reported 'Check complete, no warnings found' -- which proves the ARG substitution actually resolves and that tag really exists, not merely that the syntax parses. Gates at closure: `npm run lint` exit 0, `npm run typecheck` exit 0, `npx vitest run` 1572/1572 across 51 files (up from 1567/50 -- the 5 new tests, fully accounted for). One finding this work surfaced and did not fix, opened as D-194: the Dockerfile's `deps` stage is dead. | A human partner decision on which Node major version is canonical for this project, then align `docker/Dockerfile`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, and add an `engines` field to `package.json` (or an `.nvmrc` both CI and Docker read from) so the three cannot silently drift again. Natural to pick up alongside D-018 (hosting, still Open, and the reason `docker/Dockerfile` hasn't been touched since the D-091-era transport it still assumes) | Phase 2 (alongside D-018 hosting) | Closed-done |
| D-193 | CI now runs on every push/PR (D-146 closed) but `main` has no branch protection or required status checks, so a red run does not block a merge or a direct push | RISK | 2026-08-10 | Checked before D-146's fix, not assumed: `gh api repos/.../branches/main/protection` returned 404 'Branch not protected'. Installing the workflow does not change that -- GitHub Actions REPORTS status on every push (proven both directions during D-146's own verification: this task's first push genuinely failed a workflow-file check and was correctly reported `failure`; its second genuinely passed and was correctly reported `success`), but nothing currently PREVENTS a push or merge despite a red run. D-146's own wording is specifically that guards must 'fire on push', which is now true and is all this row's parent closes; whether they should also be REQUIRED (gating a merge, not just observing it) is a related but distinct decision nobody has made yet. Not fixed here: configuring branch protection has a real cost of its own (it would have blocked this task's own direct pushes to `main` while iterating D-146 to green) and deserves the human partner's call, not a unilateral setting change bundled into an unrelated closure **Closed-done, 2026-08-10.** Human partner ruled to enable protection now, explicitly accepting the stated cost: no more direct pushes to `main`, including for fixing CI itself. Configured with `gh api -X PUT repos/.../branches/main/protection`: required status check `lint, typecheck, test, build`; `strict: true` so a branch must be up to date with `main` before it can merge; `enforce_admins: true`; `allow_force_pushes: false`; `allow_deletions: false`. **The check NAME was read from the live API, not transcribed from the workflow file, and that distinction is load-bearing:** required-status-check matching is by check-RUN name, a check run is produced per JOB, and this workflow's top-level `name:` is `CI` while its job's name is `lint, typecheck, test, build`. Requiring `CI` would have produced protection that looks configured and blocks every merge forever, because no check by that name is ever created. The value used came from `repos/.../commits/6fc18a1/check-runs` on a real green run. `required_pull_request_reviews` is deliberately NULL: this is a single-contributor repository and GitHub does not permit an author to approve their own pull request, so requiring approvals would deadlock every merge; the gate here is the status check, not a human reviewer. **`enforce_admins: true` is the setting that actually implements this row**, and it was named to the human partner as a sub-decision rather than bundled in silently -- with it false, the repository owner (the only contributor) could still push directly, leaving this row's stated problem unfixed behind a protection page that looked correct. **Verified by attempting the thing it must prevent, rather than by reading the settings back:** an empty commit was made on local `main` and genuinely pushed, and the remote rejected it -- `remote: error: GH006: Protected branch update failed for refs/heads/main`, `Required status check "lint, typecheck, test, build" is expected.`, `! [remote rejected] main -> main (protected branch hook declined)`. The probe commit was then discarded with `git reset --hard origin/main` and local `main` re-confirmed identical to `origin/main`. Applying the D-191 check to this closure: the PROPERTY is 'a change cannot reach `main` without CI having passed on it'; what the configuration CAUSES is GitHub refusing any ref update to `main` whose head commit lacks a successful `lint, typecheck, test, build` check run, admins included; the second entails the first for both the direct-push and merge paths, which is what this row was about. **Boundary named rather than folded in:** Playwright/e2e is still not part of that check (D-185, D-188), so 'CI passed' continues to mean lint, typecheck, vitest and build -- a regression visible only to a browser test can still reach `main`. That is the same limit D-146's closure recorded, now inherited by the gate rather than removed by it. The ALLOWED direction -- a pull request with a green check can merge -- is exercised by the pull request that carries this very closure, and its outcome is reported in that merge commit; had it failed, this row would have been reopened rather than quietly left closed. Escape hatch, recorded so it does not have to be rediscovered under pressure: an admin can lift protection with `gh api -X DELETE repos/.../branches/main/protection` and restore it with the PUT above. | A human partner decision on whether/when to require this check via branch protection (repo Settings > Branches > main > required status checks), and whether to wait for the workflow to build a short track record of real green/red runs first before making it blocking | Phase 1d or Phase 2 | Closed-done |
| D-194 | `docker/Dockerfile`'s `deps` stage is dead -- it runs `npm ci --only=production` and nothing ever copies from it | RISK | 2026-08-10 | Found by reading the whole Dockerfile while making D-192's one-line base-image change, not by a tool. The file defines four stages (`base`, `deps`, `builder`, `runner`) and every `COPY --from=` in it names `builder`: the runner stage copies `public`, `.next/standalone`, `.next/static`, `prisma` and `node_modules/.prisma`, all `--from=builder`. Nothing anywhere references `deps` -- `grep -rn 'deps' docker/ package.json` returns only the stage's own `FROM base AS deps` definition. BuildKit skips stages no target depends on, so this costs nothing at build time; the cost is that the file READS as if production dependencies were installed for the runtime image, when the runtime image is actually Next.js standalone output, which carries its own resolved dependency tree (the separate `node_modules/.prisma` copy is the evidence -- standalone tracing misses the Prisma engine, so it had to be added by hand). A future reader debugging a missing-module error in the container would reasonably start at the `deps` stage and be reading a step that has never executed. Secondary: `--only=production` is deprecated in npm 7+ in favour of `--omit=dev`, so the dead stage also carries a deprecated flag. NOT fixed here, deliberately: D-192's scope is Node-version alignment, and deleting a build stage changes the shape of the image build, which deserves a full `docker build` to completion as evidence -- only `docker build --check` was run in this work, and that validates the definition without executing any stage | Delete the `deps` stage, or wire it up if a slim production-dependency layer was the actual intent, and verify with a complete `docker build` that the resulting image still starts and serves. Natural to pick up alongside D-018 (hosting), which is also why `docker/Dockerfile` has gone unexercised long enough for a dead stage to survive unnoticed | Phase 2 (with D-018 hosting) | Open |
| D-195 | `POST:/api/auth/register` is capped at 5 per 15 minutes per IP and the e2e suite already drives about five registrations, so there is effectively zero headroom -- and Phase 1d-i is about to make that suite a blocking required check | RISK | 2026-08-10 | Found by a `what-if-oracle` Deep Oracle run aimed at the Phase 1d-i decomposition (the Psi Wild Card slot -- 'a variable nobody is tracking'), then verified by the controller rather than accepted on report: `lib/rate-limit.ts:21` reads `'POST:/api/auth/register': { window: 15 * 60 * 1000, max: 5, keyBy: 'ip' }`, and registration is driven from `e2e/auth.spec.ts`, `e2e/project.spec.ts`'s `registerAndLogin`, `e2e/org-routing.spec.ts`'s `registerAndLogin`, and `e2e/two-orgs.spec.ts:115`'s 'Create Account'. **Why this is dangerous rather than merely tight.** The budget is a property of the SUITE, but it is spent per FILE, so no single spec file shows it and no reviewer reading one file can see it. The failure it produces is also misattributed by construction: the spec that exceeds the cap is not the spec that fails -- whichever registration happens to be sixth gets a 429, so adding a registering test in Phase 1d-ii reddens an UNRELATED, previously-green test, under a required check, with a 429 that looks like a product bug. The counter is in-memory and `reuseExistingServer: !process.env.CI` means a local dev server persists it across suite runs, so a developer can also poison their own next run. **Ruled by the human partner on 2026-08-10 to be fixed inside Phase 1d-i, not deferred to 1d-ii**, on the principle that the phase which arms the gate clears the chamber -- 1d-i is what makes the check blocking, so 1d-i owns the landmine it arms | Fix in Phase 1d-i, before the e2e check is promoted to required. This is a SECURITY CONTROL, so it takes the section 7.1 preventive pass and its own fresh threat derivation before any change is written -- raising a number to make a test suite pass is exactly the move that needs a reason, not a convenience. Candidate directions, none chosen yet: make the cap configurable per environment; key the limit so fixture registrations do not share one bucket; or reduce the suite's registration count. Pinned afterwards by O-10 of the 1d-i spec, which must GENERATE the expected count from `lib/rate-limit.ts` rather than hard-code it, so the next added registration fails this row's own test and not a stranger's. **LIVE DATA POINT, Task 5, 2026-08-10 (not a fix, not a closure):** the new `test-e2e` CI job (`ci.yml`) ran the full suite for real against this exact cap, unmodified. The server log shows the cap was hit exactly once — `{"event":"RATE_LIMIT_EXCEEDED","ip":"::1","details":{"pathname":"/api/auth/register","method":"POST","limit":5}}` — immediately before `security.spec.ts`'s own "excessive API calls trigger rate limit headers" test, which deliberately loops 5 registrations to observe the limit and is written to tolerate a 429 (it only asserts on `x-ratelimit-remaining` when the header is present). The run stayed green (48 passed, 1 skipped — the D-185 quarantine, 0 failed, `--fail-on-flaky-tests` did not trip: https://github.com/Alvin-Nahabwe/makai-rai-platform/actions/runs/31390525485) — but this row's own title ("effectively zero headroom") is now confirmed live rather than estimated: the cap was fully consumed by the time that one deliberate test ran, so this suite has no slack for one more real registration anywhere before it. `lib/rate-limit.ts` was deliberately NOT touched by Task 5 — out of its stated scope, and this row's own Step 1 requires a fresh `senior-security` pass before any such change **CLOSED, this commit (Task 4).** Ruled: raised `max` from 5 to 50 in `lib/rate-limit.ts`, a NAT-tolerant value chosen per the Task 4 threat derivation (`.superpowers/sdd/2026-08-10-phase1d-i-e2e-trustworthy/task-4-derivation.md`) -- 15 is the floor the suite's own registration traffic needs; ~50 covers a ~40-person cohort registering with room for typo/duplicate retries; the CPU ceiling (not abuse) bounds how high this could go, measured at ~11.5 CPU-seconds per IP per 15-minute window for 50 accepted registrations (bcryptjs cost=12, mean 230ms/hash), negligible. **Stated plainly, not implied: this change does NOT address the adversarial axis.** Live-measured in the derivation (S2.2): 7 of 7 requests accepted through the real running proxy with a rotated `x-forwarded-for`, never refused; 500 of 500 at the unit boundary. Registration remains unbounded, in principle, to anyone willing to fabricate one HTTP header, regardless of what `max` is set to -- `proxy.ts`'s IP extraction has no trusted-proxy allowlist. That gap is carried forward, not closed here, by two new rows: **D-199** (trusted-proxy allowlist, blocked on D-018, the same blocker as D-124/D-125) and **D-200** (email verification/challenge -- the derivation argues this may be the control that should exist, where the cap is merely the wrong number). Closing this row as though abuse were handled would be the single worst available outcome the derivation names (its S8 Delta Worst Case); it is not closed on that basis. This row's own title also names a vacuous test -- `security.spec.ts`'s "excessive API calls trigger rate limit headers", whose assertion chain reduced to `0 <= Infinity` then `0 <= 0` under refusal and would pass identically whether the limiter worked, was absent, or refused every request. Replaced with a test that drives real HTTP traffic to the actual boundary on its own dedicated, isolated `x-forwarded-for` (so its result depends only on requests it sends, not on the rest of the suite's registration traffic or run order), asserting `EXPECTED_CAP` successes then one refusal. Red-before-green run manually: against the unmodified `max: 5` the new test failed on request #1 (`remaining` 4, expected 49); against `max: 50` it passed (14.1s, server log confirms `limit:50` observed live). O-10's original shape -- GENERATING the suite's expected registration count from `lib/rate-limit.ts` and pinning it -- was deliberately NOT built: it creates exactly the gradient the derivation's S8 (Second Order) names, where adding a registering test turns a generated check red and the cheapest green is raising `max`, with no security reasoning at any step. `EXPECTED_CAP` in the new test is instead a hand-written, independent constant that must be updated in step with `lib/rate-limit.ts`'s literal or the test fails at the wrong boundary -- a visible, forced edit, not a silent auto-adapt. Verified live: `npx playwright test e2e/security.spec.ts` and the full `npx playwright test` suite both green; `npx vitest run` green including `__tests__/unit/rate-limit.test.ts`'s updated regression pin (now expects 50, was 5). One further finding surfaced by the derivation's Psi Wild Card, not asked for by this row but raised here for the controller per AGENTS.md §4 rather than left unrecorded: **D-201**, organization-slug squatting -- raising this cap also raises the rate at which organization slugs can be claimed, silently and permanently, against exactly this product's stated adopter population. | Phase 1d-i | Closed-done |
| D-196 | Playwright traces and screenshots capture a raw single-use invitation `acceptUrl` as visible link text, and Phase 1d-i uploads those artifacts off the machine on failure | RISK | 2026-08-10 | Found by `engineering-skills:adversarial-reviewer` (W-3) attacking the Phase 1d-i decomposition. The v1 spec had already rejected a proposed email capture-transport partly on the grounds that 'a mechanism that writes raw single-use invitation tokens to a local artifact is a mechanism for harvesting them' -- then cleared the Playwright artifact path using a NARROWER lens, reasoning only about network responses ('traces record network responses, and the invite POST's response body contains a live acceptUrl'). That misses the second half of what a trace is: traces also carry DOM snapshots and screenshots, and `MembersManager.tsx` renders the `acceptUrl` as VISIBLE LINK TEXT in both of its branches, so the token is in the pixels and the DOM as well as in the response body. `playwright.config.ts:10` sets `trace: 'on-first-retry'` and CI runs `retries: 2`, so on a known-flaky suite traces are produced routinely rather than exceptionally, and the 1d-i definition of done uploads the report on failure -- that is, OFF the machine, which the rejected transport never was. **The conclusion is still nil-risk today and this row is not a blocker:** CI organisations are ephemeral fixtures and the database is torn down with the job, so the tokens are dead before anyone could read them. What is wrong is the DERIVATION, and the derivation is what a future reader inherits -- a row scoped to 'network responses' would not fire the day someone points the e2e suite at a shared or long-lived environment, which is precisely when it should | Before pointing the e2e suite at any environment whose organisations are not torn down with the job, or before making Playwright artifacts publicly readable (note the repository is PUBLIC, so artifact visibility is a live setting rather than a hypothetical). At that point either stop rendering the raw token as link text under test, mask it in the trace, or scope artifact retention. Recorded now so the trigger is attached to the RENDERED token and not only to the network response | Phase 2 (with D-018 hosting), or immediately on the trigger above | Open |
| D-197 | AGENTS.md C4 tells the controller to run `code-review` then `simplify` on every chunk, but both are FIX-APPLYING skills -- so honouring C4 literally makes the controller apply fixes, which `subagent-driven-development` explicitly forbids, and delegating them to a reviewer collides with the read-only instruction every review dispatch carries | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-10 | Surfaced by the Task 1 reviewer of Phase 1d-i, which declined to invoke either skill mechanically and said why: the skills' own descriptions state they review AND THEN APPLY THE FIXES, and both may run as autonomous subagents with write tools, which contradicts the dispatch's explicit 'do not modify any repo file, read-and-report only'. Faced with two incompatible instructions it chose the more specific one, applied both lenses by direct manual analysis with commands and outputs cited, produced a real Important finding plus an explicit non-manufactured 'simplify: Clean' verdict, and flagged that it could not open this row itself under read-only scope. **The defect is in the dispatch design, not the reviewer's judgement, and not the skills.** There is a genuine three-way collision nobody had noticed: (1) AGENTS.md C4 binds `code-review` and `simplify` to the controller at the moment of receiving work; (2) `subagent-driven-development` says 'Never fix findings yourself in the controller session -- your context stays clean for coordination, and controller fixes skip review'; (3) a review subagent is dispatched read-only so it cannot race the implementer for the working tree. Any two of those three can hold at once. All three cannot. C4 was written before this project adopted SDD, and the two documents were never reconciled -- the same shape AGENTS.md §3 names, a rule whose enforcement point moved while its trigger stayed behind. Worth recording that the obligation was substantively MET here: both lenses were applied and one produced a finding that entered the fix loop and was addressed. What was not met is the mechanical form C4 specifies, which is exactly the distinction §6 exists to keep visible rather than let a 'close enough' judgement quietly settle **SECOND INSTANCE, 2026-08-10 (Phase 1d-i Task 3), recorded because it shows the collision has more than one failure mode.** Here the IMPLEMENTER attempted to invoke the skills and reported the tool call was rejected mid-session; it then treated the controller's interruption message -- which said 'I read all four changes. The work is good, do not redo it' -- as substituting for the checkpoint, and said so in its report rather than skipping silently. **Both halves of that are worth separating.** The implementer's honesty is the process working. The substitution is not: the controller reading a diff is not the mechanical form C4 specifies, and that sentence was written to stop the agent redoing sound work after an interruption, not to discharge a checkpoint. So the second failure mode is that C4's obligation can be *inferred as met* from an unrelated controller statement, which is worse than the first mode (a reviewer declining to run it) because it leaves no trace at all unless the subagent volunteers one. Tally so far: two of three tasks in this phase, by two different agents, for two different reasons. The mechanical C6 skill audit will therefore find zero `code-review` and zero `simplify` invocations across the whole phase, which this row exists to explain rather than let read as negligence. **THIRD INSTANCE, 2026-08-10 (Phase 1d-i Task 4).** The implementer reported honestly that it did not invoke `senior-security` or `test-driven-development` as formal skill-tool calls, having followed both practices manually -- a fresh threat derivation existed already (890 lines, its own dispatch) and red-before-green was demonstrated with real command output (the new cap test failed against `max: 5`, passed against `max: 50`). So the OBLIGATIONS were met and the MECHANICAL FORM was not, for the third time in one phase, by a third agent, for a third distinct reason: first a reviewer declining a fix-applying skill under a read-only dispatch, then an implementer inferring the checkpoint from an unrelated controller remark, now an implementer judging the substance already discharged by prior work. **That the reasons keep differing is the finding.** A collision with one cause gets fixed by removing the cause; a collision that recurs through three unrelated routes is structural, and the structure is that C4 names skills whose only documented mode applies changes, in a process whose other rules forbid the controller and the reviewer from applying changes. Tally for the C6 audit: three of six implementation tasks. **CLOSED-DONE 2026-08-12 at the Phase 1d-i C6 exit, by AGENTS.md amendment, on the human partner's ruling.** This row was targeted at this exact checkpoint and was open, so the phase could not exit without resolving or explicitly re-targeting it. C4's bullet now reads that the code-review and simplify LENSES are the obligation and names two things that discharge them -- the controller invoking the skills directly, or a dispatched reviewer applying both lenses by direct analysis and returning a separate verdict for each. **The original guard was preserved, not weakened:** a spec-compliance verdict still does NOT discharge them, because a reviewer answering 'does this diff match its brief' has reviewed neither correctness nor clarity, and that substitution is what caused D-063. What changed is only that the discharge is now expressible: the previous wording said an SDD reviewer subagent does not discharge this at all, which combined with read-only review dispatches left no satisfiable path. The amendment states which checkpoint it binds to (C4) per section 0.1. **Evidence the substance was never actually lost across the three instances:** each review in this phase did return per-lens verdicts, and they found real defects -- a misleading spatial reference in a config comment, a 'CI gate' overclaim in a register row that contradicted a sibling row four lines above, and a missed `--no-deps` optimisation costing ~20 redundant logins per CI run. Two of those entered fix loops and were addressed. So the lenses were doing their job throughout while the mechanical form was unsatisfiable, which is precisely why the fix is to name the obligation rather than to demand the tool. | Resolve before the Phase 1d C6 exit, because the C6 skill audit greps for skill invocations by name and will score `code-review`/`simplify` as zero for this phase on a literal reading -- producing a false gap next to a real one, which is worse than either alone. Candidate resolutions, none chosen: amend C4 to say the LENSES are mandatory and name the read-only analytic form as the discharge when the reviewer is a dispatched subagent; or bind the two skills to the controller only for work the controller did itself; or split each skill's review pass from its apply pass so a read-only invocation is expressible. This is an AGENTS.md amendment, so under section 0 it must state which checkpoint it binds to -- and the honest answer may be that C4 needs two variants, one for delegated work and one for direct | Phase 1d C6 exit | Closed-done |
| D-198 | `AssessmentPageClient.tsx` calls the side-effecting `autoSave(next)` from INSIDE a `setEngineState` updater, in two places, breaking React's requirement that updaters be pure | BUG | 2026-08-10 | Found while investigating D-185 (Task 7) and recorded whether or not it is implicated -- it is NOT the cause of D-185, which was root-caused to a different line (`:205`), so this row stands on its own contract violation rather than on a symptom. `handleResponseChange` (`:430-438`) runs `autoSave(next)` at `:434` inside `setEngineState((prev) => {...})`, and `handleChecklistChange` (`:440-453`) does the same at `:449`. **The Task 7 brief named only one of the two sites (`431-439`); the second was found by grepping `setEngineState` rather than trusting the brief's enumeration, per AGENTS.md Sec.3.** `autoSave` (`:318-331`) is not pure: it clears and re-arms a `setTimeout` on `saveTimerRef` that later PATCHes the assessment. Two consequences, one verified as present and one structural. VERIFIED PRESENT: React StrictMode is ON for the App Router (`node_modules/next/dist/build/define-env.js:142-144`; `next.config.ts` never sets `reactStrictMode`, whose default is `null` at `node_modules/next/dist/server/config-shared.js:118`) and the development react-dom deliberately re-invokes user functions that must be pure -- `shouldDoubleInvokeUserFnsInHooksDEV` is set from `(workInProgress.mode & StrictLegacyMode) !== NoMode` at `react-dom-client.development.js:7660-7661`, `<StrictMode>` sets both `StrictLegacyMode` and `StrictEffectsMode` even on a concurrent root (`:23440`), and the class-component equivalent of this exact pattern is double-called outright at `:7325-7339`. STRUCTURAL, and the reason this is not merely a lint preference: a render can be interrupted and discarded, so an updater invoked during a throwaway render would arm an autosave for an `engineState` that never commits -- a PATCH persisting a state the user never reached. NOT FIXED DELIBERATELY: Task 7 was an investigation, and changing this while measuring D-185 would have changed a variable mid-investigation. The correct shape is to compute `next` and call `setEngineState(next)` plus `autoSave(next)` outside the updater, or to move the autosave into an effect keyed on `engineState` | Whenever `AssessmentPageClient`'s response handlers are next touched, and no later than the fix for D-185 -- that fix edits the same data path (`:205`, ~230 lines above) and will have the file open. A cheap check that this is real rather than theoretical: count the PATCHes to `/api/v1/orgs/*/assessments/*` for one answered question under `npm run dev` and confirm the 1s debounce is being armed once, not twice | Phase 1d | Open |
| D-199 | Trusted-proxy allowlist for `proxy.ts`'s IP extraction (`x-forwarded-for`/`x-real-ip`) -- the fix that would make the rate limiter's identifier trustworthy against an adversary, rather than merely usable by honest NAT'd clients | RISK | 2026-08-10 | Opened at Task 4's D-195 closure, per RULING 2 of that task's dispatch. Live-measured (Task 4 threat derivation, S2.2): with no upstream-proxy allowlist, `proxy.ts:66-70` trusts a client-supplied header unconditionally -- 7 of 7 registration requests accepted with a rotated `x-forwarded-for`, never refused; 500 of 500 at the unit boundary. This is the SAME root cause D-124 (login rate limit bypass) and D-125 (consent-record `ipAddress` provenance) already name, widened here to `POST:/api/auth/register` specifically. The correct fix -- honour `x-forwarded-for` only from a configured set of trusted upstream addresses, otherwise use the transport peer -- cannot be written without knowing whether this app sits behind a real reverse proxy, and if so which one: choosing a trust boundary with no deployment topology to trust it against is guessing, not deciding, exactly as D-124/D-125 already concluded for their own instances of this same defect. **Blocked on D-018** (hosting/deployment model, PARKED, Open) -- the identical blocker | When hosting is decided (D-018), or at the first non-local deployment, whichever is first -- treat this, D-124 and D-125 as one piece of work, since all three need the identical topology decision before any of them can be written correctly | Phase 1d+, gated on D-018 | Open |
| D-200 | No email verification (or other challenge) gates account creation -- `RATE_LIMITS['POST:/api/auth/register']` is currently the ENTIRE anti-abuse posture of account creation on this platform, and a client-spoofable rate limit cannot carry that alone | RISK | 2026-08-10 | Opened at Task 4's D-195 closure, per RULING 2 of that task's dispatch, as its own scoped task rather than a footnote. Checked, not assumed (Task 4 threat derivation, S3.2): a repo-wide case-insensitive grep across every `.ts`/`.tsx`/`.prisma` file (excluding node_modules) for the terms captcha, turnstile, hcaptcha, recaptcha, emailVerified, verificationToken and EmailVerification returns zero hits, and `User.isActive` defaults `true` (`prisma/schema.prisma:77`) -- no approval step, no activation step. One unauthenticated HTTP request yields a fully live tenant with an owner who can log in immediately. The derivation's Phi Contrarian reading: the consensus premise going into Task 4 was "5 is the wrong number"; the contrarian (and, on the evidence, likely correct) reading is that **the rate limiter is the wrong control**, not merely misconfigured -- no rate limiter keyed on a client-supplied header can bound account creation alone, regardless of its value (see D-199). With verification in place, the register cap's exact value stops being security-load-bearing and can be set generously without re-opening this question. The pieces this task would build on already exist in this codebase: the Resend email transport (`lib/email/send.ts`, exercised live by `e2e/two-orgs.spec.ts`) and the sha256 `tokenHash` invitation-token pattern (D-097) that a verification token can copy directly. Cost estimated by analogy only, NOT scoped or spiked (effort claims are claims, AGENTS.md §5): a schema column, a token table, a mail send, a verification route, a login/access gate, and UX -- real, but shaped like a task, not a phase | Before this platform is deployed anywhere account creation is reachable by strangers (gated on the same D-018 deployment decision as D-199, though this row's fix does NOT itself depend on knowing the network topology the way D-199's does) -- or sooner, if the human partner judges the current unverified-account exposure unacceptable before then. If implemented: the obligation is "cannot reach any AUTHENTICATED SURFACE, not merely cannot log in" (D-191 shape -- "blocks login" is not "blocks access"), tested against every entry point that can mint a session, and the guard's production call sites added to `__tests__/integration/trigger-enumeration.test.ts` per AGENTS.md §3 | Phase 1d+ | Open |
| D-201 | Organization-slug squatting: raising `POST:/api/auth/register`'s rate limit raises the rate at which real institutions' slugs can be claimed by someone else first, silently and permanently | RISK | 2026-08-10 | Found by `what-if-oracle`'s Psi Wild Card slot run against the Task 4 decomposition ("what if the D-195 fix ships exactly as specified?"), not asked for by Task 4's own dispatch -- raised here for the controller per AGENTS.md §4 ("nothing is written outside assigned scope," so this row records the finding rather than the task quietly acting on it. Mechanism, verified by reading `deriveSlug`/`createOrgInTx` directly (`lib/data/preauth.ts:410-464`), not assumed: the org slug is derived SERVER-SIDE from the submitted organization name, and D-101 deliberately made a slug collision UNREPORTABLE to the caller -- "a form reporting that slug is taken is an existence oracle" -- so `createOrgInTx` retries silently on a `UNIQUE` violation and, after 5 attempts, falls back to one unconditional random-suffixed write. Consequence: an attacker who mass-registers with real institution names claims their slugs first -- `deriveSlug('Makerere University')` always derives `'makerere-university'`. When the genuine institution later registers, it collides and, by design, is told nothing: it silently receives `makerere-university-a1b2`, an opaque URL, with no error, no explanation, and no recourse surface anywhere in the product -- the exact security property that makes collisions safe (no existence oracle) is what makes the squatting invisible to its victim. Composes with D-112 (institutions whose names are not NFKD-foldable to Latin already land in the opaque `org-xxxx` namespace): the institutions most likely to get an opaque URL by squatting are also the ones most likely to get one by script-folding -- both defects concentrate on this product's stated primary adopter population (African university teams, VISION_AND_PLAN.md §3.2). Persists after any attack stops -- a squatted slug on a soft-deleted org still occupies the `organizations.slug` UNIQUE constraint (`deletedAt` does not free it, `prisma/schema.prisma:280`). Not addressed by D-200 (email verification) either: an attacker willing to verify 200 mailboxes still squats 200 slugs -- only a namespace policy (reserved names, a claim/dispute process, or institution-verified slugs) would address this, and none exists today. This is the finding that argues AGAINST a generous register cap, in tension with D-199/D-200's ergonomics argument for one -- the tension itself is worth the human partner's attention, not resolved unilaterally here | Before this platform's URLs are shown to, or chosen by, an institution whose real name another registrant could plausibly have typed first -- i.e., before any real multi-institution pilot, since D-195's own fix (raising the register cap to 50) directly increases how fast this can happen per institution per window. Decide deliberately: reserved/verified institution names, a slug claim-dispute process, or an accepted-risk call that the random-suffix fallback is tolerable | Phase 1d+ | Open |
| D-202 | CI's new `test-e2e` job runs `next start`, but the shipped Docker image runs `node .next/standalone/server.js` -- the gate exercises a production BUILD through an entrypoint production never uses | RISK | 2026-08-10 | Found by the controller while verifying Phase 1d-i Task 5, from that task's own reported observation of a harmless-looking `output: standalone` warning during `next start`. Verified directly rather than inferred: `next.config.ts:4` sets `output: 'standalone'`; `docker/Dockerfile:48` is `CMD ["node", "server.js"]` run against the `.next/standalone` tree copied at `:39`; the CI job invokes `npm run start`, and `package.json`'s `start` is plain `next start`. Those are two different server entrypoints with different module resolution and a different traced dependency set -- standalone bundles its own resolved tree, which is why the Dockerfile has to copy `node_modules/.prisma` separately. **Why this is worth a row rather than a shrug: the phase made it LESS visible, not more.** Before Task 5 nobody believed CI covered production -- D-192 said in as many words that the Dockerfile's build path was exercised by nothing. After Task 5 there is a green `e2e` job that runs `next build`, and a reasonable reader concludes the production path is gated. It is not. A genuine coverage improvement therefore produced a DECREASE in the accuracy of what people believe about coverage, which is AGENTS.md section 3's fourth question -- what did this change make possible that was not possible before -- answered unwelcomely: it made it possible to be confidently wrong. Compounds D-192 (three Node declarations, the Dockerfile's exercised by nothing) and D-194 (the Dockerfile's `deps` stage is dead) -- three rows now saying the same thing from different angles, which is itself the signal | Either run the e2e suite against the standalone server in CI so the gate matches the shipped runtime, or state explicitly in `ci.yml` and in D-192/D-194 that the Docker runtime is verified by nothing and name what would verify it. Natural to pick up with D-018 (hosting), which is the decision that determines whether the standalone image is the deployment artifact at all -- if it is not, the correct fix may be deleting `output: 'standalone'` rather than testing it | Phase 2 (with D-018 hosting) | Open |
| D-203 | Task 6's promotion of the e2e check-run to `main`'s required-status-checks list is blocked before it reached GitHub: the harness's auto-mode permission classifier denies the mutating `gh api -X PUT` call in this non-interactive session | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-10 | Attempted, not assumed: `gh api -X PUT repos/Alvin-Nahabwe/makai-rai-platform/branches/main/protection` with `required_status_checks.contexts` set to `["lint, typecheck, test, build", "e2e"]`, `strict=true`, `enforce_admins=true`, `required_pull_request_reviews=null`, `allow_force_pushes=false`, `allow_deletions=false` -- unchanged from D-193's own PUT except the added context -- returned before reaching GitHub: "Permission for this action was denied by the Claude Code auto mode classifier. Reason: Blocked by classifier." Re-read after the denial rather than assumed unchanged: `gh api repos/.../branches/main/protection --jq '.required_status_checks.contexts'` still returns `["lint, typecheck, test, build"]` -- no partial or silent mutation occurred. The two facts this task exists to carry were both re-verified live before the blocked step, so they do not have to be rediscovered when someone with permission finishes this: (1) `gh api repos/.../commits/70e00c0.../check-runs --jq '.check_runs[].name'` (the last commit on this branch pushed to `origin`) returns `lint, typecheck, test, build` and `e2e` -- the job id in `.github/workflows/ci.yml:212` is `test-e2e` but its `name:` field (line 213) is `e2e`, and `e2e` is the string GitHub actually reports. Had this been transcribed as `test-e2e` from the job id, or as `CI` from the workflow's top-level name, protection would look configured and block every merge forever on a check that is never created -- the exact trap D-193 named, now confirmed to recur even in the same-looking job. (2) the escape hatch is unchanged and untested-by-necessity-of-being-unneeded: `gh api -X DELETE repos/Alvin-Nahabwe/makai-rai-platform/branches/main/protection`, then re-PUT. Task steps 3 and 4 (probe the blocked direction with a real push; record a demotion path) were not run against live state, because step 3 needs the new dual-context protection in place to test what this task is actually for -- running it against the unchanged single-context protection would only re-prove D-193's own already-recorded result. The demotion-path policy this row exists to carry forward, stated now so it ships before enforcement does rather than being reconstructed under pressure later: a required context may be removed from `required_status_checks.contexts` when either (a) its job is deleted or renamed in `ci.yml`, making the context orphaned on GitHub's side regardless of what protection still lists, or (b) the human partner -- sole owner and contributor, the same reasoning D-193 used for `required_pull_request_reviews: null` -- rules that the check's false-positive or flake rate has made it a net cost to merge velocity rather than a net safety gain, with the evidence being a named number of consecutive false-red runs on commits independently confirmed correct, each logged as its own register row rather than recalled from memory. The decision is never silent: it is a `gh api -X PUT` re-issuing this list minus the demoted context, and a register row citing this one **RESOLVED 2026-08-10 by the human partner running the command directly.** The controller hit the same classifier denial as the subagent, so this was not a subagent-context restriction; the controller stopped, explained the situation and the exact command, and did NOT route the call through another available agent that might have had permission. That restraint is recorded deliberately: a permission denial is a decision about whether something should happen, not an obstacle to how, and shopping a refused command around agents until one accepts it would have changed a repository setting by a path the human partner never approved. The human partner then ran the PUT themselves. **Verified after the fact rather than assumed:** reading the live protection back gives `required_status_checks.contexts = ["lint, typecheck, test, build", "e2e"]` -- the pre-existing context preserved, `e2e` added -- with `strict=true`, `enforce_admins=true`, `required_pull_request_reviews=null`, force-pushes and deletions still disallowed, i.e. every D-193 setting intact. **The gate was then tested by attempting the thing it must prevent, not by reading the settings page:** an empty commit on local `main` was genuinely pushed and the remote refused it with `remote: error: GH006: Protected branch update failed for refs/heads/main.` and, decisively, `remote: - 2 of 2 required status checks are expected.` -- the count naming BOTH checks is what distinguishes a genuinely enforced second context from one that merely appears in a settings list. The probe commit was discarded with `git reset --hard origin/main` and local `main` re-confirmed identical to `origin/main`. The ALLOWED direction is proven by this branch's own merge, which must now clear both checks. **DEMOTION PATH, recorded because promotion took one command while reversal is asymmetric:** with `enforce_admins: true` there is no admin override, so if the e2e check becomes unreliable every merge stops until someone reverses it, and the reversal is not obvious under pressure. To demote, re-run the same PUT with `contexts` reduced to `["lint, typecheck, test, build"]`; to remove protection entirely as an emergency measure, `gh api -X DELETE repos/Alvin-Nahabwe/makai-rai-platform/branches/main/protection`, then re-PUT. **The evidence that should trigger demotion, decided by the human partner, not unilaterally:** e2e failing on a commit whose failure is later shown to be unrelated to that commit's changes, twice within one working period -- i.e. the check blocking correct work rather than catching incorrect work. D-146's own thesis is the reason this threshold matters: a guard that is routinely ignored is not meaningfully different from one that never ran, and a gate that blocks correct work is the fastest route to being routinely ignored. Note also that e2e carries one KNOWN un-run test -- the D-185 quarantine -- so the answer-recording path is not covered by this gate even now. | A human (or an agent session with Bash permission to mutate GitHub branch protection) runs the PUT given verbatim above -- or the equivalent change in GitHub Settings > Branches > main -- then completes this task's steps 3 and 4 for real: an empty commit genuinely pushed to local `main` and rejected with `GH006` naming both `lint, typecheck, test, build` and `e2e`, discarded with `git reset --hard origin/main`, output quoted in this row's closure | Phase 1d-i (this task's own blocked deliverable -- pick up immediately, not deferred to a later phase) | Closed-done |
| D-204 | 82 register rows carry closure or re-targeting NARRATIVE inside the STATUS column -- up to 3,798 characters -- so the one machine-readable field in the register is prose, and every audit that reads it by exact match silently skips those rows | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-10 | Found at the Phase 1d-i C6 register review, by the gate it broke. A status field reading `Open -- **re-targeted 2026-08-05 at the Plan 1b phase-exit reconciliation ...**` equals neither "open" nor anything else the query tested, so 84 of 203 rows were SKIPPED and the gate reported a pass it had no power to establish. Counted from disk, not estimated: 84 rows have a status field longer than 25 characters, accumulated across two prior phase exits (2026-08-05 and 2026-08-09) where each reconciliation appended its note to the status column instead of the explanation column. **The two genuinely dangerous ones were fixed in this same commit** -- D-144 and D-146 displayed `Open` while their own text said `Closed-done`, so finished, verified work counted as outstanding through an entire phase exit; their narrative was relocated to the explanation column (nothing deleted) and their status set to a clean `Closed-done`, after confirming both cited artefacts exist (`docs/adr/0003-...md` at 14,234 bytes from commit 884b428, and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` with main's latest run green). **The remaining 82 are ugly but not wrong** -- their leading word matches their true state -- which is why they are a row rather than a same-commit fix. **The deeper finding: the register had NO automated check of any kind.** 203 rows, three phases, the artefact AGENTS.md section 6 leans on to stop a deferral becoming a silent gap, and every audit was an awk command retyped by hand at each exit. The one structural audit that did exist -- an awk field-count check -- passed on all 203 rows throughout, because it checks a row's SHAPE and never its CONTENT -- D-191's own class, a check confirming a mechanism while the property it stands for is violated, committed against the register that records D-191 | Closed in part by `__tests__/lint/register-integrity.test.ts`, added in this commit: six assertions covering field count, duplicate IDs, sequence gaps, a hand-written status vocabulary, a non-vacuity floor on the parser itself, and the displays-Open-but-says-closed contradiction. Each was proven to fail against a deliberately corrupted copy before being committed. What REMAINS for this row: migrating the 82 legacy rows' narrative out of the status column into the explanation column. Deliberately not done now -- it is a large mechanical rewrite of rows this session did not author, with real risk of losing text, and the dangerous class is already guarded. Pick it up when a status-field migration is cheap to verify, or when someone needs the register queryable by status for reporting. Note the test tolerates the 82 by design: it asserts the leading word is a known value and does not contradict the row's own text, which is true of all of them | Phase 2 | Open |
| D-205 | C6's mandated skill audit greps the ledger and commits for skill NAMES, so it measures mentions rather than invocations and is wrong in both directions | PROCESS FAILURE | 2026-08-12 | Found by running it at the Phase 1d-i C6 exit. C4's own audit instruction says to grep the SDD ledger and branch commits for every skill named in `docs/SKILLS_INVENTORY.md`. Run as written it produced: `run` scoring 272, which is the English word and not the skill; `code-review` 13 and `simplify` 15, for skills that D-197 records were never mechanically invoked in this phase at all; and `writing-plans` ZERO, for a skill the controller genuinely did invoke, scoring nothing only because no ledger line or commit message happens to name it. So the instrument over-counts prose collisions and under-counts real invocations that left no textual trace. **This is the same class the audit exists to catch, committed by the audit itself** -- it confirms a MECHANISM (does this string appear) while the PROPERTY it stands for (was this skill actually invoked) can be violated in either direction with the check unchanged, which is D-191's shape. Recorded rather than fixed in the same breath because amending C6's audit method is a separate process change from the C4 amendment the human partner ruled on this exit, and expanding one approved amendment into two is the laundering shape section 6 exists to prevent | Before the next phase exit, since that exit will run the same instrument and get the same unreliable answer. The honest fix is probably not a better grep: an invocation leaves a trace in the controller's own tool-call record and in each subagent's report, neither of which is in the ledger. Candidate directions, none chosen: require every dispatch to name its invoked skills in a fixed, greppable format in the report file; or drop the grep and make the audit a reading obligation over the task reports, the way D-191 was deliberately made a reading obligation rather than another test | Phase 1d-ii C6 exit | Open |
---

## Rollback annotation — 2026-08-02

Rows **D-055 through D-062** were opened by Phase-1a Tasks 1–5, whose commits were rolled back
to `9739454` on 2026-08-02 (see D-063). Their commit citations therefore point at objects that no
longer exist on `phase1a-isolation-spine`; the commits are preserved on branch
`rollback/phase1a-unadherent` and remain readable there.

Per rule 6 these rows are **retained, not deleted**. They are not automatically valid either: each
described a real consequence of an implementation that no longer exists, so each must be
re-evaluated during the redo and either re-opened against the new commits, or closed with a note
that the redo did not reproduce the condition. Treating them as still-true would be exactly the
transcription error rule 8 forbids.

## Closure log

Closed rows keep their register entry above (status updated) and gain an entry here with
evidence. Nothing is deleted.

**D-041** — Closed 2026-08-02. `lib/data/identity.ts` was introduced in `ceece2d` (Task 4) as
the documented boundary for `User`/`ConsentRecord`, satisfying the row's letter. Fix-round-1
review of that commit found the boundary was documentation-only: `identityDb` connects as
`makrai`, verified live to be a Postgres superuser with `rolbypassrls = t` (`SELECT rolsuper,
rolbypassrls FROM pg_roles WHERE rolname='makrai'` → `t|t`), so no RLS policy from Task 5 could
ever have constrained it — `identityDb.project.findMany()` type-checked and would have returned
every tenant's rows. `fad9627` closes the gap by narrowing `identityDb`'s exported type to
`Omit<PrismaClient, 'project' | 'projectMetadata' | 'assessment' | 'remediationItem' |
'organization' | 'membership' | 'invitation'>`, verified live in two directions: (1) with the
fix in place, `tsc --noEmit` reports exactly the 3 known/unrelated errors and
`__tests__/integration/identity-boundary.test.ts`'s 7 `@ts-expect-error` assertions all
type-check as genuine errors; (2) with the fix temporarily reverted (`identityDb: PrismaClient`
instead of `NonTenantClient`), the same 7 assertions immediately became "Unused
`@ts-expect-error` directive" errors (10 total), proving the guard actually fires rather than
passing vacuously, before the fix was restored. The boundary is now compiler-enforced, not
documentation-only.

**D-041 — closure evidence re-anchored 2026-08-03 (Task 4 redo).** The paragraph above is
retained unedited because nothing here is deleted, but two of its load-bearing citations no
longer resolve on this branch and a reader would otherwise trust them. `ceece2d` and `fad9627`
are commits of the **rolled-back** attempt: `git merge-base --is-ancestor <sha> HEAD` reports
neither is an ancestor of `HEAD`, so the code they describe was not present in the tree when
this row was read at Task 4 start (`ls lib/data` → "No such file or directory"). The
`__tests__/integration/identity-boundary.test.ts` it cites does not exist on this branch
either. The row's *substance* is nonetheless satisfied, by the Task 4 commit that carries this
paragraph: `lib/data/identity.ts` now exists as the documented `User`/`ConsentRecord` boundary,
and the boundary is compiler-enforced by an **allowlist** — `Pick<PrismaClient, 'user' |
'consentRecord'>` — rather than the `Omit<...>` denylist described above. The allowlist is
strictly stronger and was chosen because `Omit` left three holes the earlier text did not
account for: `$queryRaw*`/`$executeRaw*` survived it, `$transaction`'s handle was typed with
the full model set, and any tenant model added later was admitted by default. Verified live by
four `tsc --noEmit` probes, the fourth a negative control: `identityDb.project.findMany()` →
`Property 'project' does not exist on type 'NonTenantClient'`; `identityDb.$queryRaw` →
`Property '$queryRaw' does not exist`; `identityDb.$transaction(...)` → `Property
'$transaction' does not exist`; `identityDb.user.findMany()` → **no error**, i.e. the allowlist
still permits what it exists to serve. Status stays `Closed-done`.

**D-060** — Closed 2026-08-03 by the Task 4 commit that carries this entry. The row recorded
that the Task-4 brief's client code omitted the HMR-safe singleton guard, so every Next.js dev
hot-reload would leak a `PrismaClient` and its `pg.Pool` until the server exhausted
connections. All three clients created by this task now carry the guard, mirroring the existing
pattern read directly at `lib/db.ts:5-17` rather than recalled: `lib/data/tenant.ts` guards
`appClient` via `globalForData`, `lib/data/identity.ts` guards `identityDb` via
`globalForIdentity`, and `lib/data/preauth.ts` guards its module-private `ownerClient` via
`globalForPreauth` — each assigning to the `globalThis` slot only when
`process.env.NODE_ENV !== 'production'`, exactly as `lib/db.ts` does. Pool sizes are also
pinned rather than defaulted (`max: 10` for the app client, `max: 5` each for identity and
preauth), because four default pools would reserve 40 of the server's connections;
`SHOW max_connections` was re-run live at execution time rather than inherited from the brief's
comment and returned **100**, with 6 in use. **What was NOT verified live:** this closure rests
on source inspection and pool arithmetic, not on an observed dev-mode hot-reload — no route
imports any of the three clients yet (Plan 1b wires them), so there is currently no dev code
path on which HMR leakage could be demonstrated or refuted. The guard is verified present and
correct by reading; it is not verified effective by exercise.

**D-070, D-072, D-074, D-113** — Closed-done, Task 7 (2026-08-04). All four described the same
underlying population from four angles — the routes/pages still on the superuser `lib/db`
connection — and all four close on the same commit that ports the last of them. Verified live:
`npm run typecheck` 0 (was 13: 3 `orgId`-missing from D-070, 10 `auth-guard`/`session.user.role`
from D-113); `npm run lint` 0 problems with the entire ESLint override block named in D-074
deleted outright (`git diff` shows the block removed, not emptied entry-by-entry); `grep -rln
"from '@/lib/authz'" app` returns nothing (`lib/authz.ts` deleted, D-072's two-authorities risk
closed by removal, not reconciliation); `__tests__/integration/port-completeness.test.ts`'s
second assertion (`grep -rln "from '@/lib/db'\|from '.*\/db'" app lib components` filtered to
outside `lib/data/`) is empty, and `lib/db.ts` itself was deleted as orphaned once nothing
imported it. `npm run verify` (lint → test → build) passes end-to-end for the first time since
D-070 was opened at Task 2.

**CORRECTION, 2026-08-05 (final Plan 1b whole-branch review, CRITICAL-1) — read before trusting
the paragraph above.** D-074 was closed on the evidence "`npm run lint` 0 problems with the
entire ESLint override block named in D-074 deleted outright." That evidence was real but did
not prove what the closure implied: **the `lib/db`/`auth` ban it was closing had already stopped
being enforced**, from the Task 5 commit that added a SECOND `no-restricted-imports` config
object (the `createOrgContext` ban, D-089) on an overlapping `files` glob, until the CRITICAL-1
fix (see D-134 below). ESLint flat config replaces a rule's options per matching config object
rather than merging them, so the second block silently deleted the first's patterns for every
file both applied to — a live violator existed the whole time
(`app/(public)/invitations/[token]/page.tsx` imported `auth` directly) and `npm run lint`
correctly reported 0 problems throughout, because the deletion was a valid config, not a lint
error. **The allowlist reaching zero and the ban being enforced are two different facts, and
D-074's closure conflated them** — deleting the (by-then largely decorative) allowlist block
looked identical, in `git diff` and in `npm run lint`'s output, to a working ban getting stricter.
Not re-closing this row: it is `Closed-done` on its own narrow claim (the allowlist reached zero
and was deleted) and that claim is still true. The compound claim a reader would reasonably take
from it — "the raw-client ban has been enforced since Task 7" — was false for two days and is the
subject of D-134, not this row.

See D-134 below (new row, `## Register`) for the finding itself and its fix.

## Review cadence

- **Every phase exit:** full register review. No phase exits with an `Open` row whose target
  is that phase, unless the row is explicitly re-targeted with justification.
- **Every spec written under `superpowers:brainstorming`:** its Deferrals section lands here
  in the same commit.
- **Every `what-if-oracle` run:** its decision triggers become rows if they imply deferred work.
